# Lexicon index

GENERATED by tools/build_index.py on 2026-09-10. 342 entries, 2006 Hebrew headwords. Do not edit by hand.

**How to use this file.** It is a table of contents, not the guide. Find your Hebrew
word in the headword list, open the entry it points to, and apply that entry's
*Bottom line*. The entry is where the words are actually told apart, with each
distinction attributed to the authority who makes it, and disagreements between
authorities laid out rather than flattened. Part 2 below gives each entry's full
ruling so you can often decide without opening anything.

---

Where a row names an English rendering, that rendering is that word's own.
Where it says *see entry*, the entry tells the cluster apart in prose and in
its Bottom line; open it rather than guessing from the neighbouring words.

| גרב | garav | **moist boils (dry, when it stands alone)** | paired with cheres it is the moist kind and cheres the dry; standing without cheres it means the dry kind (Rashi, from Bechoros 41a) | [boils-shechin-garav-cheres.md](entries/boils-shechin-garav-cheres.md) |
| גרגרן | gargaran | **glutton** | built on gargeres though gorging is done with the veshet; the tension is the article's central question (Tosafos Yom Tov, Rabbi Binyamin Mussafia) | [throat-garon-gargeres-kaneh.md](entries/throat-garon-gargeres-kaneh.md) |
| גרגרת | gargeres | **windpipe, neck** | in Tanach the neck one hangs a necklace on, always plural for the trachea's rings (Rashi to Mishlei 1:9); in the Mishnah the windpipe set against the veshet | [throat-garon-gargeres-kaneh.md](entries/throat-garon-gargeres-kaneh.md) |
| גרון | garon | **throat, windpipe** | the Biblical word, chiefly the pipe speech comes through (Malbim), stretched to the visible neck and once even to the food pipe; Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim reverses this and makes the food pipe primary | [throat-garon-gargeres-kaneh.md](entries/throat-garon-gargeres-kaneh.md) |
| גרזן | garzen | **axe; pick** | the Biblical word covering both the woodsman's axe and the stonecutter's pick, from ג-ר-ז, cutting and carving (Radak); Menachem Ibn Saruk reads it as a quadriliteral instead, and it never appears in Rabbinic Hebrew except when a pasuk is being quoted | [cutting-tools-garzen-kardum-maatzad.md](entries/cutting-tools-garzen-kardum-maatzad.md) |
| גרם | gerem | **bone, itself** | the bone as one part within a larger frame, whence a single step of a staircase (Metzudas Tzion to Melachim II 9:13) and causation (Rav Hirsch); for the Vilna Gaon the hollow interior where the marrow sits | [bone-etzem-gerem.md](entries/bone-etzem-gerem.md) |
| גרמא | grama | **indirect causation** | rabbinic; one link in a chain of causes (Rav Hirsch), or the "self" that brought a thing about; Targum renders sabovti as garamti (Shmuel I 22:22), per Rabbi Eliyahu HaBachur | [bone-etzem-gerem.md](entries/bone-etzem-gerem.md) |
| גרממיא | germamia | **Germania** | Chazal's identification of Gomer (Yoma 10a; Yerushalmi Megillah 1:9); the Vilna Gaon emends to Germania, and the Aruch says plainly it is what we call the Land of Ashkenaz | [germany-ashkenaz-germamia-lotir.md](entries/germany-ashkenaz-germamia-lotir.md) |
| גרמני | germani | **a white-skinned man, or a German** | from gerem, bones being white (Rambam and Rabbi Tanchum HaYerushalmi to Negaim 2:1); the Vilna Gaon rejects that and reads it as a man of Germanica | [bone-etzem-gerem.md](entries/bone-etzem-gerem.md) |
| גרמניא | | see entry | one of the *names for Germany* words | [germany-ashkenaz-germamia-lotir.md](entries/germany-ashkenaz-germamia-lotir.md) |
| גרש | | see entry | one of the *flour* words | [flour-kemach-soles.md](entries/flour-kemach-soles.md) |
| גשם | geshem | **rain** | the commonest word; on one reading the general term for rain of every kind, on another the heavy or blessed rain specifically; also the ordinary course of nature, whence the later gashmiyus, materiality | [rain-geshem-matar-yoreh-malkosh.md](entries/rain-geshem-matar-yoreh-malkosh.md) |
| גשם | geshem | **rain** | rain as connection, heaven to earth, tzurah joined to chomer (Rabbi Moshe Shapiro); the constructive counterpart of mabul | [month-bul-marcheshvan.md](entries/month-bul-marcheshvan.md) |

| יהגה | yehegeh | **moans** | used of both a lion and a dove (Donash); from ה-ג, diligence and consistency, so a steady, sustained crying (Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim) | [animal-sounds-shoeg-nohem-noer.md](entries/animal-sounds-shoeg-nohem-noer.md) |

| יין | yayin | **wine** | the ordinary word, over 140 times in Tanach; on the older tradition aged wine, earning the name only forty days into production (Rashi to Yoma 76b), which Yoma 76b expounds from yelalah, wailing | [wine-yayin-tirosh-chemer-shechar.md](entries/wine-yayin-tirosh-chemer-shechar.md) |

The Rid holds the two are simply one word in two orders | [tired-ayef-yaef-yagea.md](entries/tired-ayef-yaef-yagea.md) |

| כידון | kidon | **javelin, dart** | a small romach carried by kings and nobles (Rambam to Keilim 11:8) and thrown at the enemy; Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim derives it from kad, a jug, for the spikes protruding at its sides | [spear-romach-chanit-kidon.md](entries/spear-romach-chanit-kidon.md) |

| ככר | | see entry | one of the *bread* words | [bread-lechem-kikar-challah-pat.md](entries/bread-lechem-kikar-challah-pat.md) |
| ככר | kikar | **loaf** | always in kikar lechem; a rounded loaf with body to it, spherical rather than flat (Rabbi Shaul Goldman); see the separate bread entry | [cake-ugah-chararah-gluskos.md](entries/cake-ugah-chararah-gluskos.md) |
| ככר | kikar | **grassland plain** | the plain of the Yarden (Bereishis 13:10); Ramban narrows it to lush grassland fed by rivers spilling into it | [plain-mishor-sharon-eil-kikar.md](entries/plain-mishor-sharon-eil-kikar.md) |
| כלא | kala | **cease, be held back** | stopping because the purpose is served or the reason has lapsed (Rabbi Yosef Grayever, Rabbi Shlomo Aharon Wertheimer); Malbim, held back against its own nature, from kele, a prison | [stopping-chadal-mana-chasach-kala-pasak.md](entries/stopping-chadal-mana-chasach-kala-pasak.md) |
| כלוב | keluv | **basket, cage** | twice in Tanach, once for unripe figs (Amos 8:1) and once for birds being fattened (Yirmiyahu 5:27), whence the modern sense of a cage | [basket-tene-sal-keluv.md](entries/basket-tene-sal-keluv.md) |
| כלום | | see entry | one of the *nothing* words | [nothing-hevel-rik-tohu-bohu.md](entries/nothing-hevel-rik-tohu-bohu.md) |
| כליות | kilayos | **kidneys** | the ordinary word, thirty-one times in Tanach, half of them in the korbanos; also the innermost thoughts and motives Hashem examines (Yirmiyahu 11:20; Tehillim 7:10) | [kidneys-kilayos-atzeh-tuchos.md](entries/kidneys-kilayos-atzeh-tuchos.md) |
| כליל | | see entry | one of the *crown* words | [crown-keter-atarah-nezer.md](entries/crown-keter-atarah-nezer.md) |
| כלימה | kelimah | **humiliation** | the graver word, for what is inherently disgraceful; shame that comes from outside, another person humiliating him (Malbim, Wertheimer), leaving him stripped of his shape (Rav Hirsch) | [shame-bushah-klimah-cherpah.md](entries/shame-bushah-klimah-cherpah.md) |
| כלכלה | kalkalah | **hamper, large basket** | Mishnaic; an especially large sal for storing all kinds of food, from kalkal, "to sustain" (Rabbi Tanchum HaYerushalmi) | [basket-tene-sal-keluv.md](entries/basket-tene-sal-keluv.md) |

| כנה | kinah | **a louse** | the singular, absent from Tanach but standard in Mishnah and Gemara (*Parah* 9:2, Shabbos 12b); at *Parah* 9:2 it is a *kinah* of the grain, a field pest | [lice-kinnim-kalmta-parosh.md](entries/lice-kinnim-kalmta-parosh.md) |
| כנור | kinnor | **harp, lyre, or a berry** | Rabbi Nosson of Rome gives both senses; Megillah 6a ties the city's name to it, its fruit being as sweet as a kinnor's sound, not to the lake's shape | [kinneres-ginosar-tiveria.md](entries/kinneres-ginosar-tiveria.md) |
| כנים | kinnim | **lice** | the plural, "many lice"; the classical lexicographers (Ibn Janach, Rashbam, Ibn Ezra, Ibn Parchon) hold the mem is not part of the root, which is the biliteral כ-נ | [lice-kinnim-kalmta-parosh.md](entries/lice-kinnim-kalmta-parosh.md) |
| כנם | kinam | **the lice, as a species** | a singular form naming the whole species of lice rather than individuals (Ibn Janach, Rashbam, Ibn Ezra, Ibn Parchon), used twice in the makkah account (Shemos 8:13 to 14) | [lice-kinnim-kalmta-parosh.md](entries/lice-kinnim-kalmta-parosh.md) |
| כנעני | cnaani | **trader** | "Canaanite" used for "merchant" (Pesachim 50a; Rashi and Radak to Yeshayahu 23:8); Malbim places him between the socher and the rochel | [merchant-socher-rochel-cnaani-tagar.md](entries/merchant-socher-rochel-cnaani-tagar.md) |
| כנף | | see entry | one of the *bird* words | [bird-of-tzipor-kanaf.md](entries/bird-of-tzipor-kanaf.md) |

| כנרת | Kinneres | **Kinneret** | the name in Chumash and Nach, taken from the fortified city of Kinneres in Naftali's territory (Yehoshua 19:35), the prominent town on its shore at the time | [kinneres-ginosar-tiveria.md](entries/kinneres-ginosar-tiveria.md) |
| כס | keis | **throne** | appears once, in the oath against Amalek (Shemos 17:16); the missing aleph is deliberate, marking that Hashem's Name and throne are incomplete while Amalek stands (Tanchuma, cited by Rashi) | [chair-kisse-keis-kursa.md](entries/chair-kisse-keis-kursa.md) |
| כסא | kisse | **throne** | the standard word, 135 times in Tanach, always with a final aleph; a seat of rank, covered or canopied to show the honor of the one sitting (Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim), and by Rambam a seat that implies importance and power | [chair-kisse-keis-kursa.md](entries/chair-kisse-keis-kursa.md) |
| כסה | kisseh | **throne** | the same word spelled with a final hey, only three times (Melachim I 10:19 twice, Iyov 26:9); Radak treats it as identical to kisse, and Rav Hirsch uses the spelling to link it to keseh, the covered new moon of Rosh Hashanah | [chair-kisse-keis-kursa.md](entries/chair-kisse-keis-kursa.md) |
| כסות | kesus | **covering** | worn by covering oneself rather than by dressing, like a shawl (Malbim); Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim: it names clothing's power to protect from heat or cold; Maharik takes it the opposite way, as the fitted garment | [clothing-beged-levush-kesus-simlah.md](entries/clothing-beged-levush-kesus-simlah.md) |

| פתע | peta | **suddenly (with no warning one could have used)** | the Gemara (Kerisus 9a) fixes it on shogeg alone; Rashi reads it as immediacy, one event following another too fast to have guarded against; Rav Hirsch makes it the subjective surprise, foreseeable had one thought it through | [suddenly-peta-pitom.md](entries/suddenly-peta-pitom.md) |

| פתרוס | Patros | **Upper Egypt** | not a synonym for Mitzrayim; either a son of Mitzrayim and his people (Bereishis 10:14) or, in Egyptian, the land of the south, Upper Egypt (Rabbi Aharon Marcus); the paytan Yanai uses it for Egypt as a whole by poetic license | [egypt-mitzrayim-patros-rahav.md](entries/egypt-mitzrayim-patros-rahav.md) |
| צאן | | see entry | one of the *sheep and goats* words | [sheep-and-goats-seh-keves-ayil-eiz.md](entries/sheep-and-goats-seh-keves-ayil-eiz.md) |
| צב | tzav | **turtle; covered wagon** | the turtle looks bloated by its shell, and the covered wagon is shaped like the turtle (Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim, on Vayikra 11:29 and Bamidbar 7:3) | [splendor-tzvi-tzivyon-tzava.md](entries/splendor-tzvi-tzivyon-tzava.md) |
| צבא | tzava | **legion, army** | literally a gathering of people, an army only because they share one want (Rav Hirsch, Rabbi Chaim Friedlander); on the two-letter root, a standing force ready for orders, against a militia that must be assembled (Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim) | [splendor-tzvi-tzivyon-tzava.md](entries/splendor-tzvi-tzivyon-tzava.md) |
| צבי | tzvi | **splendor, glory; desire; deer** | Rashi reads it as beauty, Radak as desire and glory; the same word names the deer (Devarim 14:5), which Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim ties to desire and Rabbi Aharon Marcus to its stately standing | [splendor-tzvi-tzivyon-tzava.md](entries/splendor-tzvi-tzivyon-tzava.md) |
| צבי | | see entry | one of the *will* words | [will-ratzon-chefetz.md](entries/will-ratzon-chefetz.md) |
| צביון | tzivyon | **consent, or ideal form** | Rashi and Rabbeinu Gershom: each part of creation was made according to its will, having consented; the Ritva, following the Aruch, reads beauty, each thing made in its most beautiful form | [splendor-tzvi-tzivyon-tzava.md](entries/splendor-tzvi-tzivyon-tzava.md) |
| צבע | tzeva | **color, dye, colorant** | the color a thing is given, from tzovea, dyeing; an artificial color imposed by painting or dyeing, as against a thing's natural color (Shadal) | [color-tzeva-gavan-ein-mareh.md](entries/color-tzeva-gavan-ein-mareh.md) |
| צהר | tzohar | **brightness, noon** | the root Rabbi Yosef Kimchi gives for yitzhar, since oil is clear and shining (Radak); Ibn Ezra takes the "noon" sense, since lamp oil gives light like the sun at midday | [oil-shemen-yitzhar.md](entries/oil-shemen-yitzhar.md) |
| צום | tzom | **fast** | the Biblical word, near fifty times, for abstaining from food; Radak in his father's name derives it from "gathering", as in tzomes hagidim, where the sinews converge, so a tzom is a fast the whole community joins | [fasting-tzom-taanis.md](entries/fasting-tzom-taanis.md) |

## Part 2: the entries, with their full rulings


### action

**גישה / קירוב** — approach | gishah, gisha, vayigash, higish, higishah, nigash, kiruv, keiruv | draft | [approach-gishah-kiruv.md](entries/approach-gishah-kiruv.md)

*Gishah* is **to approach** in the strong sense: coming right up to something, and usually across a gap of standing or in awe of it. *Kiruv* is **to draw near** or **bring closer**, a step taken while still on the way, between parties on a level. So Yehudah *vayigash* to the viceroy (Bereishis 44:18) because he came up close and deliberately, not merely nearer. In Aramaic the distinction disappears: Targum renders both by *kiruv* cognates.

**חמוש / חלוץ / מזוין / זין / ויירק / חומש / חלצים / אזן** — armed | chamush, chamushim, chomesh, chimesh, chalutz, chalutzim, chalatzim, chalitzah | draft | [armed-chamush-chalutz-mezuyan.md](entries/armed-chamush-chalutz-mezuyan.md)

All three mean **armed**, and the Midrashim equate them outright. *Chamush* is the Chumash's word (Shemos 13:18), tied by the grammarians to the *chomesh*, the flank where a weapon is girded, and by the derashos to *chamesh*, five. *Chalutz* is the **vanguard**, the men drawn out from the nation to go at the head of the army. *Mezuyan* is post-biblical, from Aramaic *zayin*, a weapon. Targum renders all of them (and *vayarek* of Avraham) by *zariz*, ready at a moment's notice. The trap: at Shemos 13:18 "armed" and "a fifth of them" are both live readings of one word, so translate one and footnote the other.

**חרש / חשב / רקם / אמן / חרש** — artisan | charash, choresh, charashim, choshev, maaseh choshev, rokem, roikem, rikmah | draft | [artisan-charash-choshev-rokem.md](entries/artisan-charash-choshev-rokem.md)

The Torah's three words for Betzalel's and Oholiav's skills (Shemos 35:35, 38:23) are not synonyms. *Charash* is the **craftsman** who reshapes raw material, wood, stone or metal. *Choshev* is the **designer**, who plans and combines finished materials into a figured work, hence *maaseh choshev* brocade. *Rokem* is the **embroiderer**, working in colors. The trap: both are "embroidery" in English, and the Gemara (Yoma 72b) distinguishes them by how many faces the work shows, which no single English word carries. Keep both transliterated in any Mishkan passage.

**בוא / ביאה / הליכה / הלך / אתה** — coming | bo, boh, biah, bi'ah, lavo, lehavi, lech, leich | draft | [coming-biah-halichah-atah.md](entries/coming-biah-halichah-atah.md)

*Bo* is **come**: it looks at the destination, and it covers both the arriving and the being on the way. *Lech* is **go**: the setting out and the walking itself, with no necessary entering. *Atah* is **arrive**: like *bo* it faces the destination, but it names only the arrival and never the journey. What a translator must not do is let one English "come" cover *bo* and *atah* together; where a pasuk has both, *atah* is the one that has landed.

**בקיעה / קריעה / גזירה / חתיכה / בתר / נתח / פתיתה / מילה / פלח / קטב / פרימה / גדע / קציצה / קטיפה / כרת** — cutting | bokea, bekiah, bakia, korea, kriah, kriyah, keriah, gozer | draft | [cutting-bokea-korea-gozer.md](entries/cutting-bokea-korea-gozer.md)

*Bokea* is **splitting open**, from the inside outward. *Korea* is **tearing**, and specifically of something soft, by a force from outside. *Gozer* is **cutting** with a blade through something thick, and by extension **decreeing**. *Chatichah* is the ordinary Rabbinic word for **cutting**, originally cutting in half. The trap: English "cut" and "split" will absorb all of them, and the splitting of the sea is called by all three verbs in different sources, each saying something different about who did what to what.

**ריקוד / כרכור / פזז / רקד / מרקד / מכרכר / מפזז** — dancing | rikud, rikkud, rikod, rekidah, miraked, mirakedin, kirkur, micharker | draft | [dancing-rikud-kirkur-pizuz.md](entries/dancing-rikud-kirkur-pizuz.md)

*Rikud* is the general word for **dancing**, and per Rabbi Moshe Tedeschi-Ashkenazi it names the joy that is the occasion for it rather than any particular step. *Kirkur* (*micharker*) is the footwork: **whirling, capering**, which Rabbi Yosef Kara glosses as circular motion. *Pizuz* (*mifazez*) is the wild, fast part: **prancing** or **leaping about**, the hands and fingers moving with the feet. The one thing not to get wrong: at Shmuel II 6:14 David is only *micharker*, and at 6:16, through Michal's eyes, he is *mifazez umecharker*. The added verb is the point of the story, so the English must differ across the two pesukim; never render both as "danced."

**כרה / חפר / חפירה / חצב / חציבה / הכרה** — dig | karah, kara, korah, chafirah, chafira, chofer, chafar, chatzivah | draft | [dig-karah-chafirah-chatzivah.md](entries/dig-karah-chafirah-chatzivah.md)

*Karah* is the opening of a dig, the preparatory or partial digging that does not finish the work: **dig** in the sense of **begin to dig, hollow out**. *Chafirah* (verb *chofer*) is digging carried to completion and depth, down to the water: **dig out, dig through**; in the abstract, **search out**. *Chatzivah* is hewing rock with a tool: **hew, quarry** (Malbim; Rabbi Yosef Kara instead makes it digging a round pit). The one thing not to get wrong: a verse that uses both verbs of one pit (Tehillim 7:16; Bereishis 26:25 against 26:32; Bamidbar 21:18) is not repeating itself, and *karah* is the *earlier, lesser* act (Malbim, Mecklenburg, Netziv, Rav Hirsch), so never render the pair as "dug and dug" or reverse their weight.

**דלה / שאב / דלי / דליות / דל** — drawing water | dalah, dalo, daloh, dilitani, yidlenah, shoev, shoeiv, shaav | draft | [drawing-water-dalah-shoev.md](entries/drawing-water-dalah-shoev.md)

Both verbs mean **draw water**, and the difference is effort. *Dalah* is drawing from a deep well: lower the pail, haul it up, so render it **draw up** or **draw out** (Malbim). *Shoev* is drawing where the water is already at hand, from a spring or surface pool, so plain **draw** is right. If a passage turns on the labor involved, do not let one English "draw" cover both.

**שתיה / השקאה / משקה / הגמיאיני / תשוקה / שוקק / משק** — drinking | shtiyah, shesiyah, lishtos, lishtot, hashkaah, lehashkos, lehashkot, hishkah | draft | [drinking-shtiyah-hashkaah-gemiah.md](entries/drinking-shtiyah-hashkaah-gemiah.md)

*Lishtos* is **to drink**, the act itself, and it is said only of people and animals; over 220 appearances in Tanach. *Lehashkos* is **to give to drink**, to water, almost always in *hifil*; over 60 appearances, and it covers watering the ground as well as people and beasts, because it names the providing and not the drinking. *Mashkeh* is the **beverage**, often a strong one. *Hagmieini* is Rivkah's word, **let me sip** or **let me swallow**. The trap: English "drink" does both jobs, so "she gave him to drink" and "he drank" collapse into one verb; in the Rivkah narrative the Torah alternates the two roots deliberately, and the translation has to keep them apart.

**סבא / שכר / שיכרון / שכר / זולל** — drunkenness | sove, soveh, sova, sava, savim, sovim, savaim, savyuta | draft | [drunkenness-sove-shachar-sheichar.md](entries/drunkenness-sove-shachar-sheichar.md)

*Sove* is **swilling**, drinking intoxicants to excess; by Malbim it names the drinking itself, whether or not the drinker actually got drunk. *Shachar* is **getting drunk**, the state achieved. The noun *sheichar* is the **strong drink** that produces it. In the *ben sorer u'moreh* pasuk, *zollel* is the meat side (gluttony) and *sove* the drink side (swilling); do not let one English word cover both. The trap: "drunkenness" absorbs *sove* and *shichrus* alike, and Malbim's distinction is exactly the difference between the two.

**תמורה / חליפה / חלף / המר ימיר** — exchange | temurah, temura, hamer yamir, yamir, vayamiru, chalifah, chalifa, chalipha | draft | [exchange-temurah-chalifah.md](entries/exchange-temurah-chalifah.md)

*Temurah* is **substitution**: putting a second thing in the place of the first, with the eye on the man who does it and, in most readings, on a downgrade. *Chalifah* is **exchange** or **swapping out**: the eye is on the thing being gotten rid of, and it is the neutral or even upgrading word. Vayikra 27:10 uses both of the same prohibited act; Vayikra 27:33, stating the consequence, uses only *hamer yamir*. Do not let one English verb carry both, and do not read *chalifah* as merely a stylistic variant.

**נס / ניסה / בורח / בריחה / רץ / ערק** — fleeing | nas, nos, nisah, nissah, vayanusu, veyanusu, boreach, borach | draft | [fleeing-nas-boreach.md](entries/fleeing-nas-boreach.md)

*Nas* is **fleeing** from a danger already upon a man, openly and in terror. *Boreach* is **running away** from a feared future threat, with nobody actually in pursuit, and usually done quietly so as not to be seen. Sforno and Malbim agree on that core. The trap: English "flee" and "escape" will take either word, but *boreach* is the one that carries stealth and a threat not yet arrived, so never render it "fled in terror."

**מכה / מכות / מלקות / לקה** — flogging | makkah, maka, makkot, makkos, malkut, malkus, malkuyot, lakah | draft | [flogging-makkot-malkut.md](entries/flogging-makkot-malkut.md)

*Makkah* is a **blow**, and from there a **wound** and a **plague**; the plural *makkot* is the Torah's own word for judicial **lashes**. *Malkut* is the Mishnaic word for the same punishment, **flogging**, built on the Aramaic *lakah*. They translate alike but are not one word in two coats: *makkeh* is the one who strikes and *lokeh* the one struck (Rabbi Eliyahu HaBachur), and several authorities read a real difference of scope into the pair. Do not render *lakah* "hit" everywhere; outside the flogging sugyos it means impaired, not struck.

**מחילה / סליחה / כפרה** — forgiveness | mechilah, mechila, mechilas, mechilat, mochel, mocheil, mechal, selichah | draft | [forgiveness-mechilah-selichah-kapparah.md](entries/forgiveness-mechilah-selichah-kapparah.md)

*Selichah* is **forgiveness** proper: the bond between the sinner and the One sinned against is mended (Rav Hirsch); it is the Biblical word (*salachti*, Bamidbar 14:20). *Mechilah* is **pardon**: the penalty remitted, the debt waived (Rav Hirsch); it is Rabbinic, never Biblical, and doubles as the word for forgoing what one is owed. *Kapparah* is **atonement**: every consequence of the sin wiped out (Rav Hirsch), at a price and usually through a korban (Malbim). The liturgy's סלח לנו, מחל לנו, כפר לנו wants three English words, "forgive us, pardon us, grant us atonement"; never let "forgive" cover all three. Rav Hirsch's three-way map is followed here because it is the only account that places all three words.

**אסיפה / קבץ / אגודה / קהילה / עדה / אגר** — gathering | asifah, asaf, asof, leesof, asif, kovetz, kavatz, lekavetz | draft | [gathering-asifah-kovetz-kehillah-agur.md](entries/gathering-asifah-kovetz-kehillah-agur.md)

*Asifah* is **gathering in**, bringing scattered things to where they belong; *kovetz* is **collecting**, with no bringing-in implied. *Aggudah* goes further: the gathered are **bound** into one unit. *Kehillah* is an **assembly** of people, *eidah* a **congregation** joined for a purpose, *agur* is **stockpiling** for later. English "gather" absorbs the first two, so where they stand in consecutive pesukim the difference must show in the English.

**שמירה / נצירה / נטירה** — guarding | shemirah, shmirah, shemira, shomer, shamar, tishmor, netzirah, netzira | draft | [guard-shemirah-netzirah-netirah.md](entries/guard-shemirah-netzirah-netirah.md)

*Shemirah* is the general word, and it is bounded in time: **guard**, **watch**, **keep**, and by extension **wait for** something whose hour has not yet come. *Netzirah* is the stronger and the perpetual watch: **safeguard**, **watch over** (unceasingly). *Netirah* is holding something in the mind: **harbor** (a grudge), **hold on to**. The trap is *lo sitor* (Vayikra 19:18); it is not "do not guard" but "do not keep it stored up inside," and any English that uses "keep" there will read as the wrong verb.

**גילוח / גזיזה / גלב / ספר / מגלב / מספרים / תער** — haircut | giluach, gilu'ach, galach, gizah, geziza, gezizah, gozez, geiz | draft | [haircut-giluach-gizah-galav-sapar.md](entries/haircut-giluach-gizah-galav-sapar.md)

*Giluach* is **shaving**, a person's hair cut down to the skin; *gizah* is **shearing**, an animal's wool, and a cut that leaves something standing. *Galav* is a **barber** (or his razor), a *hapax legomenon* in Yechezkel 5:1. *Sapar* is not Biblical at all: it is the Targumic and Mishnaic word, "barber" and "to give a haircut," and it is the ordinary word today. The trap is that English "cut hair" covers all of them; the *metzora* and the Nazir are *giluach* and never *gizah*, and the difference is halachically load-bearing.

**פסח / דילוג / קפיצה / פיסח** — jumping | pesach, pesah, poseach, piseach, pisei'ach, dilug, dilluk, doleg | draft | [jumping-pesach-dilug-kefitzah.md](entries/jumping-pesach-dilug-kefitzah.md)

*Dilug* is **skipping**: the focus is on what got passed over. *Kefitzah* is **leaping**: the focus is on covering ground fast. *Pesach* holds both, which is what Rashi means when he calls it an expression of *dilug* and *kefitzah* together, so "pass over" is right for the Korban Pesach and the makkah, and "jump" is wrong. Do not let one English word cover *dilug* and *kefitzah* where a pasuk sets them side by side.

**נוהג / מוליך / נחה / נהל / מנהג / מנהיג / מנהל** — leading | noheg, nohaig, nihag, vayinhag, yinhagu, manhig, minhag, nahag | draft | [leading-noheg-molich-nachah-nohel.md](entries/leading-noheg-molich-nachah-nohel.md)

Four verbs, all "lead," and they are not interchangeable. *Noheg* is to **drive**: leading by force, from strength, keeping the charges on a fixed course, and it is the one word of the four that can be used of leading something to its ruin. *Molich* is to **take** or **bring**, a form of *holech*, moving people from one place or state to another. *Nachah* is to **guide**: leading that shields those led from harm and brings them to rest. *Nohel* is to **lead with care**, the leader who not only shields but provides and sustains. The trap is Shiras HaYam, where *nachisa* and *neihalta* stand in one pasuk (Shemos 15:13); one English "led" for both erases the very contrast the pasuk is built on.

**לחיכה / לקיקה / חיך / ילק / מליקה** — licking | lechichah, lechicha, lechikhah, lichach, lakak, lekikah, lekika, yalok | draft | [licking-lechichah-lekikah.md](entries/licking-lechichah-lekikah.md)

Both mean **lick**, and the early lexicographers define one by the other, so a translator is usually safe either way. Where the passage turns on the difference: *lekikah* is **lapping up** liquid, the dog-at-the-water sound (Rabbi Moshe Tedeschi-Ashkenazi); *lechichah* is **licking away** something firmer, an ox stripping a field bare (Bamidbar 22:4). The one thing not to get wrong is that *lechichah* in a war context is not a picture of affection: it means consuming, wiping clean, and English "lick" in the sense of *defeat* is exactly the register the Moabites intended.

**עירוב / מזיגה / דימוע / דמעה / זג / נבוך / בוכה / נחמה** — mixing | iruv, eiruv, irbuv, taarovet, taaruvos, hisarvu, vayisarvu, mizug | draft | [mixing-iruv-mizug-dimua.md](entries/mixing-iruv-mizug-dimua.md)

*Iruv* is the general word and it is for **dry** things intermingling: **mixing, intermingling**. *Mizug* is for **liquids**, specifically the pouring of one into another: **mixing, diluting** (a cup of wine). *Dimua* is the halachic term for **terumah** that has fallen into unsanctified produce: **commingling** (leave it transliterated in a halachic passage). The one thing not to miss: *dimua* and *dimah* ("tear") are the same root ד-מ-ע, and most rishonim hold the two senses connected, so a source that plays tears against mixture is not punning loosely.

**רציחה / הריגה / קטילה / מיתה** — murder | retzichah, retzicha, rotzeach, ratzach, tirtzach, harigah, hariga, harag | draft | [murder-retzichah-harigah-ketilah.md](entries/murder-retzichah-harigah-ketilah.md)

*Retzichah* is **murder**: killing with no legal warrant, and the word itself passes the verdict. *Harigah* is **killing** plain, lawful or not, direct or brought about at a distance, and it covers animals and plants as well as people. *Ketilah* is the Aramaic word the Targumim use for both, so render it "kill"; in the few Hebrew pesukim it is disputed. Not to get wrong: *lo tirtzach* is "do not murder," never "do not kill," and the choice of root is why shechitah and judicial execution are not in the prohibition.

**הניא / יניא / הפר / הפרה / בטל / בטלה / פור / נא** — nullify | heini, heni, yani, hafarah, hafaras nedarim, heifer, hefer, batel | draft | [nullify-heini-heifer-batel.md](entries/nullify-heini-heifer-batel.md)

*Heini* (*yani*) works on what has not yet come about: **restrain**, **hold back**, stop a plan from being carried out. *Heifer* works on what already exists: **annul**, **break**, crumble a standing legal reality out of being. That is why Bamidbar 30 uses both of a husband nullifying his wife's neder: he *heini*s her plan and *heifer*s the vow (Rabbi Shlomo Aharon Wertheimer). *Batel* is the Rabbinic word, largely Aramaic in origin: **void**, and by extension **idle, futile**. The trap is rendering *heini* and *heifer* both as "annul" in a single pasuk, which is the one place the Torah is distinguishing them.

**נתן / שם / שת / סמים / בשם / אסם / סימן / שתות / שתי** — placing | natan, nasan, sam, sim, som, shat, shas, shes | draft | [placing-natan-sam-shat.md](entries/placing-natan-sam-shat.md)

Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim ranks the three by how deliberate and how lasting the placing is. *Natan* is the loosest: **put** something down, anywhere. *Sam* is **place**, into a spot designated for it, as part of a plan, but for no stated duration. *Shat* is **set** or **lay**, placed into an order and meant to stay there indefinitely. The trap is that English "put" flattens all three; where a pasuk switches words mid-sentence (Shemos 10:1-2, Shemos 40:18) the switch is the whole point.

**לחץ / דחק / נגישה / נוגשים / אץ / אלץ** — pressure | lachatz, lochatzim, lochatz, dchak, dechak, d'chak, negisah, negisha | draft | [pressure-lachatz-dchak-negisah-atz.md](entries/pressure-lachatz-dchak-negisah-atz.md)

*Lachatz* is **pressure** applied, with the eye on the one applying it. *D'chak* is **crowding** or **squeezing out**, pressure that shoves a man from his place, usually so that someone else can have it. *Negisah* is the **taskmaster's driving**, relentless and up close. *Atz* is **hurrying** someone along. *Alatz* is **wearing a man down with words**. The trap: English "press" takes all five. The Haggadah's *v'es lachatzeinu, zeh had'chak* is a gloss of one by the other, so a translation that renders both "oppression" makes the derashah say nothing.

**משה / משך / שאב / שאף / משי** — pulling and drawing | moshe, mashah, mishitihu, yamsheini, mashui, moshech, meshech, moshech | draft | [pulling-moshe-moshech-shoev-shoef.md](entries/pulling-moshe-moshech-shoev-shoef.md)

*Moshech* is the general verb: **pull, draw**, taking something from its place toward oneself. *Moshe* is narrow and almost always in water: **draw out of the water** (Rashbam). *Shoev* is **draw water**, in Tanach from a well; *shoef* is its air counterpart, **inhale**, and in *sheifah*, **yearning** (Wertheimer). English "draw" swallows all four; where the Hebrew is *moshe*, keep the water.

**פוצר / דוחה / דוחף / הדף / דופק** — pushing | potzer, potzeir, patzar, docheh, dochech, dachah, dechitani, dechi | draft | [pushing-potzer-docheh-dochef-hadof.md](entries/pushing-potzer-docheh-dochef-hadof.md)

*Potzer* is to **press** or **urge** someone until he relents; it is almost always about a person's will, not his body. *Docheh* is to **push away**, to remove a thing from its place, and in halachic Hebrew for one law to **override** another. *Dochef* is to **hurry** someone, pushed into moving faster than planned. *Hadof* is to **thrust away** or **dislodge**, particularly what is loosely held. *Dofek* is repeated beating: to **knock** at a door, to **drive** animals too hard, later a **pulse**. The trap: English "push" will take all five, and *docheh* in a sugya is not physical at all.

**מוסר / תוכחה / תוכחת / יסורים** — rebuke | mussar, musar, mosar, yissurin, yisurin, yissurim, yisurim, tochachah | draft | [rebuke-mussar-tochachah.md](entries/rebuke-mussar-tochachah.md)

*Tochachah* is **reproof** (or **rebuke**): spoken, argued, proving to the other that he did wrong, about his past deeds, and possible between equals. *Mussar* is **discipline** (or **chastisement**): from a superior to a subordinate, by word or by blow, aimed at his future conduct; the Vilna Gaon ties it to *yissurin*, afflictions. Together, מוסר ותוכחת is "discipline and reproof." Do not let "rebuke" absorb both; and when *mussar* means the ethical discipline itself (the sefarim, the study, the movement) it is a proper term, **moral instruction**, and not "rebuke" at all.

**גאולה / פדיון / פדות / גואל / פודה / גאל / פדה / פדע** — redemption | geulah, ge'ulah, geula, goel, go'el, pidyon, pidyan, pedus | draft | [redemption-geulah-pidyon.md](entries/redemption-geulah-pidyon.md)

*Pidyon* (verb *podeh*) is redemption by exchange: **ransom**, **buying back**, the fact of being freed. *Geulah* (verb *goel*) is redemption by right of kinship and by force, with no payment: **redemption**, **deliverance**, and it carries the sense of being raised up, not merely let out. The majority reading (Vilna Gaon, Malbim, Chatam Sofer) makes *geulah* the fuller and warmer term and *pidyon* the transactional one; a translator must not flatten *pidyon* into "redemption" where the passage is about a price paid or a firstborn traded.

**שוב / תשובה / השבה / חזרה / שבת** — return | shuv, shuva, shuvah, teshuva, teshuvah, hashava, hashavah, lehashiv | draft | [return-shuv-chazarah.md](entries/return-shuv-chazarah.md)

*Shuv* is returning **to** a place or condition: **return**, and *teshuvah* is **repentance** precisely because it is a return, not a mere improvement. *Chazarah* is the about-face itself, the act of **turning back** or **reverting** from where one now stands. The fact a translator must not lose: *shuv* names the destination, *chazarah* names the U-turn, so *chazar b'teshuvah* is not a redundancy but two halves of one motion. *Chazarah* is Mishnaic; it never appears in Tanach, so a rendering of a pasuk should never lean on it.

**ישועה / תשועה / הצלה / פורקן / שועה / מושיע / מציל** — salvation | yeshuah, yeshua, yeshuos, yeshuot, teshuah, teshua, teshuos, teshuot | draft | [salvation-yeshuah-teshuah-hatzalah-purkan.md](entries/salvation-yeshuah-teshuah-hatzalah-purkan.md)

*Yeshuah* and *teshuah* are the grand words: **salvation**, total release from oppression, and specifically salvation that answers a cry (*shav'ah*) for help. *Hatzalah* is the plain one: **rescue**, being pulled out of danger (root sense "separate, remove"); the Rokeach calls it partial or temporary next to *yeshuah*. *Purkan* (Aramaic, the Targum's *pidyon*) is **redemption**, being bought back. The one thing not to get wrong: *hatzalah* is not "salvation" in the full sense; render it "rescue" or "saving," and reserve "salvation" for *yeshuah*/*teshuah*.

**חטא / עון / פשע / עבירה** — sin | chet, cheit, chait, chatas, chatat, chataim, chatati, chatasi | draft | [sin-chet-avon-pesha.md](entries/sin-chet-avon-pesha.md)

*Chet* is the general and lightest word: **sin**, and beside its neighbors the **inadvertent sin** (*shogeg*). *Avon* is **iniquity**, the willful sin (*meizid*). *Pesha* is the **rebellious sin**, done to defy Hashem. That is the Gemara's ladder (Yoma 36b), and the *vidui* keeps its ascending order: "I have sinned, I have done iniquity, I have rebelled." Rabbi Netanel Weil: the ladder holds only when the words stand together; a lone *chet* or *avon* covers every kind of sin and is simply "sin." *Aveirah*, the Rabbinic catch-all, is **transgression**.

**שחיטה / זביחה / טביחה / טבח / נחירה** — slaughter | shechitah, shechita, shechitas, zevichah, zevicha, zevach, zevah, tevichah | draft | [slaughter-shechitah-zevichah-tevichah.md](entries/slaughter-shechitah-zevichah-tevichah.md)

*Shechitah* is **ritual slaughter** in the halachic sense, and by Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim's reading it is also the **general word for putting a living thing to death**. *Zevichah* is slaughter **for a purpose**, above all **sacrificial slaughter**. *Tevichah* is slaughter **for eating**, plain **butchering**, with no ritual sense at all. The trap is that English "slaughter" swallows all three; where a pasuk turns on the difference, name the purpose (sacrifice, food) rather than repeat the same verb.

**שמיטה / חליצה / שלף / של / אלץ** — slipping off | shemittah, shmita, shemitah, shamat, hishmit, chalitzah, chalitza, chalatz | draft | [slipping-off-shemittah-chalitzah-shalaf.md](entries/slipping-off-shemittah-chalitzah-shalaf.md)

*Shemittah* is **releasing**: loosening one's grip so that a thing slips out of one's control, which is why the same word covers letting a field lie and letting a debt go. *Chalitzah* is **extracting** something from what encloses it. *Shalaf* is **drawing** a thing quickly out of a loose holder, most often a sword from its sheath. *Shal* is a shoe **slipped off** with no hands at all. The trap: all four become "remove" in English, and then Shoresh Yesha's three-way distinction among the shoe-removal verbs disappears.

**תור / רגל / מרגלים / תרים / חפר** — spying | tor, tur, latur, lasur, tarim, ragel, leragel, meragel | draft | [spying-latur-leragel.md](entries/spying-latur-leragel.md)

The two verbs are not interchangeable. *Latur* is to **scout out** with an eye for what is good; *leragel* is to **spy out** with an eye for what is weak (Rabbi Yaakov Tzvi Mecklenburg). Bamidbar calls Moshe's twelve *tarim*, **scouts**, throughout, and never *meraglim*; only Devarim, looking back, uses *vayachperu* and *leragel*. A translation that calls them "the spies" in Bamidbar has already decided the parashah's question.

**סקילה / רגימה / מרגמה / רגם** — stoning | sekilah, skilah, sakal, saklu, sakel, regimah, regem, ragam | draft | [stoning-sekilah-regimah.md](entries/stoning-sekilah-regimah.md)

Both mean **stoning**, and Targum collapses them (it renders *sekilah* as *regimah* every time), so the difference has to be carried by the English. *Sekilah* names the act of throwing stones and does not by itself mean the man died, which is why the Torah adds *mos yumas* alongside it; render it **stoning** or **pelting with stones**. *Regimah* names the outcome, a man buried and subdued under a heap thrown by the whole community; render it **stoning to death**. The same root ס-ק-ל also means clearing a field of stones, the exact opposite use of stones, so context alone decides.

**חדל / מנע / חשך / כלא / פסק** — stopping | chadal, chodel, vechadal, mana, mona, timna, chasach, chosech | draft | [stopping-chadal-mana-chasach-kala-pasak.md](entries/stopping-chadal-mana-chasach-kala-pasak.md)

*Chadal* is **cease**: it stops because it cannot go on, and the halt comes from within. *Mana* is **withhold**, a deliberate refusal, usually of what should not have been refused. *Chasach* is **hold back** or **spare**, a check applied from outside. *Kala* stops because the reason has lapsed: **cease**, **be held back**. *Pasak* is **break off** in the middle. All five take English "stop," which is the trap; where two stand near each other, give each its own verb.

**זנות / זנה / סטיה / שטה / סוטה / שוטה / שטן / שיטה** — straying from a path | zenus, zenut, zanah, zonah, vezanu, zan, mazon, stiyah | draft | [straying-zenus-stiyah.md](entries/straying-zenus-stiyah.md)

Both roots name departure from a track, and neither is primarily about the sin English readers expect. *Zenus* is **straying** from a loyalty owed, which is why the Nevi'im use it for avodah zarah as readily as for adultery; render it by the disloyalty, not by "prostitution," unless the pasuk is literal. *Stiyah* is **deviation** from a marked path, and its family (*sotah*, *shoteh*, *satan*, *shtus*) all name something off the track rather than something wicked. The trap is that English "immorality" and "madness" carry judgments the Hebrew roots do not.

**פליטה / פליט / שריד / קליטה / מקלט / שרד** — survivor | pleitah, pleita, peleitah, palit, palet, pelet, sarid, sridah | draft | [survivor-pleitah-sarid-klitah.md](entries/survivor-pleitah-sarid-klitah.md)

*Pleitah* is rescue by **expulsion**: the man is spit out of the danger, so a *palit* is a **refugee**. *Klitah* is rescue by **reception**: the man is taken in, as a *miklat* receives him, so render it **refuge** or **taking in**. *Sarid* is the one who could not get out and lived anyway: **survivor** or **remnant**. The trap is the stock pair *sarid u'palit*, where "survivor" will swallow both; keep them apart as "survivor nor refugee."

**גניבה / גנב / גזילה / גזלן / חמס / חמסן / חמצן / לסטים** — theft | genevah, genivah, geneivah, ganav, gannav, gezeilah, gezelah, gezel | draft | [theft-genevah-gezeilah-chamas.md](entries/theft-genevah-gezeilah-chamas.md)

*Genevah* is theft **by stealth**: the *ganav* hides himself, so render it **stealing** and the actor **thief**. *Gezeilah* is taking **openly and by force**, so render it **robbery** and the actor **robber**. The distinction is not severity but visibility, and the halachah runs the opposite way to intuition: the Torah fines the *ganav* double and the *gazlan* not at all. *Chamas* is broader, **violence** or **injustice**, sometimes theft; in Chazal a *chamsan* is a coercive buyer who does pay, so he is a wrongdoer but not a thief. *Listim* is a Greek loanword for an armed **brigand**, and whether he counts as *ganav* or *gazlan* is left open.

**השלכה / זריקה / הזיה / רמה** — throwing | hashlachah, hashlacha, vayashlech, vatashlech, zerikah, zerika, zarak, zarko | draft | [throw-hashlachah-zerikah-hazayah-ramah.md](entries/throw-hashlachah-zerikah-hazayah-ramah.md)

*Hashlachah* is careless, disparaging throwing: **throw away**, **cast off**, or even **drop**; it says nothing about force or target. *Zerikah* is deliberate, forceful throwing at a place: **throw**, and where the object is fine or liquid, **sprinkle**. *Hazayah* is *zerikah* at a distance (or, per Malbim, by hand rather than by vessel): **sprinkle**. *Ramah* is throwing upward in an arc onto its target: **hurl**, **shoot**. The trap is treating *hashlachah* as vigorous; Maharal and Rashbam both read it as the opposite.

**רומס / דורך / בוס / רמש** — trampling | romes, ramas, remos, tirmos, romsem, dorech, darach, tidroch | draft | [trampling-romes-dorech-boss.md](entries/trampling-romes-dorech-boss.md)

*Dorech* is neutral: **tread**, **step on**, and in a winepress or olive press **press**. *Romes* is destructive: **trample**, treading meant to smash what lies under the foot. *Boss* is contemptuous: **trample down**, treading on what had no business being trodden on, so that the act itself says the thing is worth nothing. When the three are close together, keep "tread" for *dorech* and do not spend "trample" on it; Tehillim 91:13 pairs *tidroch* and *tirmos* in one pasuk and the English has to hold them apart.

**קשירה / אסירה / קטירה / כפות / צרור / עקידה / חבישה / חיתול / איסור** — tying | keshirah, keshira, kesher, kasher, asirah, asira, assur, asir | draft | [tying-keshirah-asirah-kafut.md](entries/tying-keshirah-asirah-kafut.md)

*Keshirah* is **tying** where the thing tied goes along with it: **bind, tie, join**. *Asirah* is **tying down** against the will of the one tied: **bind, detain, restrain**, and it is the root of *assir* (prisoner) and *issur* (prohibition). *Kafut* is the Mishnaic and Aramaic **tied down** of a man or an animal held fast under compulsion. *Tzror* is **binding tight**, two things knotted into one bundle. *Ketirah* is simply the Aramaic of *keshirah*. Malbim's consent test is the one thing not to get wrong: consent belongs to *keshirah*, coercion to *asirah*.

**מלחמה / לוחם / קרב / פולמוס / תרוניא / קניגיא** — war | milchamah, milchama, lochem, locheim, krav, kerav, pulmus, pulmos | draft | [war-milchamah-krav-pulmus.md](entries/war-milchamah-krav-pulmus.md)

*Milchamah* is the general Biblical word: **war**, the whole enterprise, over three hundred times in Tanach, with the verb *lochem*, **to wage war**. *Krav* is the closing of the two sides: **battle, combat**, the engagement itself (*yom krav*, *klei krav*). *Pulmus* is Mishnaic and names a particular **campaign** or war of a named enemy (the *pulmus* of Vespasian, of Titus). Do not render *krav* "war" in a pasuk where *milchamah* also appears; *krav* is the fighting, *milchamah* the conflict.

**כביסה / רחיצה / שטיפה / הדחה / מריקה / תמרוק / קינוח** — washing | kevisah, kevissah, kibbus, vchibsu, rechitzah, rechitza, rachatz, shetifah | draft | [washing-kevisah-rechitzah-shetifah-hadachah.md](entries/washing-kevisah-rechitzah-shetifah-hadachah.md)

*Kevisah* is **laundering**: objects only, never a body, with the water going right through the cloth. *Rechitzah* is **washing** a body or a part of one, by immersion or by pouring. *Shetifah* is **sluicing**, cleaning under a strong flow of much water. *Hadachah* is **rinsing**, the same surface work with little water. *Merikah* is not water-cleaning at all by Rabbi Avraham Bedersi but **scouring**, buffing off rust or tarnish. *Kinuach*, Rabbinic only, is **wiping**. The trap: English "wash" absorbs the first four at once, and Vayikra 6:21 and Yeshayahu 4:4 each put two of them in a single verse.

**מלאכה / עבודה / מלאכת עבודה / שירות** — work | melachah, melacha, melakhah, melochoh, meleches, melechet, melachos, melachot | draft | [work-melachah-avodah.md](entries/work-melachah-avodah.md)

*Melachah* is **work** as the thing accomplished: directed at a result, creative, the idea carried into a product (Rav Hirsch; Rabbi Yitzchak Shmuel Reggio; Rabbi Naftali Hertz Wessely); in hilchos Shabbos it is the technical name of the thirty-nine categories, and there it is "work" or *melachah*, never "labor." *Avodah* is **labor** or **service**: the worker's own toil and subjection, work owed to a master, hard or obligatory (Malbim; Rabbi Shlomo Aharon Wertheimer; Alshich; Pappenheim), and, turned toward Heaven, the **service** of Hashem. *Sheirus* is light **service**. "Work" absorbs both main words; give *melachah* "work" and *avodah* "labor" or "service," and keep "service" wherever *avodah* is *avodas Hashem*.


### body

**עור / עורון / סומא / סגי נהור / סנורים** — blindness | iver, ivver, ivaron, ivvaron, averet, avares, suma, sumah | draft | [blindness-iver-suma-sagi-nahor-sanverim.md](entries/blindness-iver-suma-sagi-nahor-sanverim.md)

*Iver* is the Biblical **blind man** and *ivaron* his **blindness**; *suma* is the same in Aramaic. *Sagi nahor*, "light enough," is the euphemism, to be rendered so the euphemism survives. *Sanverim* is not ordinary blindness but a **dazzlement** or disordering of sight, struck miraculously; it occurs three times in Tanach and every authority reads it as something other than *ivaron*. Not to be lost: for Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim *iver* names an eye that works but is covered over, which is what makes the bribed-judge metaphor work.

**שחין / גרב / חרס / ילפת / אבעבועות** — boils | shechin, shchin, shichana, garav, gerev, cheres, charas, yalefet | draft | [boils-shechin-garav-cheres.md](entries/boils-shechin-garav-cheres.md)

*Shechin* is the umbrella word: **boils**, or more exactly an **inflamed sore**, a wound gone hot, not a burn. The other three are kinds of it. Paired, *garav* is the **moist** sort and *cheres* the **dry**; *garav* on its own means the dry sort (Rashi). *Yalefet* is the sort that **clings**, never leaving the man. *Avabuos* are the **blisters** of the makkah in Mitzrayim. The thing not to get wrong: *garav* changes meaning depending on whether *cheres* stands beside it, so check the pasuk before choosing a word.

**עצם / גרם / גרמא / גרמני** — bone | etzem, atzamos, atzamot, atzmi, atzmo, atzmus, gerem, garem | draft | [bone-etzem-gerem.md](entries/bone-etzem-gerem.md)

Both mean **bone**, and both also mean **the thing itself**. *Etzem* is the bone as something hard, strong, and self-contained, and it is the general word; *gerem* is the bone as one part within a frame, which is why the same root gives a single step of a staircase and, in rabbinic Hebrew, *grama*, indirect **causation** (Rav Hirsch). The Vilna Gaon puts the line differently: *etzem* is the bone's hard outside, *gerem* the hollow inside where the marrow is. The trap: English "bone" absorbs both, and with them the reason *grama* is a word about causing rather than about bones.

**שדים / שד / דדים / דד / עטינים / זיז / לשד** — breast | shadayim, shadaim, shad, shod, dadim, dad, atinim, atin | draft | [breast-shadayim-dadim-atinim.md](entries/breast-shadayim-dadim-atinim.md)

*Shadayim* (singular *shad* or *shod*) is the standard Biblical word: **breasts**, twenty-four times in Tanach, only once in Chumash (Bereishis 49:25). *Dadim* is the rarer Biblical pair-word, and by the Mishnah's Hebrew it plainly means **breasts** too, including breast-shaped **spigots**. *Atinim* is a hapax in Iyov 21:24, understood as **breasts** or, of an animal, **udders**. *Ziz* (Yeshayahu 66:11) is disputed: Radak reads it as *ziv*, **radiance**, while others read it as **breast**. Rabbi Shlomo of Urbino (*Ohel Moed*) lists the first three as Biblical synonyms; do not assume *dadim* always matches *shadayim*, since Malbim holds they name different parts of the body.

**כסס / כסיסה / לעס / לעיסה / יקוסס / כסנין / קסקס** — chewing | koses, kosses, kas, kesisah, kesisa, kisisah, lachus, lo'es | draft | [chew-koses-loes.md](entries/chew-koses-loes.md)

Both are rabbinic; neither is biblical, and Biblical Hebrew has no plain verb for chewing at all (*maaleh geirah* means brings up the cud, not chews it). Render *koses* **chew** in the sense of gnawing something raw or eating it in an unusual way, and *lo'es* **chew** in the ordinary sense of working food before swallowing. Rashi flatly equates them (*lo'es* means *koses*), so treat them as synonyms unless a halachic passage turns on the difference; where it does, follow Rabbi Meir Mazuz: *le'isah* implies chewing toward swallowing, *kesisah* does not.

**בגד / לבוש / מלבוש / כסות / שמלה / שלמה / מד / מעיל / מעטה** — clothing | beged, begadim, levush, malbush, lovesh, kesus, kesut, sut | draft | [clothing-beged-levush-kesus-simlah.md](entries/clothing-beged-levush-kesus-simlah.md)

*Beged* is the general word, **garment**, and covers the rest (Malbim). *Levush* is not a garment-type but what one dresses in, so **apparel**, and in practice the distinctive dress of a station (*levush malchus*). *Kesus* is a **covering**, draped rather than put on; *simlah* (or *salmah*) a **cloak**, a *kesus* that also confers honor. *Mad* is the **fitted garment**, *me'il* the **outer robe**, *maateh* any **wrap**. The trap: *beged* and *levush* both come out "clothing," and the difference between them carries two separate derashos.

**פגר / נבלה / חלל / פגרים** — corpse | peger, pegarim, peggar, neveilah, nevelah, nevela, challal, chalal | draft | [corpse-peger-neveilah-challal.md](entries/corpse-peger-neveilah-challal.md)

*Peger* is a human **corpse**, and by Rabbi Eliyahu HaBachur the rotting body of a rasha. *Neveilah* is a **carcass**, an animal that died without shechitah, and by the same authority it can name a human body too, but only one killed by an outside force. *Challal* is the body **emptied** of its soul, hence in Tanach the word for the slain. The trap is letting "corpse" cover all three: *neveilah* in a halachic passage is a technical term and must not be softened, and *peger* carries a note of contempt that "body" will lose.

**זוהמה / לכלוך / טינוף / אלח / גועל** — dirt | zuhamah, zuhama, zohamah, mezuham, lichluch, likhlukh, lichluchis, tinuf | draft | [dirt-zuhamah-lichluch-tinuf-goal.md](entries/dirt-zuhamah-lichluch-tinuf-goal.md)

*Go'al* is dirtying by something inherently disgusting (blood, excrement, forbidden food): **defilement, befouling**. *Tinuf* and *lichluch* are dirtying by something not disgusting in itself but unwanted where it landed (Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim): **soiling** and **grime**, with *lichluch* specifically wet (Mishnah *Mikvaos* 9:4). *Zuhamah* is the unwanted residue that clings and usually smells: **filth**. *Elach* is the spoiling of something homogeneous by an incompatible admixture: **tainted, sullied**. The trap is that all five reach for English "dirty"; the difference is whether the dirtying agent is disgusting in itself (*go'al*), merely misplaced (*tinuf*, *lichluch*), or a foreign element mixed in (*elach*).

**חלב / שומן / פדר / תרבא / חלב** — fat | cheilev, chelev, chelavim, chelvei hatamid, shuman, shumman, pader, peder | draft | [fat-cheilev-shuman-pader.md](entries/fat-cheilev-shuman-pader.md)

*Cheilev* is **fat**, and in halachic contexts specifically the **forbidden fat**: the hard visceral globs offered on the mizbeach and barred from eating. *Shuman* is post-biblical and names the **permitted fat**, the soft fat attached to the meat. *Pader* is the **fatty membrane** dividing the innards, or on another view the organ cluster of trachea, lungs, heart, and liver. *Tarba* is the Aramaic standing in for *cheilev*. The one thing not to get wrong: *cheilev* also means **the choicest part** of a non-animal thing, and in that borrowed sense has nothing to do with fat at all.

**פעם / רגל / פעמון / פעמותיו** — foot | paam, paamim, peamim, paamosav, paamotav, paamon, poem, regel | draft | [foot-paam-regel.md](entries/foot-paam-regel.md)

Both words mean **foot** and both can mean **time, instance**. *Regel* is the plain foot, the limb; *paam* is the foot as it strikes the ground, so its primary sense is **step** and, by extension, **corner** (the low corner of a thing) and **each occurrence**. *Shalosh regalim* and *shalosh peamim* both render "three times." The trap: *regel* in the sense of "time" occurs in Tanach only twice, in Bilaam's donkey (Bamidbar 22:28, 32) and in the three festivals (Shemos 23:14); everywhere else it is the limb, and a translator should not reach for "time" without one of those two contexts.

**מצח / פדחת / ציץ / צח** — forehead | meitzach, metzach, meizach, mitzcho, padachat, padachas, padachta, pedachat | draft | [forehead-meitzach-padachat.md](entries/forehead-meitzach-padachat.md)

*Meitzach* is the biblical word, some thirteen times in Tanach; *padachat* is the word of Chazal, absent from Tanach. From the Geonim onward they are treated as the same thing in two strata of the language (Rav Hai Gaon; Rabbeinu Chananel; *Sefer HaAruch*; and Rashi, who glosses *padachat* as *meitzach* every time), so render both **forehead**. The one thing not to get wrong is Shemos 28:38: since most authorities hold the *tzitz* sat above the forehead where the *tefillin* go, the Ritva and *Tosafos Yeshanim* either read the pasuk as "above his *meitzach*" or take biblical *meitzach* as **the top of the head**, not the brow. Do not render that pasuk "on his forehead" without knowing which reading you are committing to.

**שער / שערה / שערא / מזיא / בינתא / נימא** — hair | saar, sear, se'ar, saarah, searah, saara, sara, mazia | draft | [hair-saar-mazia-binta-nimah.md](entries/hair-saar-mazia-binta-nimah.md)

*Saar* (Aramaic *saara*) is the plain word for hair, whether one strand or the whole growth: **hair**. In Talmudic Aramaic the three others divide it up: *mazia* is the head of hair as a mass, **hair** or **a head of hair**; *binta* is one single strand, **a hair**; *nimah* is a hair that protrudes or is unwanted, and outside the hair sense it is a **string** or **thread**. The trap is that English "hair" flattens all four, and two of them stand side by side in the same Gemara, where the point is precisely that a *binta* came loose from the *mazia*.

**כליות / עצה / טוחות** — kidneys | kilayos, kilayot, kilyah, kulya, atzeh, aitzeh, tuchos, tuchot | draft | [kidneys-kilayos-atzeh-tuchos.md](entries/kidneys-kilayos-atzeh-tuchos.md)

*Kilayos* is the plain word for **kidneys**, and in a *pasuk* about Hashem searching a man it is his **innermost thoughts**. *Tuchos* is a poetic word for the same organ, so render it **kidneys** with Chazal, or **innards** with Ibn Ezra. *Atzeh* is a hapax and genuinely disputed: **kidneys** on the Gemara's reading, **the base of the spine** on Targum Onkelos's; say which you follow. Note that both *kilayos* and *tuchos* appear only in the plural.

**צרעת / נגע / צרוע / מצורע / צרעה** — leprosy | tzaraas, tzaraat, tzara'at, nega, nego, negaim, negoim, tzarua | draft | [leprosy-tzaraas-nega-metzora.md](entries/leprosy-tzaraas-nega-metzora.md)

*Tzaraas* is the **affliction itself**, the impurity that has taken hold; *nega* is the **spot**, the point of contact where it strikes, and where the Torah pairs them, *nega tzaraas*, it is naming two things and not one. *Tzarua* and *metzora* both mean **leper**, but *tzarua* is the man while the affliction is on him and *metzora* still fits him until the purification is complete (Perlmutter). Do not render *tzaraas* "leprosy" as a medical identification; Rav Hirsch holds that an affliction that is merely pathological is by definition not a *nega* at all.

**מסוה / מסכה / פרצוף / סותה / מסכת** — mask | masveh, masveh, masechah, masecha, masekhah, partzuf, partzufim, partzufa | draft | [mask-masveh-masechah-partzuf.md](entries/mask-masveh-masechah-partzuf.md)

*Masveh* is the Biblical **mask** or **veil**, and it appears only of the covering Moshe Rabbeinu put over his face. *Masechah* in Tanach is not a mask at all but a **molten image**, cast metal, as in *egel masechah*; the modern sense of "mask" is a later development. *Partzuf* is a **face**, a Greek loan-word, and it is the word the early poskim use for the Purim custom. The trap: reading the modern *masechah* back into a pasuk turns a golden calf into a disguise.

**אף / חוטם / נחירים / חור / אפים** — nose | af, ahf, apayim, appayim, anpin, chotem, chotam, chotmin | draft | [nose-af-chotem-nichirayim.md](entries/nose-af-chotem-nichirayim.md)

*Af* is the biblical word: **nose** in a minority of its appearances, **anger** or **even, also** in most, and in the dual *apayim* usually **face**. *Chotem* is the rabbinic word for the organ, and in the plural *chotmin* the **nostrils** proper. *Nichirayim* is a single biblical word for the two **nostrils** as openings (Iyov 41:12). The one thing not to get wrong: *af* is not primarily an anatomical word, and in the vast majority of pesukim it means anger, not nose. Check the context before letting a nose into the English, and never translate *apayim* as "nostrils" where the pasuk means the face.

**דבר / מגפה / נגף / צפרדע / דבורה** — plague | dever, mageifah, magefah, mageifos, mageifotai, negef, nogef, tzfardea | draft | [plague-dever-mageifah-negef.md](entries/plague-dever-mageifah-negef.md)

*Dever* is **pestilence**, a lethal contagious disease. *Mageifah* is the wider word for a **plague** or **blow**, from a root that means striking, and by Rashi's reading it does not by itself mean anything fatal. *Negef*, *mageifah*'s close relative, is the exception: Malbim reads it as a plague that does kill, and Rashi glosses it as *dever*. The one thing not to get wrong: *mageifah* is not a milder *dever* and not a graver one. It names the striking; *dever* names the disease.

**תער / מורה / מתער** — razor | taar, taer, morah, mora, mitaar, temurah, mumar | draft | [razor-taar-morah.md](entries/razor-taar-morah.md)

*Taar* is the plain word for a **razor**; the Torah uses it for the *nazir* (Bamidbar 6:5). *Morah* is the word the Neviim use in the same formula (Shoftim 13:5, 16:17; Shmuel I 1:11), and it is contested: Radak renders it **razor** throughout, while Targum and Rashi render it **scissors** by Shimshon and **the fear of men** by Shmuel. No source distinguishes the two words where both mean a razor, and no source in the column draws one. So render both "razor" unless the passage is Shmuel I 1:11, where the reading itself is the question.

**גמד / גומד / נמוך / גוץ / ננס / גדם / מך** — shortness of stature | gamad, gamadim, gomed, gemed, namuch, namoch, nemucha, gutz | draft | [short-gamad-namuch-gutz-nanas.md](entries/short-gamad-namuch-gutz-nanas.md)

*Gomed* is a **measure**, an *amah* or a cut-down *amah*, and *gamadim* in Yechezkel are read either as sounders of the sea's depth or as **dwarfs** an *amah* tall. *Namuch* is **low**, the plain Rabbinic word for short in height, of a person or a fence alike. *Gutz* is **stunted**, from an Aramaic sense of cutting off, as though the man's height had been cut short. *Nanas* is the Rabbinic term for a **dwarf**, and equally for short boards or low walls. The one thing not to get wrong: none of these is exclusively about people, so a short wall in a sugya is *namuch* or *nanas*, not a metaphor.

**שכם / כתף** — shoulder | shechem, shchem, sh'chem, katef, kasef, kesef, k'sefayim, kesefos | draft | [shoulder-shechem-katef.md](entries/shoulder-shechem-katef.md)

Both mean **shoulder**, and no single ruling settles them. The most useful division: *katef* is the shoulder proper, the whole joint; *shechem* is the shoulder where it meets the neck and runs into the upper back, so **upper back** or **shoulders** (plural) often serves it better. *Shechem* alone carries the borrowed senses of a parcel carried there and, from that, a **portion** or allotment. Do not render both with a bare "shoulder" in a pasuk that uses them together.

**שינה / תרדמה / תנומה / לינה / דורמיטא / ישן / שכיבה** — sleep | sheinah, sheina, sheintah, yashein, yashen, yashan, tardeimah, tardema | draft | [sleep-sheinah-tardeimah-tenumah.md](entries/sleep-sheinah-tardeimah-tenumah.md)

*Sheinah* is ordinary **sleep**, and doubles as the general term. *Tardeimah* is **deep sleep**, the sleep that overpowers a man rather than being chosen. *Tenumah* is **drowsiness** or **dozing**, the half-asleep state in which one still answers to his name. *Linah* is not sleep at all but **staying overnight**, and it applies equally to meat and to a corpse. Ibn Ezra's ranking, *tenumah* under *sheinah* under *tardeimah*, is the majority reading and the safe default; the Netziv reads the words as stages of one cycle, which inverts *tardeimah*.

**נצב / עמידה / עומד / התיצב / מצבה** — standing | nitzav, nitzavim, nitzavta, hisyatzvu, hityatzvu, vatetatzav, mutzav, matzeivah | draft | [standing-nitzav-amidah.md](entries/standing-nitzav-amidah.md)

*Amidah* is plain **standing**: upright and stationary, the posture opposite sitting, lying and walking, with no purpose implied. *Nitzav* is **taking up a position**, standing for a reason, in readiness or anticipation. The majority reading (Chazal, Pappenheim, Malbim, Mecklenburg) is this purpose distinction, not an intensity one. What a translator must not do is render both "stood" in a pasuk that switches from one to the other; every source in the field builds on exactly those switches.

**שן / מתלעות / מלתעות / ככא / ניבי / גלל / חיט** — teeth | shein, shen, shina, shinayim, mesalos, metallot, mitalot, maltaos | draft | [teeth-shein-mesalos-kaka.md](entries/teeth-shein-mesalos-kaka.md)

*Shein* is the general word for a **tooth**, and by extension **ivory** and the "teeth" of a tool. *Mesalos* and *malteos* (the same word with the lamed and tav switched) are the **back teeth**, the thick grinding teeth near the throat, or on a second reading the **fangs** of a wild animal; either way they are the specific term against the general one. *Kaka* is the Targum's Aramaic for *shein*, *nivi* its Aramaic for *mesalos*, and the split proves the Hebrew pair is not two names for one thing. *Gallal* is "tooth" only as the Gemara's name for a category of damage. *chit* is Mishnaic and disputed: the **gums** (Rashi) or the **teeth** themselves (Rabbeinu Gershom). Do not render *mesalos* simply "teeth"; the word is chosen against *shein* every time it appears.

**גרון / גרגרת / קנה / ושט / גרגרן** — throat | garon, garone, gargeres, gargeret, gargeros, gargerot, kaneh, kane | draft | [throat-garon-gargeres-kaneh.md](entries/throat-garon-gargeres-kaneh.md)

All three name the **windpipe**, but they belong to different strata: *garon* is the Biblical word, *gargeres* the Mishnaic, *kaneh* the Talmudic (Rabbi Yaakov Emden; Rabbi Tanchum HaYerushalmi). In Tanach *garon* and *gargeres* often mean the visible **throat** or **neck** rather than the pipe inside it; from the Mishnah on, the word is technical and its partner is *veshet*, the **food pipe**. The one thing not to get wrong: in any *shechitah* context *gargeres* or *kaneh* is the windpipe and *veshet* the esophagus, and rendering either loosely as "throat" destroys the halachah.

**עיף / יעף / יגע** — tired | ayef, ayeif, yaef, yaeif, yaaf, yagea, yagia, yaga | draft | [tired-ayef-yaef-yagea.md](entries/tired-ayef-yaef-yagea.md)

*Ayef* is **tired**, the common word and the one Modern Hebrew kept. *Yaef* is the same three letters in a different order and reads **exhausted**, one with nothing left; that is the whole point of *ha'nosen la'yaef koach* in the morning berachos. *Yagea* is **worn out from toil**, weariness traced to exertion rather than to a man's own capacity. The trap is that English "tired" will swallow all three; where a passage turns on the degree, hold *ayef* and *yaef* apart.

**יקיצה / עורה / ויקץ / מקיץ / מעורר / ער / נעור** — waking | yekitzah, yekitza, hakitzah, vayikatz, mekitz, hekitz, oorah, urah | draft | [waking-yekitzah-oorah.md](entries/waking-yekitzah-oorah.md)

*Yekitzah* is **waking up**, one clean break from sleep to waking, and it is the Torah's word for coming out of a dream or a stupor. *Oorah* is **rousing** or **stirring**, a gradual coming-to that can begin while a man is still asleep. So *yekitzah* is intransitive and complete; *oorah* is the word for waking someone else, and for the half-state in between. The trap is that English "awake" and "awaken" cover both; where a text pairs them (*HaMe'orer yesheinim v'haMeikitz nirdamim*), the two must be rendered by two different English verbs.

**כנף / אבר / אגף / גף / גדפא / נוצה** — wing | kanaf, kenaf, kanfei, ever, eiver, evrah, avir, abir | draft | [wing-kanaf-ever-agaf.md](entries/wing-kanaf-ever-agaf.md)

*Kanaf* is the **wing**, and specifically its outer reach, which is why it also means the **edge** or **corner** of a garment or a land and why it is the wing that shelters. *Ever* is best rendered **pinion**, the outer wing with its flight feathers, and in Chazal's Hebrew it widens to any **limb**. *Agaf* is the wing where it joins the body, and by extension a **flank**; it appears only in Yechezkel. Devarim 32:11 uses *kanaf* and *ever* in one pasuk, so they cannot both be "wings" there.

**פצע / חבורה / חובל / חברבורות** — wound | petza, peza, potzeh, potzea, chaburah, chabura, chavurah, chovel | draft | [wound-petza-chaburah-chovel.md](entries/wound-petza-chaburah-chovel.md)

*Petza* is an **open wound**, a **laceration** that breaks the skin and bleeds; *chaburah* is a **bruise**, blood pooled under skin that stayed whole (Rashi to Shemos 21:25, and the Gra). *Chovel* is the Rabbinic verb, **to injure**. The pair stands in one pasuk four times (Bereishis 4:23; Shemos 21:25; Yeshayahu 1:6; Mishlei 20:30), so rendering both "wound" erases the distinction the Torah drew. Rav Saadia Gaon and the Rashbam divide the pair on other lines entirely.


### cognition

**שכח / שכחה / נשה / תשי / נשני / כחש / שכיח** — forgetting | shachach, shakach, shichachah, shichecha, tishkach, nashah, nasha, nashani | draft | [forgetting-shachach-nashah.md](entries/forgetting-shachach-nashah.md)

*Shachach* is the ordinary word, over a hundred times in Tanach and the word Devarim uses for every warning not to forget: **forget**. *Nashah* is rare and, on the majority reading, heavier: **forget entirely, put out of mind for good**. Malbim: what is *shachach* can still be recalled, what is *nashah* cannot. Rav Hirsch adds the moral note, that *nashah* attaches to a man who held the thing lightly, so the forgetting is his own fault. A minority (Rabbi Eliyahu Katz) denies any difference at all.

**שמע / האזין / אוזן** — hearing | shema, shma, tishma, shamoa, shomea, haazinu, haazin, haazanah | draft | [hearing-shema-haazinu.md](entries/hearing-shema-haazinu.md)

*Haazinu* is from *ozen*, an ear: **give ear**, the plain physical act of listening. *Shema* is **hear** in the fuller sense, taking in what is said and understanding it (Malbim), or hearkening in answer to a call (Rokeach). On distance, the Sifrei has *haazinu* for what is near and *shema* for what is far; Chizkuni, Abarbanel and Rabbi Yitzchak Arama hold the reverse. The trap: English "hear" and "listen" are used loosely, but Devarim 32:1 and Yeshayahu 1:2 assign the two Hebrew verbs to heaven and earth in opposite order, and any translation that does not keep them apart destroys the question the meforshim are answering.

**זכור / זכר / זכרון / פקד / פקידה / פקדון / שמור** — remember | zachor, zachar, zecher, zeicher, zechirah, zikaron, zichronos, zichronot | draft | [remember-zachor-pakad.md](entries/remember-zachor-pakad.md)

*Zachor* is remembering in the mind: **remember, call to mind**. *Pakad* is remembering that issues in action: **attend to, take note of** (and in the right context "count," "appoint," "punish," "deposit"). Malbim: *zechirah* is in thought, *pekidah* is in deed. The trap is rendering both as "remembered" and flattening a pasuk that turns on the difference; when Hashem *pakad* someone, something happened to them.

**ראה / הביט / השקיף / צופה / שור / מציץ / משגיח** — seeing | raah, ra'ah, roeh, hibit, hibbit, habatah, hashkafah, mashkif | draft | [seeing-raah-hibit-tzofeh.md](entries/seeing-raah-hibit-tzofeh.md)

*Ra'ah* is plain **seeing**, the eye doing its work, including what a man happens upon without meaning to. *Hibit* is the deliberate **look**, and in the Midrash the look of one positioned above what he sees. *Mashkif* is **looking out** or **down** from a height, one who sees without being seen; in the Chumash Rashi takes it as boding ill. *Tzofeh* is **gazing** at what is not present to the eye, which is why a navi is a *tzofeh* and why waiting for what has not yet come is *mitzapeh*. *Shur* is seeing from far off, *metzitz* is **peeking** through a narrow opening, and *mashgiach* is watching that acts on what it sees. The trap is English "look," which will absorb *hibit*, *mashkif*, and *metzitz* at once.

**תעה / תועה / תועים / טעה / טעות / מטעה / טעותא** — straying and error | taah, ta'ah, toeh, to'eh, toim, toeim, toah, taut | draft | [straying-toeh-taut.md](entries/straying-toeh-taut.md)

The two are pronounced alike and spelled differently, and the working assumption of the commentators is that **תעה (tav) is "stray, be lost"** and **טעה (tet) is "err, make a mistake"**; in Rabbinic Hebrew *ta'us* always means a mistake. Radak holds outright that they are one word, the tav being the orthography of Tanach and the tet that of Chazal, so do not build an argument on the spelling alone. Where a source does distinguish them, follow Rabbi Shlomo Aharon Wertheimer: both men are lost on the road, but the *to'eh* with a tav knows he is lost, while the *to'eh* with a tet is confident he is on the right road and is not.

**הוראה / למוד / אלף / הדרכה / תורה / תלפיות / אלוף** — teaching | horaah, hora'ah, lhorot, lehorot, yoreh, moreh, limmud, limud | draft | [teaching-horaah-limmud-alef.md](entries/teaching-horaah-limmud-alef.md)

*Hora'ah* is **instruction** given once and expected to stick; in Mishnaic Hebrew it narrows to **halachic ruling**. *Limmud* is **teaching by drilling**, repeating until the lesson is engraved and second nature. *Alef* is teaching that happens **incidentally**, by example or by inference, without anyone setting out to teach. *Hadrachah* is not teaching at all but **guidance** in applying what one already knows. The one thing not to get wrong: *hora'ah* and *limmud* are stages, not synonyms, and Shadal reads a whole tragedy in Tanach off the difference.

**מחשבה / הרהור / רעיון** — thought | machshavah, machshava, machashavah, machshavos, machshavot, hirhur, hirhurim, harhor | draft | [thought-machshavah-hirhur-raayon.md](entries/thought-machshavah-hirhur-raayon.md)

*Machshavah* is the deliberate, worked-out thought, kin to *cheshbon*: **thought**. *Raayon* is the spark that surfaces unbidden and may or may not be developed: **idea**, **notion** (Rabbi Shlomo Aharon Wertheimer; Rashi and Ramban, though, treat it as a plain synonym of *machshavah*). *Hirhur* is a thought already shaped toward words, or a thought of the heart rather than the brain (Vilna Gaon): **musing**, or in a mussar setting **stirring** (of the heart). The one thing not to get wrong: *hirhur* is not a vaguer, lesser "thought"; on the Gaon's first reading it is the *last* stage, thought at the threshold of speech, and on his second it is the heart's thought, which is why it is the word for the stirrings a person is answerable for.

**חכמה / בינה / תבונה / דעת / חכם / נבון** — wisdom | chochmah, chochma, chachmah, chokhmah, binah, bina, tevunah, tevuna | draft | [wisdom-chochmah-binah-daat-tevunah.md](entries/wisdom-chochmah-binah-daat-tevunah.md)

*Chochmah* is received knowledge, what one takes in from a teacher or from any source: **wisdom**. *Binah* (with *tevunah*) is understanding one thing from within another, building on what one already holds: **understanding** (a *navon* is **discerning**). *Daat* is the joining of the two, knowledge made certain and internalised: **knowledge**. The one thing not to get wrong: *chochmah* is the *lowest* of the three, not the crown (Shemos Rabbah 41:3; Rashi to Shabbos 31a), so keep the Betzalel order, "wisdom, understanding, and knowledge" (Shemos 31:3), and never let "wisdom" swallow *daat*.


### emotion

**כעס / חמה / חרון / חרון אף / רוגז / עברה / זעם / זעף / קצף / אף / אנף / עשן / פנים / נאץ / עצבות / רעם / שצף** — anger | kaas, ka'as, kaas, cheimah, cheima, chemah, chema, charon | draft | [anger-cheimah-ketzef-charon-rogez.md](entries/anger-cheimah-ketzef-charon-rogez.md)

*Kaas* is the plain, generic word: **anger**. *Cheimah* is heated anger held in the heart: **wrath**. *Charon* is anger past heat to burning: **burning anger**; the idiom *charon af*, **fierce anger**. *Evrah* overflows its object onto others: **fury**. *Zaam* is angry contempt that breaks into cursing: **indignation**. *Ketzef* is anger at the boil, outward and visible: **rage**. *Rogez* is the body shaken out of its calm: **agitation**. *Af* is anger on the face: **anger**. Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim ranks them by intensity (kaas, zaam, cheimah, charon, charon af, ketzef, evrah), so a pasuk that stacks two never gets one English word twice.

**תאוה / חמדה / חפץ** — desire | taavah, ta'avah, taava, tayvah, tayvuh, taivah, taavos, hitavu | draft | [desire-taavah-chemdah.md](entries/desire-taavah-chemdah.md)

*Taavah* is **craving**, and in a mussar context **lust**: the desire whose fulfillment is direct bodily pleasure, born of the man's own appetite and imagination, and in Scripture almost always the desire for intimacy (Vilna Gaon; Malbim). *Chemdah* is **coveting**: desire drawn by the object's own worth and roused by seeing it, typically for money, a house, fine clothes (Vilna Gaon; Rabbi Pappenheim and Rabbi Mecklenburg; Malbim). *Chefetz* sits between them, the Vilna Gaon's word for desire for food; render "desire" and see the *ratzon* entry. Chazal pair them: *chemdah* with theft, *taavah* with illicit relations (Chagigah 11b). Do not let "desire" flatten the two; the contrast is inside (*taavah*) against outside (*chemdah*).

**אימה / פחד / מורא / יראה / דאגה** — fear | eimah, eima, aimah, aima, pachad, pachas, morah, mora | draft | [fear-eimah-pachad-yirah-daagah.md](entries/fear-eimah-pachad-yirah-daagah.md)

*Eimah* is the felt emotion of being afraid: **dread, terror**. *Pachad* is the sudden, short-lived start of fear, its outward reaction: **fright, fear**. *Yirah* and its noun *morah* are the sustained fear that has built up over time: **fear** (the general word). *Daagah* is fear of a loss or of a coming evil: **worry, anxiety**. The one thing not to get wrong: *pachad* is sudden and brief, *morah* is continual and therefore the more intense (Rashi, Rabbi Shlomo Aharon Wertheimer); do not render *pachad* as the deeper fear.

**שמחה / ששון / גילה / גיל / רינה / רנה / דיצה / חדוה** — joy | simchah, simcha, sasson, sason, sosson, gilah, gila, gil | draft | [joy-simchah-sasson-gilah.md](entries/joy-simchah-sasson-gilah.md)

*Simchah* is the inner, continual gladness: **joy, gladness** (the general word). *Sasson* is joy shown outwardly, in dress, music, celebration: **rejoicing** (Malbim); for the Vilna Gaon it is the joy attained at the end, where *simchah* begins the pursuit. *Gilah* is a burst of joy that does not last (Malbim), or the joy of recalling past joys (Gra): **exultation, a burst of joy**. *Rinah* is joy that breaks into song of Hashem's praise: **joyous song**. *Ditzah* and *chedvah* are the Aramaic words for joy: **mirth, delight**. The one thing not to get wrong: both ששון ושמחה and שמחה וששון are scriptural and Chazal darshen the order (Sukkah 48b), so keep the Hebrew order and give each word its own English; never a single "joy."

**צחוק / שחוק / יצחק / ישחק / מצחק** — laughter | tzchok, tzechok, tzchoq, metzachek, vayitzchak, yitzchak, schok, sechok | draft | [laughter-tzchok-schok.md](entries/laughter-tzchok-schok.md)

*Tzchok* and *s'chok* are the same word: **laughter**, with sin and tzadi interchanging, and the lexicographers find no difference of meaning between them. Both run the full range from laughing and playing to mocking and deriding, so context, not the root, decides whether to render "laugh," "play" or "mock."
