The whole discussion of *Yitzchak* against *Yischak* is therefore not lexical but homiletical and kabbalistic; do not translate the two spellings as if they meant different things.

**אהבה / אהוב / אוהב / רחים / רחימו / חיבה / חביב / חובב / עגב / עגבים** — love | ahavah, ahava, ahuv, ahuvah, ohev, ahavim, rechim, r'chim | draft | [love-ahavah-chibah.md](entries/love-ahavah-chibah.md)

*Ahavah* is the general word and the widest: **love**; in its true sense the love that gives itself to the beloved (Rav Hirsch, Rabbi Eliyahu Lopian) and embraces the whole person, faults included (Rabbi Yosef Zechariah Stern). *Chibah* is love held close and inward, love of what is dear in someone: **fondness, affection**; *chaviv*, **dear**. *Rechim* is the Targum's Aramaic for *ahavah* (in Hebrew the root is mercy, save Tehillim 18:2): **love**. *Agav* is the counterfeit: **lust**, never love. The one thing not to get wrong: *chibah* is not a synonym to flatten into "love"; beside *ahavah* it is the narrower, inner, selective word.

**רחום / רחמים / רחמנות / חנון / חן / חנינה / חוס / חס / חמלה** — mercy | rachum, rachamim, rachamin, rachmanus, rachmanut, chanun, chanan, chein | draft | [mercy-rachum-chanun-chus-chemlah.md](entries/mercy-rachum-chanun-chus-chemlah.md)

*Rachum* (*rachamim*) is **mercy**, **compassion**: the savior is moved by the sufferer's plight and acts. *Chanun* (*chein*) is **grace**, **favor**: the sufferer finds favor and is given a free gift he need not deserve (Rashi: *matnas chinam*). *Chus* is **sparing**, refusing to let something be lost because one values it; *chemlah* is **pity** that will not see a thing ruined for its own worth, and it reaches the one who cannot ask (Malbim, Wertheimer). The one thing not to get wrong: *rachum* looks at the giver's heart, *chanun* at the receiver's standing; render the Thirteen Attributes "merciful and gracious," never "merciful and merciful."

**שלום / שלוה / שקט / השקט / שאנן / שלאנן / שלו / שלם** — peace | shalom, sholom, shalem, shaleim, shalvah, shalva, shalev, shaleiv | draft | [peace-shalom-sheket-shalvah.md](entries/peace-shalom-sheket-shalvah.md)

*Shalom* is the general word: **peace**, the absence of war and, at root, wholeness with nothing lacking (Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim). *Shalvah* is peace felt within, or a pocket of peace inside a larger discord: **tranquility, ease** (Malbim, Pappenheim), with a shadow of complacency (Bedersi, Rav Hirsch). *Sheket* is the cessation of motion and outward disturbance: **quiet, stillness, calm**; whether it ranks above *shalom* (Bedersi) or below it as a mere ceasefire (Rabbi Shlomo Aharon Wertheimer) is disputed, and Wertheimer's reading is the more useful for prose. *Shaanan* is the fullest rest, free even of the busyness of prosperity: **serenity** (Bedersi, Pappenheim). The one thing not to get wrong: *shalom* is about parties in harmony, *shalvah* and *sheket* about a person or a scene at rest; do not render all three "peace."

**עדן / עדנה / מעדנים / עדין / עונג / ענג / ענוג / פינוק / תפנוקים** — pleasure | eden, edna, ednah, maadanim, ma'adanim, maadanei melech, adin, adinah | draft | [pleasure-oneg-eden-pinuk.md](entries/pleasure-oneg-eden-pinuk.md)

*Eden* (with *ednah*, *maadanim*) is pleasure of the body, above all of taste and touch: **sensual pleasure, delight of the senses**; *maadanim*, **delicacies**. *Oneg* is pleasure of the soul, mind and heart, even when it arrives by way of the body: **delight, enjoyment**; *oneg Shabbos* is therefore delight in Shabbos taken through good food, never "physical pleasure." *Pinuk* is for Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim not pleasure at all but playfulness and flattery, though the Targumim use it for both *oneg* and *eden*: **pampering, indulgence**. The one thing not to get wrong: the body/soul split between *eden* and *oneg* (Pappenheim) is the whole distinction; do not let "pleasure" cover both.

**בושה / כלימה / חרפה / חפרה** — shame | bushah, busha, boshet, klimah, kelimah, klima, cherpah, cherpa | reviewed | [shame-bushah-klimah-cherpah.md](entries/shame-bushah-klimah-cherpah.md)

*Bushah* is the general, milder word: **shame**. *Klimah* is the graver, inherently disgraceful kind: **humiliation** (or **disgrace**). *Cherpah* is shame inflicted by another's insult or reproach: **disgrace, reproach**; its metathesised twin *chafrah* is the mortified wish to dig a hole and hide: **mortification**. When two appear together the Torah writes the milder first (Radak), so a doubled pair keeps the ascending order in English: "shame and humiliation," never the reverse.

**חיל / חלחל / דחיל / זחל / אזחיל / זחלתי** — trembling | chil, chal, chalu, chalchalah, vatischalchal, dachil, dechilah, dachla | draft | [trembling-chil-dachil-zachal.md](entries/trembling-chil-dachil-zachal.md)

*Chil* is the Hebrew word and it is bodily: **trembling**, the shaking that fear produces, the same word Tanach uses for a woman's labor pangs. *Dachil* is the Aramaic word and it is general: **fearful, dread**, and Targum uses it to render *yirah*, *nora*, and *pachad* alike. *Zachal* carries two senses in Tanach, **crawling** and **fear**, and the fear sense is very likely the Aramaic *dachil* with a dalet turned to zayin (*Metzudas Tzion*). The trap is rendering all three "fear," which is exactly what the piyut that occasioned this discussion refuses to do.

**רעדה / זיע / זעוה / רתת / רטט / רעש / רגז / געש / חרד** — trembling | raadah, raada, raad, vatirad, marid, ziyah, ziya, zia | draft | [trembling-raadah-retet-raash.md](entries/trembling-raadah-retet-raash.md)

*Ra'adah* is **trembling**, the deep shake of fear. *Ziyah* is a **shudder**, a quiver felt rather than seen, and the Targum's word for both *vayecherad* and *ra'ash*. *Retet* is the **lighter** tremble (Wertheimer). *Ra'ash* is **quaking** or **tumult**, vigorous movement of any kind. *Rogez* is **agitation**, physical in Hebrew and angry in Aramaic. Yoma 4b strings four together and Shmuel II 22:8 puts three in one pasuk, so a translator needs distinct English words, not one repeated.

**בטחון / בטח / מחסה / תקוה / תוחלת / חכוי / סמיכה / משען / סעד / שבר / כסל** — trust | bitachon, bitochon, betach, boteach, machseh, machaseh, chasah, lachasos | draft | [trust-bitachon-machseh-tikvah.md](entries/trust-bitachon-machseh-tikvah.md)

*Bitachon* is **trust**, the inner confidence that the future will be good (Malbim: a state of the heart, silent about action). *Machseh* is **refuge**, trust lived out (Malbim), or trust in Hashem beyond what He promised (Vilna Gaon). *Tikvah* is **hope**, *tocheles* **expectant waiting** with some doubt, *chikui* **waiting** in full certainty (Wertheimer's ascending scale). *Semichah* is **leaning** with one's whole weight, *mishan* **leaning for support** out of need, *saad* **support** given (Malbim, Pappenheim). *Sever* is **hopeful looking-to**; *kesel* **assurance** that nothing bad will happen. Chovos HaLevavos counts these as ten levels of one thing; do not collapse them all into "trust."

**רצון / חפץ / צבי** — will | ratzon, ratson, razon, rotzeh, rotze, retzono, retzoncha, retzon Hashem | draft | [will-ratzon-chefetz.md](entries/will-ratzon-chefetz.md)

*Ratzon* is **will**: the wanting a man chooses with his mind, and so as a rule aimed at something good (Malbim). *Chefetz* is **desire**: the stronger, more concrete pull that comes unchosen and can fasten on what is bad for him; the majority (Rabbi Pappenheim, Rabbi Tzvi Yehuda Kook, Rabbi Margolios, Malbim) rank it the more intense of the two. *Tzvi* in the sense of wanting is **yearning**, a wanting that persists (Rabbi Pappenheim). Never swap them: *ratzon* is not "desire" and *chefetz* is not "will". Minority: Rabbi Edel reads the pair as means (*ratzon*) against final goal (*chefetz*); keep it in mind when a passage speaks of *retzon Hashem*.


### nature

**לוז / שקד / שקדים / משקדים** — almond | luz, luzin, shaked, shakeid, shekeidim, shkedim, meshukadim, shoked | draft | [almond-luz-shaked.md](entries/almond-luz-shaked.md)

*Shaked* is the Hebrew word for the almond, and its root ש-ק-ד means watchful and diligent, which is the whole point of the *nevuah* in Yirmiyahu 1:11 to 12: render it **almond**, and keep the wordplay with **vigilant** visible. *Luz* is the Aramaic (and Arabic) word for the same thing according to Rabbi Saadia Gaon as Radak and Ibn Ezra cite him, so **almond** again, though a substantial set of *Rishonim* read *luz* as **hazel** and Targum Onkelos never once renders *shaked* as *luz*. The trap is deciding either word's English without checking which *Rishon* the passage is following.

**בהמה / בהמות / חיה / חיות** — animal | behemah, beheimah, behema, behemot, behemos, beheimos, chayah, chaya | draft | [animal-behemah-chayah.md](entries/animal-behemah-chayah.md)

*Behemah* is **a domesticated animal**, one that lives among people and serves them; *chayah* is **a wild animal**, one that lives in open country (Ibn Ezra, Radak, and this is the accepted reading). Ramban draws the line differently, by diet: *behemos* eat only plants, *chayos* eat flesh. The one thing not to get wrong is that these are halachic categories before they are descriptions: *cheilev* is forbidden only in a *behemah*, and *kisui hadam* applies only to a *chayah*. Where a passage is halachic, do not flatten both to "animal."

**שואג / נוהם / יהגה / נוער / שוקק / נוהק** — animal sounds | shoeg, shoeig, shaag, nohem, noheim, naham, yehegeh, yehgeh | draft | [animal-sounds-shoeg-nohem-noer.md](entries/animal-sounds-shoeg-nohem-noer.md)

Biblical Hebrew assigns each animal its own verb, exactly as English does not let a cow bark. A lion has four: *shoeg* is **roars** (hunger, dominance), *nohem* is **growls** (satisfaction turned wild), *yehegeh* is **moans** (steady and sustained, also said of a dove), and *noer* is a sudden **roar** let out by a cub. A bear is *shokek*, a wild donkey *nohek* (**brays**), a cow *goeh* (**bellows**), a horse *tzohel* (**neighs**), a bird *mitzaftzef* (**chirps**), a dog *novayach* (**barks**). Do not render all four lion verbs "roar" in a passage that lists them; the point of the list is that they differ.

**פנג / צרי / נטף / קטף / בושם / בשמים / אפרסמון / בלסם / אפופלסמון** — balsam | panag, pannag, tzari, tzori, tzri, nataf, notef, ketaf | draft | [balsam-panag-tzari-afarsemon.md](entries/balsam-panag-tzari-afarsemon.md)

*Tzari* is the balsam resin of Tanach and of the *ketores*: render it **balm** or **balsam**, and keep it distinct from the incense's own biblical name for the same ingredient, *nataf*, literally **the dripping one**. *Bosem*/*besamim* is the wide word, **fragrance, spices**, never balsam specifically in Tanach. *Afarsemon* is the Rabbinic word for **balsam oil**. *Panag* is a *hapax legomenon* at Yechezkel 27:17; *Yossiphon* and Rashi read it as balsam, but Radak objects that Yechezkel lists *panag* and *tzari* separately, so leave it untranslated or gloss it. The trap: "balsam" will swallow *tzari*, *panag*, *bosem* and *afarsemon* at once, flattening a whole disagreement.

**בעיר / בהמה / חיה / בער / בור** — beast and boor | beir, be'ir, beira, behemah, beheimah, behema, chayah, chaya | draft | [beast-beir-behemah-baar-bur.md](entries/beast-beir-behemah-baar-bur.md)

*Behemah* is the ordinary word for a **domesticated animal**; *chayah* a **wild animal**; *be'ir* a rare word for a **beast**, and for Rav Hirsch the most subdued of the three, doing nothing but what instinct drives it to. The same root gives *ba'ar*, the **brute** or **boor**, and the Mishnaic *bur*, the **uncultured man**. When a pasuk pairs *ba'ar* with *behemah* (Tehillim 73:22) the pairing is the point; keep the two English words distinct.

**עוף / ציפור / כנף / צפרא** — bird | of, ohf, oaf, tzipor, tzippor, tsipor, kanaf, canaf | draft | [bird-of-tzipor-kanaf.md](entries/bird-of-tzipor-kanaf.md)

*Of* is the broad word, **fowl** or anything that flies. *Tzipor* is the narrower **bird**, and what narrows it is disputed: every bird as against insects (Radak), only a kosher bird (Sifri and Rambam), or the small chirping songbird that greets the morning (Ramban). *Kanaf* is literally **winged creature**, the wing itself doing the naming, and it reaches to grasshoppers and other winged insects. The trap is Bereishis 7:14, where all three stand in one pasuk: an English rendering that writes "bird" three times destroys the very inclusion Rashi reads there.

**ספיר / תכלת / כחול** — blue | sapir, sappir, sampiryon, sanpiryon, techeilet, techeiles, techelet, kachol | draft | [blue-sapir-techeiles-kachol.md](entries/blue-sapir-techeiles-kachol.md)

The Torah has no word for the color blue. *Sapir* is a **stone**, *techeiles* a **dyed wool**, and *kachol* comes from the name of a cosmetic powder. Render *sapir* "sapphire," leave *techeiles* transliterated with a gloss, and be wary of "blue" for *kachol*, whose range runs to dark and black. The trap is writing "blue" three times and giving an English reader a color vocabulary the Hebrew does not have.

**גמל / נאקה / בכר / בכרה / כר / כרכרות** — camel | gamal, gamalim, gemalim, nakah, naakah, becher, bechor, bichrah | draft | [camel-gamal-nakah-becher-kar.md](entries/camel-gamal-nakah-becher-kar.md)

These are not synonyms but stages and kinds. *Becher* (masculine) and *bichrah* (feminine) are the **young camel**, newly born and not yet able to carry loads; grown, it becomes *gamal* for a male and *nakah* for a female (Rabbi Nosson of Rome, *Sefer HeAruch*). *Gamal* is the default word and covers females too when no distinction is being drawn; Tanach marks a she-camel by the phrase *gemalim meinikos*, nursing camels (Bereishis 32:16). *Kar* and *kirkaros* are contested, and rendering either simply "camel" adopts one view against others. The trap: English "camel" absorbs all four and erases the age and gender the Hebrew is carrying.

**שור / פר / פרה / עגל / עגלה / אלף / בקר / בוקר** — cattle | shor, tor, par, parah, para, egel, eglah, egla | draft | [cattle-shor-par-egel-bakar.md](entries/cattle-shor-par-egel-bakar.md)

*Shor* is the **ox**, and it bears that name from the day it is born. *Par* is the **bull**, *parah* the **cow** or **heifer**, and the name attaches only once the animal is grown. *Egel* and *eglah* are the male and female **calf**. *Aleph* is the strong working **ox** of Tanach's poetry, eight appearances. *Bakar* is the collective, **cattle**, and its *boker* is the **herdsman**. The trap: English "ox" and "cow" will take all five, and *shor* is the word that most needs guarding, since it is not a synonym for *par* at any age.

**גבינה / חריץ / שפות / גבן / גב / גבנונים** — cheese | gvinah, gevinah, gvina, charitz, charitzei hechalav, shfot, shefot, shfot bakar | draft | [cheese-gvinah-charitz-shfot.md](entries/cheese-gvinah-charitz-shfot.md)

*Gvinah* is the ordinary word and the one Chazal use: **cheese**. *Charitz* (*charitzei hechalav*, Shmuel I 17:18) and *shfot* (*shfot bakar*, Shmuel II 17:29) each occur once, and Targum Yonasan renders both as *guvnin d'chalva*, cheese; but each is disputed, *charitz* possibly meaning unweaned kids or milk still in the udder, *shfot* possibly the udders themselves or butter. Render both as "cheese" with a footnote, since the halachic weight of Bechoros 6b rests on which reading you take.

**אפרוח / גוזל / פרגית / ניפול** — chick | efroach, efroah, efroch, gozal, gozel, gozla, pargit, pargis | draft | [chick-efroach-gozal-pargit-nipol.md](entries/chick-efroach-gozal-pargit-nipol.md)

*Efroach* is the Torah's word for a **hatchling**, the bird that has just "flowered" out of its egg. *Gozal* is a **fledgling**, primarily a young dove but extended to any young bird; where the two are contrasted (Rabbi Yaakov Kamenetsky, Rabbi Shmuel Ashkenazi) the *efroach* is the younger, still tied to its mother, and the *gozal* the older, already able to fly. *Pargit* is a Rabbinic word for a **young chicken**. *Nipol* is a **fallen nestling**, defined by the halachic case, not the species. The trap: English "chick" or "fledgling" will absorb *efroach* and *gozal* both, and the whole point of a passage may be which stage is meant.

**הדר / אתרוג / אתרונגא / טרונגא / תפוח** — citron | hadar, esrog, etrog, esrogim, etrogim, etroga, esroga, etronga | draft | [citron-hadar-etrog-tapuach.md](entries/citron-hadar-etrog-tapuach.md)

*Hadar* means **beautiful** or **splendid**; nothing in the word says citron. The Torah's *pri eitz hadar* (Vayikra 23:40) is the esrog by Mesorah, and the Gemara's derivations are asmachta (Ibn Ezra, Rambam). *Esrog* is the **citron**, the Mishnaic and Targumic name. *Tapuach* is an **apple**, and only in Shir HaShirim 2:3 does Rabbeinu Tam read it as a citron. Do not render *hadar* "citron" in the pasuk; translate the word and let the halachah supply the rest.

**ענן / עב / אד / נשיא / חזיז / נבל / כף / נוד** — clouds | anan, ananim, av, avim, eid, ed, eidim, edim | draft | [clouds-anan-av-eid-nasi-chaziz.md](entries/clouds-anan-av-eid-nasi-chaziz.md)

*Anan* is the plain, general **cloud** (close to a hundred times in Tanach). *Av* is the **thick cloud** that darkens the sky. *Eid* is the **rising vapor** or mist that clouds are formed from. *Nasi* is the **rain cloud** that carries and lifts water. *Chaziz* is the **thundercloud** that comes with lightning. Chazal count these five as the words for cloud; the trap is that four of them are not interchangeable descriptions but different states of cloud, so English "cloud" for all five flattens a Midrash that is counting five names on purpose.

**קור / קר / צנה / צנים / צננים / צונן / מצונן** — cold | kor, kar, karim, mayim karim, tzinah, tzina, tzinim, tzninim | draft | [cold-kor-tzinah.md](entries/cold-kor-tzinah.md)

*Tzinah* is ordinary coldness, the kind a body is built to withstand: **cold, chill**. *Kor* is the severe kind, the cold that hurts: **bitter cold, freezing** (Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim). Rabbi Yehoshua Steinberg draws the line differently: *kor* is the drop in temperature, *tzinah* is a cold that surrounds and envelops a person like snow. Both words are rare in Tanach, so the register matters more than the frequency suggests: do not render *kor* with a mild English word.

**צבע / גוון / עין / מראה** — color | tzeva, tzeva', tzova, tzovea, tzovei'a, tzivonim, tzavua, gavan | draft | [color-tzeva-gavan-ein-mareh.md](entries/color-tzeva-gavan-ein-mareh.md)

*Tzeva* is **color** in the sense of dye: the colorant, and the color a thing has been given (Shadal: an artificial color, from *tzovea*, dyeing). *Ein*, construct of *ayin*, is a thing's own single **color**, and is Rambam's usual word for it. *Mareh* is the **appearance**, the whole sight a thing presents, which may include several colors (Malbim). *Gavan* is Aramaic, **color** or **shade**, and belongs to Talmudic and Targumic language, not to Tanach. The trap: English "color" flattens all four, and with them the difference between what a thing looks like and what has been done to it.

**ים / מערב / אחרון / מזרח / קדם / פנים / צפון / שמאל / אסתן / דרום / נגב / תימן / ימין** — compass directions | yam, maarav, ma'arav, meurav, acharon, mizrach, kedem, kodem | draft | [directions-tzafon-darom-mizrach-maarav.md](entries/directions-tzafon-darom-mizrach-maarav.md)

Each direction has three Hebrew names, and each name says *why*: **east** is *mizrach* (where the sun shines out), *kedem* (the sun's first, forward position), or *panim* (facewards); **west** is *yam* (seaward, toward the Mediterranean), *maarav* (where shadows blur), or *acharon* (the sun's last place); **north** is *tzafon* (hidden from the sun), *smol* (left), or the Aramaic *estan*; **south** is *darom* (the sun's daytime path), *negev* (the dry country), or *teiman*/*yamin* (right). Render all of them by the compass point in the body, and only unpack the sense where the passage or a derashah leans on it. Bereishis 28:14 pairs *yam*, *kedem*, *tzafon*, *negev*; Devarim 3:27 pairs *yam*, *tzafon*, *mizrach*, *teiman*.

**גד / כוסבר / כוסברתא / גידא / גודגדניות / מגד / מגדים / מגדנות** — coriander | gad, gada, gida, kusbar, kusbara, kusbarta, kusbarat, gudgedaniyot | draft | [coriander-gad-kusbarta.md](entries/coriander-gad-kusbarta.md)

*Gad*, the seed the man resembled (Shemos 16:31, Bamidbar 11:7), is most widely identified as **coriander**: so Targum Yonasan, Menachem Ibn Saruk, Radak, and Rashi, who render it with the Aramaic *kusbarta* and the Old French *coliander*. Rav Hai Gaon, quoted by Rabbi Yehudah Ibn Balaam, says **mustard seed** instead, and Ibn Ezra records both and leaves it open. Onkelos declines to translate at all. The one thing not to get wrong: "white *gad* seed" does not mean the seed is white. Rashi, the Rashbam, and Radak all say the man was white and only its shape was like a *gad* seed, and that *gad* seeds are not white.

**חושך / אפילה / אפלה** — darkness | choshech, chosheich, hoshech, chosech, afeilah, afelah, afela, ofel | draft | [darkness-choshech-afeilah.md](entries/darkness-choshech-afeilah.md)

*Choshech* is plain **darkness**, the dark of an ordinary night. *Afeilah* is **thick darkness**, and every authority who separates the two makes it the heavier word (Radak). Where they stand together, as in *choshech afeilah* at Makkas Choshech (Shemos 10:22), render the pair rather than repeat one word: "a thick darkness". The trap is writing "darkness" twice and losing the fact that the Torah escalated.

**חמור / חמורה / אתון / פרא / ערוד / עיר / סיח / פרד / פרדה / ימים** — donkey | chamor, chamorim, chamorah, aton, asson, atonot, atonos, atonim | draft | [donkey-chamor-aton-pere-ayir-pered.md](entries/donkey-chamor-aton-pere-ayir-pered.md)

These are different animals, not stylistic variants. *Chamor* is the domesticated donkey and, in Biblical Hebrew, of either sex (Radak): **donkey**. *Aton* is the she-donkey, the **jenny** or **she-ass** (Radak; Bilaam's animal through Bamidbar 22). *Pere* (Aramaic *arud*) is the undomesticated **wild ass**; Radak classes *chamor* as a *behemah* and *pere* as a *chayah*. *Ayir* is the young one, a **foal**; *pered* is the **mule**. The trap is Zecharyah 9:9, where *chamor* and *ayir* stand in one pasuk and a single English "donkey" erases the picture.

**יבש / חרב / נגב / יבשה / חרבה** — dry | yavesh, yaveish, yavshah, yabashah, yabasha, vativash, chorev, charev | draft | [dry-yavesh-chorev-negev.md](entries/dry-yavesh-chorev-negev.md)

*Charev* is **dried** in the partial sense: the surface has dried but moisture remains underneath. *Yavesh* is **dry** outright, drained through and through. The order is fixed: wherever the two stand together, *charev* comes first and *yavesh* second (Malbim), so a translation must show a progression, not repeat one English word. *Negev* is **arid** or **parched**, closer to *charev* than to *yavesh*, and in Tanach it usually means "south" rather than "dry" at all.

**עפר / אפר / אבק / דשן** — dust | afar, ofor, eifer, efer, avak, ovok, deshen, doshen | draft | [dust-afar-eifer-avak-deshen.md](entries/dust-afar-eifer-avak-deshen.md)

*Afar* is **dirt** or **soil**, the general word, and the one that can grow things. *Eifer* is **ashes**, what fire leaves, and it grows nothing. *Avak* is **dust**, fine powder produced by pulverizing rather than by burning. *Deshen* is a narrower word for ashes, tied to fat and to the Mikdash. The fact not to lose: the difference between *afar* and *eifer* is fertility, not fineness, which is why the Torah calls the ashes of the Parah Adumah *afar* and not *eifer*.

**תבל / ארקא / חלד / חדל / חרבה / יבשה / קרקע** — earth (the seven names) | taivel, tevel, teivel, arka, arah, ara, cheled, chedel | draft | [earth-taivel-arka-cheled.md](entries/earth-taivel-arka-cheled.md)

Avos d'Rabbi Nasan (version 1, ch. 37) counts seven words for the earth: *eretz*, *adamah*, *arka*, *charavah*, *yabashah*, *taivel*, *cheled*. Of these, *taivel* is **the inhabited world**, the settled parts as against *eretz* for the whole; *arka* is **earth**, a hapax in the one Aramaic pasuk of Yirmiyahu; *cheled* is **the transient world**, the earth under time; *charavah* and *yabashah* are **dry land**. Never confuse *taivel* with *tevel*, abomination: same letters, different nikud, one root. And *karka* is not on this list at all.

**פיל / שנהב / שנהבים / פלדש** — elephant | pil, pila, pilim, pilei, shenhav, shenhab, shenhabim, shenhavim | draft | [elephant-pil-shenhav.md](entries/elephant-pil-shenhav.md)

*Pil* is the ordinary word for **elephant**, but it is post-biblical; it belongs to the language of Chazal, not to Tanach. *Shenhav* (plural *shenhabim*) is the biblical word, and most of the commentators render it **ivory**, not the animal (Targum, Rashi, Radak, Metzudos, Ralbag, Rabbi Yosef Karo). A minority read it as the elephant itself (Ibn Janach, HaKallir, Rabbi Avraham ben Shlomo, and the commentary printed as Rashi to Divrei HaYamim). Render *shenhabim* at Melachim I 10:22 as **ivory** and note the minority; even on the minority reading the word names the elephant precisely as the source of ivory, so "ivory" loses less than "elephant" would.

**רעב / כפן / בצורת** — famine | raav, raavon, kafan, kefan, kafna, kefina, kafnah, batzores | draft | [famine-raav-kafan-batzores.md](entries/famine-raav-kafan-batzores.md)

*Ra'av* is the standard word, some 130 occurrences: **famine**, or **hunger** when the subject is a person. *Kafan* appears in Hebrew only twice, both in Iyov (5:22, 30:3), and is the harder case: read it as **dearth** or **starvation**, the famine of prices or of a stomach that has stopped complaining, not as a second word for the same thing. *Batzores* is the rabbinic term, closest to **drought** (the root's sense of withholding). Never render *kafan* simply "famine" where it stands beside *ra'av* in the same pasuk; the pairing is the whole reason the distinction is being drawn.

**אש / אור / להב / להבה / שלהבת / להט / לפיד / אבוקה / נר / נורא / שרגא** — fire | aish, esh, eish, ur, or, ohr, lahav, lehavah | draft | [fire-aish-lehavah-shalheves-lapid.md](entries/fire-aish-lehavah-shalheves-lapid.md)

*Aish* is the general word: **fire**. *Ur* is a **fire** too, but a smaller one (Malbim). *Lahav* and *lehavah* are the **flame** that shoots outward from a fire, which is why the same word means a **blade**. *Shalheves* is a flame gripping some material it is consuming. *Lahat* is fire moving fast, a **blaze**. *Lapid* is a **torch**, the object carrying the fire, and *avukah* a multi-wick torch against *ner*, the single-wick **lamp**. The one thing not to get wrong: *lehavah* is not a bigger fire than *aish*; it is the outward-reaching part of one.

**דג / דגה / דאג / נונא / מוניני / גילדנא / טרית / אברומא / צחנתא** — fish | dag, dagim, dagah, daga, digas, digat, digei, dayag | draft | [fish-dag-dagah.md](entries/fish-dag-dagah.md)

*Dag* is **a fish**, the singular; *dagah* is the loaded form, and what it adds is disputed: **a large fish or a small one** (Chazal), **a female fish** (Rashi), **a dead fish** (Zohar), or **fish as a collective**, the species rather than the individual (Ibn Ezra). Where a passage switches from *dag* to *dagah*, as Yonah does between 2:1 and 2:2, the switch is the point and English "fish" for both erases it; say which reading you are following. The Aramaic *nuna* and its diminutives (*munini*, *gildna*, *avruma*, *tzachanta*) are all **small fish**, not a species name.

**נון / כוורא / שיבוטא / סנדל / תנין / נין** — fish | nun, nuna, nuni, kavra, kevara, shibuta, shibbuta, sandal | draft | [fish-nun-kavra-shibuta.md](entries/fish-nun-kavra-shibuta.md)

*Dag* is the Hebrew for fish and the only one Tanach uses; *nun* and *kavra* are its Aramaic counterparts, and both render **fish** plainly. Do not translate *shibuta* or *sandal* at all: they are named species, so transliterate and gloss them, since every English equivalent on offer is a guess. *Tanin* is a **sea monster**, not a fish. The one thing not to get wrong is that *nun* never occurs in Tanach in the sense of fish; where it does occur, in *Yehoshua bin Nun*, it is a man's name, and the fish sense is a derashah on it, not a translation.

**אוכל / מזון / יבול / תבואה / תנובה / דגן / בר / שבר / עבור** — food | ochel, ochl, maachal, machal, mazon, mezonos, mezonot, yevul | draft | [food-ochel-shever-tevuah-dagan.md](entries/food-ochel-shever-tevuah-dagan.md)

*Ochel* is **food**, anything eaten, the widest word. *Mazon* is **sustenance**, food that actually nourishes. Of the produce words: *tevuah* is **crop**, what comes forth (ripe or not); *tenuvah* is **ripe produce**; *yevul* is **produce** seen as perishable; *dagan* is threshed **grain**; *bar* is grain **cleaned of its chaff**; *shever* is **provisions**, grain bought in a famine. The trap: Onkelos renders *ochel*, *bar*, *shever* and *dagan* all by *avur*, and English "grain" or "food" will do the same. Where a pasuk uses two of them, the difference is usually the state of the grain, so translate the state.

**מזל / גד / מזר** — fortune | mazal, mazel, mazalos, mazalot, mazaltov, gad, gada, mazar | draft | [fortune-mazal-gad-mazar.md](entries/fortune-mazal-gad-mazar.md)

*Mazal* is a **constellation** in its one Biblical use and, in Chazal, the **destiny** or Divine influence channeled to a person or thing. *Gad* is **fortune**, and more precisely the spiritual force assigned to a particular thing (Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim). *Mazar* is a hapax; render it **constellation** with Rashi, noting Ibn Ezra. The trap is "luck": *mazal* is never blind chance, and a translation that writes "luck" imports the very idea Chazal reject.

**גן / גנה / פרדס / בוסתן / קן / מגן** — garden | gan, ginah, gina, ginot, ginos, ginunyita, pardes, pardeis | draft | [garden-gan-pardes-bustan.md](entries/garden-gan-pardes-bustan.md)

*Gan* is the general **garden**, an enclosed and guarded plot; in Tanach it is the only common one of the three, and in the Mishnah it appears as *ginah*. *Pardes* is the narrower and grander word, usually **orchard**, and Koheles names both in one pasuk (Koheles 2:5), which is the proof they are not synonyms. *Bustan* is Talmudic Aramaic for a **garden**, a Persian word, and Rashi defines it by *pardes* while Rashbam defines it by *gan*. The one thing not to get wrong: never render *pardes* as "paradise" in a pasuk, an English word that reached us through the Septuagint and reads a later theology back into the text.

**גפן / שורק / שורקה / שדמה / שריג / סרק** — grapevine | gefen, gafna, sorek, soreik, sorekah, sorekah, shdeimah, shedeimah | draft | [grapevine-gefen-sorek-shdeimah.md](entries/grapevine-gefen-sorek-shdeimah.md)

*Gefen* is the plain word: **grapevine**. *Sorek* is not a second word for the same thing but a **choice vine**, the best stock, by most readings. *Shdeimah* is the disputed one: Rashi makes it a **grain field**, the grammarians a **vine**, Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim simply rich, nourishing **ground**. *Sarig* is the **tendril**. Rabbi Shlomo of Urbino (*Ohel Moed*) lists *gefen*, *sorek*, and *shdeimah* together as "grapevines", but the sources that treat each word separately do not let them collapse: keep the quality in *sorek* and keep *shdeimah* open.

**סוס / רכש / אחשתרנים / רמכים / רמך** — horse | sus, susim, sussim, rechesh, rechashim, richashei, achashtranim, achashtran | draft | [horse-sus-rechesh-achashtranim.md](entries/horse-sus-rechesh-achashtranim.md)

*Sus* is the ordinary **horse**. *Rechesh* is a particular kind, fast, used for couriers or for pulling: **swift steed** or **courier horse**, not plain "horse." *Achashtranim* and *remachim* occur together only in Esther 8:10, and Megillah 18a says outright that we do not know what they mean; the majority reading makes *achashtranim* **mules** and *remachim* the **mares** that bore them. Never render the four words of that pasuk as four synonyms for "horse."

**ארץ / אדמה** — land | eretz, aretz, aratzos, aratzot, adamah, adama, admas, admat | draft | [land-eretz-adamah.md](entries/land-eretz-adamah.md)

*Eretz* is **land** as territory: a country, a nation's settled land, and by extension the earth itself down to its core. *Adamah* is **ground**: the arable surface, the soil worked for crops. Render *eretz* "land" or "country" and *adamah* "ground" or "soil", and do not let "earth" cover both. One caution: the majority (the Gra, Malbim, Rabbi Moravsky) hold *eretz* is the broader word and *adamah* the thin top layer, but the Maharshal inverts it, so a passage that argues from which word is broader must be checked against the commentator in front of you.

**ארץ / אדמה / רצון / חפץ** — land (running versus passive) | eretz, aretz, ratz, ratzah, ratzon, ritzah, adamah, adama | draft | [land-eretz-adamah-ratzon.md](entries/land-eretz-adamah-ratzon.md)

*Eretz* is **land** and *adamah* is **ground**, and this article grounds the difference in the two roots: *eretz* from REISH-TZADI, running, and *adamah* from DALET-MEM, likeness and so inertness. *Eretz* is therefore the active land, and often Eretz Yisrael in particular; *adamah* the passive ground, and often the lands outside it. *Ratzon* belongs to the same root as *eretz*: it is a person's **will** at its deepest, as against *chefetz*, the immediate goal in front of him. The trap is "earth," which will take both Hebrew words at once.

**כנים / כנם / כנה / כן / קלמתא / כלמי / פרעש** — lice | kinnim, kinim, kinam, kinah, kein, ken, kalmta, kalmei | draft | [lice-kinnim-kalmta-parosh.md](entries/lice-kinnim-kalmta-parosh.md)

*Kinnim* is **lice**, the plural; *kinam* is the same creature named as a **species**; *kinah* is a single **louse**, a word of the Mishnah and Gemara rather than of Tanach. The Aramaic is *kalmta*, and in the Gemara *kalmei*. A *parosh* is not a louse at all but a **flea**. The trap is treating *kein* in Yeshayahu 51:6 and Bamidbar 13:33 as "louse" without saying so; the standard reading there is "so, likewise", and the louse reading is Malbim's.

**ארי / אריה / כפיר / לביא / ליש / שחל / שחץ / גור** — lion | ari, aryeh, arye, kfir, kefir, lavi, laviyah, layish | draft | [lion-aryeh-kfir-lavi-layish.md](entries/lion-aryeh-kfir-lavi-layish.md)

All of these render **lion** in English, and Chazal count six or seven of them. There are three ways to take the set, and the choice governs the translation. Chazal (Sanhedrin 95a; *Yalkut Shimoni*) treat them as one animal named for seven different traits. Radak and the Vilna Gaon treat them as stages of one lion's life: *gur* the nursing cub, *kfir* the young lion, *aryeh* or *ari* the grown lion, *lavi* and *layish* older still, *shachal* the oldest. Abarbanel flattens them to two, *gur* and *kfir* young, *aryeh*, *layish* and *lavi* grown. Default to Radak's progression, render *gur* "lion cub" and *kfir* "young lion," and never let plain "lion" cover two of these words in one pasuk.

**ארבה / גזם / ילק / חסיל / חגב / סלעם / חרגול / צלצל / צפוני / חנמל** — locust | arbeh, arbe, gazam, yelek, yelak, chasil, chagav, chagavim | draft | [locust-arbeh-gazam-yelek-chasil.md](entries/locust-arbeh-gazam-yelek-chasil.md)

These are species names, not synonyms. *Arbeh* is both one species and the umbrella word: render it **locust** and let it carry the swarm. *Gazam* is the cutter, *yelek* the licker, *chasil* the finisher, and in Yoel 1:4 they arrive in that fixed order, each eating what the one before left; keep four distinct English words there, since one repeated "locust" destroys the pasuk. *Chagav* is the general term for **kosher** grasshoppers (Meiri, *Ohel Mo'ed*). The trap is translating any of them as "grasshopper" in a plague context, where the sense is a devouring swarm.

**אחו / אגם / ביצה / סוף / גומא / בוץ** — marsh | achu, achoo, agam, agama, agamim, bitzah, bitza, bitzaim | draft | [marsh-achu-agam-bitzah.md](entries/marsh-achu-agam-bitzah.md)

*Agam* is the standard word for a **marsh**, and Rashi also glosses it as a standing **pond**. *Bitzah* is a **mire**, ground held in mud, from *botz*, mud. *Achu* divides the meforshim: Rashi makes it a **marsh**, plain and simple; Ramban and Rav Hirsch make it the **riverbank growth**, the reeds and grasses, not the place. *Suf* is not a marsh at all but the **reeds** that grow in one, which is why *Yam Suf* is the Sea of Reeds. The trap: English "marsh" and "swamp" will take *achu*, *agam* and *bitzah* together, and Iyov 8:11 puts *bitzah* and *achu* in one pasuk with *gome* beside them.

**שמן / יצהר / צהר** — oil | shemen, shamen, shuman, shemen zayis, shemen zayit, yitzhar, itzhar, tzohar | draft | [oil-shemen-yitzhar.md](entries/oil-shemen-yitzhar.md)

*Shemen* is the general word, **oil**, which is why the Torah must say *shemen zayis zach* to specify **pure olive oil**. *Yitzhar* is **olive oil** in itself, and is never paired with *zayis* in Tanach. Every occurrence of *yitzhar* is agricultural, the yield of the press, so where a passage lists produce, render it "oil" as a crop and *shemen* as the substance. What a translator must not do is let one English word erase the fact that *yitzhar* already carries "olive."

**מישור / שרון / איל / ככר / אבל / פדן** — plain | mishor, meishar, mishur, sharon, sharona, eil, eil paran, elon | draft | [plain-mishor-sharon-eil-kikar.md](entries/plain-mishor-sharon-eil-kikar.md)

*Mishor* is the neutral word: **plain**, any level stretch, and it may be barren. *Sharon* is a **fertile plain**, rich enough to farm (Radak), and in Tanach it is usually the proper name of the coastal plain. *Eil* / *eilonei* is contested: **plain** for Targum Onkelos, the **name** of a particular plain for Rashi, and **wooded land outside a city** for Ramban. *Kikar* is the **grassland plain** of the Yarden, *avel* a bare **prairie**, and *padan* the Aramaic of *sadeh*, **field**. The one thing not to get wrong: *mishor* also means moral straightness, so a *mishor* in a mussar passage is uprightness, not geography.

**ארס / סם המות / ראש / רוש / חמת / רעל / תרעלה / עכס / לענה** — poison | eres, iras, sam hamaves, sam hamavet, sam hachaim, samim, rosh, rowsh | draft | [poison-eres-rosh-chamat-sam-hamaves.md](entries/poison-eres-rosh-chamat-sam-hamaves.md)

*Eres* is **venom**, in practice snake venom, and it is Talmudic, not Biblical. *Sam hamaves* is the general **poison**, literally the deadly drug, and never venom. *Rosh* is **poison** in Tanach, drawn either from a snake's head or from a poisonous plant. *Chamat* is **venom** as what an angered snake emits. *Ra'al* is disputed: poison, or a draught that leaves a man limp and reeling. The trap: English "poison" absorbs all of them, and Chullin 3:5 turns on *sam hamaves* being precisely not venom.

**ארגמן / ארגון / סגול / לילך** — purple | argaman, argamon, argavan, argevan, argevana, argamana, segol, sagol | draft | [purple-argaman-segol-lilach.md](entries/purple-argaman-segol-lilach.md)

Classical Hebrew has no word for purple. *Argaman* is not a color word at all: every rishon takes it as **wool dyed** a particular shade, and the shade the sources describe is a **red**, whether crimson, orange, or a yellowish red, never a mixture of red with blue. Render it "*argaman*" and gloss it as red-dyed wool; if a translation demands the conventional "purple," say in a note that the English word once covered any deep red. *Segol* and *lilach* are Modern Hebrew flower-names pressed into service as color words, of no use in a Chumash.

**גשם / מטר / יורה / מלקוש** — rain | geshem, geshm, gashmiyus, gashmiyut, matar, mattar, yoreh, moreh | draft | [rain-geshem-matar-yoreh-malkosh.md](entries/rain-geshem-matar-yoreh-malkosh.md)

*Geshem* and *matar* both mean **rain**, and the authorities split on which is the general word and which the specific one; render both "rain" and let the context carry any distinction. *Yoreh* and *malkosh* are not kinds of rain but seasons of it: *yoreh* is **the first rain**, at the start of the rainy season, and *malkosh* **the last rain**, in early spring before the harvest. Never render *yoreh* and *malkosh* as "early rain and late rain" without saying what they are early and late to; the pasuk in Shema (Devarim 11:14) is promising a full year's cycle.

**אדום / שני / תולעת שני / חמוץ / אמוץ / שרוק / חום / חכלילי / סומק / זהורית / גיחור** — red | adom, adum, adumah, admoni, adamdam, odem, shani, shni | draft | [red-adom-shani-chamutz.md](entries/red-adom-shani-chamutz.md)

*Adom* is plain **red** and the deepest of the shades. *Shani* (*tola'as shani*) is **crimson**, the worm-dye of the Mishkan, never flattened to "red" where the ritual color matters. *Chamutz* and *amutz* are a weaker red: **red-stained** in Yeshayahu, **chestnut** in Zechariah. *Seruk* is **pink**, *chum* **brown**, *chachlilei* a drinker's **bloodshot** eye; in Aramaic *sumak* is everyday red.

**נהר / נהרות / יאור / יאורים / נחל / נחלים** — river | nahar, neharot, neharos, yeor, yeior, yor, yeorim, yeorot | draft | [river-nahar-yeor-nachal.md](entries/river-nahar-yeor-nachal.md)

*Nahar* is the default **river**, a natural watercourse fed by tributaries. *Yeor* is the Nile and rivers that behave like it, a river that floods past its banks; where it stands beside *nahar* it means **an irrigation canal** (Rashi to Shemos 7:19), and standing alone it is **the River**, the Nile. *Nachal* is a **brook** or **wadi**, the seasonal watercourse that cuts a valley and runs to the sea, and the same word names the valley itself. The one thing not to get wrong: *nahar* and *yeor* are only sharply distinguished when they appear together (Rabbi Malkiel Tzvi Tannenbaum); apart, either can mean a river of any kind.

**אבן / סלע / לבנה** — rock | even, ehven, avanim, avnei, sela, selah, slaim, selaim | draft | [rock-even-sela.md](entries/rock-even-sela.md)

*Even* is the general word: **stone**, any naturally occurring one, as against *leveinah*, a man-made brick (Malbim). *Sela* is a **crag**, a hard rock still fixed in the ground and often a rocky height, which is why it also became the name of a coin. Rabbi Immanuel Chai Ricci puts the difference in attachment (*sela* joined to the ground, *even* loose enough to pick up); Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim puts it in hardness, with *even* the hypernym that includes *sela*. The trap: English "rock" and "stone" are near-interchangeable, so a passage that contrasts *even* and *sela* (the Mishnah at *Zevachim* 13:3) needs two clearly different English words.

**צור / חלמיש / סלע / אבן** — rock | tzur, tzor, tzurim, tzarur, tzorer, tzayir, chalamish, halamish | draft | [rock-tzur-chalamish.md](entries/rock-tzur-chalamish.md)

The scale runs *even* (any stone), *sela* (hard crag), *tzur* (harder still), *chalamish* (hardest of all). Render *tzur* **flintrock** or **hard rock**, and where it is a divine epithet, **Rock**; Rambam adds a second sense, the quarry a stone is hewn from, hence **source**. *Chalamish* is conventionally **flint**, but Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim makes it a subdivision of *tzur*, the very hardest stones. The distinguishing fact: Malbim, a *sela* is porous and can hold water, a *tzur* cannot, which is why Rabbi Chaim Vital calls a *tzur* the stone that gives fire. The trap: rendering both *sela* and *tzur* "rock" flattens Devarim 32, where the two are theologically distinct.

**גבר / שכוי / זיז שדי / תרנגול** — rooster | gever, gevurah, sechvi, sekhvi, sechavi, ziz sadai, ziz saday, tarnegol | draft | [rooster-gever-sechvi-tarnegol.md](entries/rooster-gever-sechvi-tarnegol.md)

*Tarnegol* is the ordinary word for a **rooster** in Mishnaic Hebrew and the safe rendering wherever it appears. *Gever* means **man** or **male** far more often than rooster; read it as a bird only where the context forces it (the Mishnah's "call of the *gever*," Targum Yonasan on Etzion Gever). *Sechvi* is the genuinely open word: Chazal fix it as **rooster** in the morning berachah, but most mefarshim read it as the **heart**. *Ziz sadai* is a bird of some kind, which Targum renders **wild fowl**. Never translate *sechvi* as "rooster" in Iyov 38:36 without noting that Rashi, Ibn Ezra, Radak, Rosh, and Tur read it otherwise.

**שושנה / חבצלת / ורד / נרקיס** — rose | shoshanah, shoshana, shoshan, shoshanim, chavatzeles, chavatzelet, chavatzalet, vered | draft | [rose-shoshanah-chavatzeles-vered.md](entries/rose-shoshanah-chavatzeles-vered.md)

None of the three can be pinned to a modern species with confidence, and a translator should know that before choosing a word. *Shoshanah* is the Biblical flower, best rendered **rose** in a Torah context (Rabbi Eliyahu HaBachur: it grows among thorns, and lilies do not), though Modern Hebrew has reassigned it to the **lily**. *Chavatzeles* is either the same flower at a different stage or in a different place, or a **daffodil**; *Shulchan Aruch* rules it a *narkis*. *Vered* is post-Biblical **rose**, and the Targumim use it to render *shoshanah*. The trap: one English "rose" flattens all three, and Shir HaShirim 2:1 sets *chavatzeles* and *shoshanah* side by side in a single pasuk.

**שה / צאן / עז / תיש / גדי / שעיר / רחל / איל / כבש / כשב / טלה / עתוד / יובל** — sheep and goats | seh, tzon, tzon, eiz, ez, izzim, tayish, tayesh | draft | [sheep-and-goats-seh-keves-ayil-eiz.md](entries/sheep-and-goats-seh-keves-ayil-eiz.md)

Sort by species before reaching for an English word. General (either species): *seh* = **a lamb or kid**, one animal; *tzon* = **flock**. Goats: *eiz* = **she-goat**, *tayish* = **he-goat**, *gedi* and *seir* = **kid**. Sheep: *rachel* = **ewe**, *ayil* = **ram**, *keves* and *tleh* = **lamb**. *Kesev* is either an exact metathesis of *keves* or a slightly older or more general sheep, depending on whose reading you follow. The trap is English "sheep," which will swallow *seh*, *tzon*, *keves*, *kesev*, and *tleh* together and lose the species in the bargain.

**נחש / שרף / תנין / צפע / צפעוני / אפעה / עכשוב / פתן / שפיפון** — snake | nachash, nahash, saraph, saraf, seraph, tanin, tannin, tzefa | draft | [snake-nachash-saraph-tanin-peten.md](entries/snake-nachash-saraph-tanin-peten.md)

*Nachash* is the general word, **snake**, and on Wertheimer's second reading specifically the snake that can be charmed. *Saraph* is the **fiery serpent** whose venom burns. *Tanin* is the **sea serpent** (Rashi: simply a large fish). *Tzefa* / *tzifoni* and *efeh* are **vipers**, the most venomous and the most malignant. *Peten* is the **asp** that charming cannot touch. *Shephiphon* is a small **adder** that keeps its body on the ground, and *achshuv* is a *hapax* whose identity is unsettled. Avos d'Rabbi Nassan lists the seven beside *nachash* but its own explanations have been corrupted by copyists; the distinctions below come from other sources.

**מין / זן / סוג / מינים / תמונה** — species | min, minim, minnim, minei, zan, zen, znim, zenei | draft | [species-min-zan-sug.md](entries/species-min-zan-sug.md)

*Min* is the Torah's word: **kind** or **species**, a grouping of individuals that share defining properties. *Zan* is its Aramaic equivalent, the word Targum uses for every *min* in Tanach; in its three Hebrew appearances the meforshim argue whether it means "type" at all. *Sug* is post-biblical and, in the language of logic, the **genus** above the *min*: Rambam through Rabbi Moshe Ibn Tibbon, and Ramchal, make *sug* the parent category and *min* the child. The one thing not to get wrong: *min* is not the modern biological species. Ramban and the Ran already say the Torah's *minim* need not map onto the taxonomies of today, so "species" in a translation should be understood as "kind."

**גיחון / שילוח / גחון / נגיחה / שלח** — spring | gichon, gihon, shiloach, shiloah, shelach, shilucha, gachon, gochon | draft | [spring-gichon-shiloach-gachon.md](entries/spring-gichon-shiloach-gachon.md)

*Gichon* and *Shiloach* are two names for one body of water, the spring by Yerushalayim (Rashi, Radak, Targum). Render both as proper names, **Gichon** and **Shiloach**, never as a common noun like "spring" or "aqueduct"; where a passage needs a gloss, "the Gichon spring." The trap is the *other* Gichon: the river of Bereishis 2:13 that circles Kush is, for Rashi and Rabbi Avraham Maimuni, a different river entirely that merely shares the name, so do not let one English "Gichon" flatten the two. *Gachon* (Bereishis 3:14) is not a place at all but the serpent's **belly**, from the same biliteral stock.

**שמש / חמה / חרס / ירח / לבנה / סהר** — sun and moon | shemesh, shamash, shimush, chamah, chama, chamanim, cheres, charas | draft | [sun-and-moon-shemesh-chamah-yareach.md](entries/sun-and-moon-shemesh-chamah-yareach.md)

Three words each, and each names its luminary by a different aspect. *Shemesh* is the **sun** as servant, doing its assigned work; *chamah* the **sun** as the hot one; *cheres* the **sun** as the baker of pottery, or the pottery-colored one at sunrise and sunset. *Yareach* is the **moon** as the traveler on its path; *levanah* the **moon** as the white one; *sahar* the **crescent**, the moon by its shape. All six are Rabbi Aharon Marcus's account. In ordinary translation all three suns are "the sun" and all three moons "the moon"; surface the aspect only where the passage turns on it.

**קוץ / חוח / סלון / סירים / דרדר / אטד / חדק / שמיר / שית / ברקן / חרול / קימוש / עקרב / סיכים / סנה** — thorns | kotz, kotzim, koutz, choach, chochim, choah, salon, salonim | draft | [thorns-kotz-dardar-sneh-charul.md](entries/thorns-kotz-dardar-sneh-charul.md)

Hebrew has some twenty words in this field; Rashi (to Yechezkel 2:6) and Rabbi Saadia Gaon both say so outright, and Rabbi Shlomo of Urbino (*Ohel Moed*) lists seventeen. Default *kotz* to **thorn** (the plain, most frequent term), *dardar* to **thistle**, *sneh* to **thornbush** (never translated where it names the Burning Bush), *charul* to **nettle**, *chedek* to **brier**, *sikim* and *tzninim* to **thorns** in the idioms "thorns in your eyes / in your sides." The trap: קוץ ודרדר is two species, not a doubled phrase, so it must read "thorn and thistle," never "thorns and brambles" as one blur.

**חדקל / דגלת / הדקל / תיגרס / דקל** — tigris | chidekel, chidakel, hiddekel, chiddekel, diglat, diglas, deglat, hadekel | draft | [tigris-chidekel-diglat.md](entries/tigris-chidekel-diglat.md)

*Chidekel* and *Diglat* are one river under two names, and both render **Tigris**: *Chidekel* is the Biblical Hebrew, *Diglat* the Aramaic that Targum and Shas use. Rashi says so explicitly, so never translate them as two rivers. Where a passage expounds the name, keep the Hebrew visible: Berachos 59b hears *chad* (sharp) and *kal* (swift) in *Chidekel*, and that derashah dies in English. *Tigras* in Chullin 59b is a separate word for an animal, not the river.

**עץ / אילן / אילנא** — tree | eitz, etz, atzei, eitzim, ilan, ilana, ilanos, ilanot | draft | [tree-eitz-ilan.md](entries/tree-eitz-ilan.md)

*Eitz* is the Hebrew word and *ilan* the Aramaic one, but in *lashon Chazal* they divide the field: *ilan* is the **living tree**, *eitz* is the **wood**. In Tanach *eitz* does both jobs, so a Chumash rendering picks "tree" or "wood" by context alone. The one thing not to get wrong: the berachah says *borei pri ha'eitz* over the fruit of an *ilan*, and that is not a slip.

**ירק / ירוק / ירקות / כרפס / כרפסא / רק** — vegetable | yerek, yarak, yarok, yerakot, yerakos, karpas, karpasa, kusbarta | draft | [vegetable-yerek-yarok-karpas.md](entries/vegetable-yerek-yarok-karpas.md)

*Yerek* (plural *yerakos*) is the generic word: **vegetable**, any of them. *Yarok* is the color, **green**, though in Rabbinic Hebrew it stretches from yellow through green to blue. *Karpas* is **not** a synonym for *yerek*: in the Mishnah it names one particular plant (the Yerushalmi identifies it as parsley), and in Megillas Esther it is a **fabric** or a **color**, not a food at all. The trap is treating Seder-night *karpas* as the Hebrew for "vegetable"; it became the standard term only through a piyut.

**דונג / שעוה / קירא / נכאת** — wax | donag, donog, shaavah, shaava, shayva, sheava, kira, kiri | draft | [wax-donag-shaavah-kira.md](entries/wax-donag-shaavah-kira.md)

*Donag* is the Biblical word for **wax**, and it always appears as the substance that melts away in fire. *Shaavah* is the Mishnaic and Targumic word for the same substance, but named from the honeycomb side: **beeswax**, or **candle wax** in a halachic context. *Kira* (and *keirosa*, *kiri*) is **wax** too, but it is a Greek and Latin loan-word, not Hebrew or Aramaic, so never build a derashah on it. *Nechot* in the Yosef narrative is ordinarily a **spice**; render it wax only where you are following the Midrash.

**לח / רטוב / רענן / רטפש / טופח / מפולם** — wetness | lach, leicho, lachim, ratuv, ratov, yirtavu, rotev, roteiv | draft | [wet-lach-ratuv-raanan.md](entries/wet-lach-ratuv-raanan.md)

*Lach* is **moist**: a little wet, and on Pappenheim's reading the inner moisture of a living thing. *Ratuv* is **wet** proper: enough liquid to show on the surface and to wet whatever touches it, and it comes from outside. *Ra'anan* is **fresh, verdant**, the moisture that keeps a plant growing. *Rutfash* is **revivified**, flesh restored to youthful moisture. *Tofeach* and *mefulam* are Rabbinic: **damp enough to transfer** and **sodden**. The trap is English "wet," which absorbs *lach* and *ratuv* and loses the halachically live difference between a little moisture and enough to wet something else.

**לבן / חיור / צחר / חלבון** — white | lavan, laban, livnah, libbun, chivar, chiver, chivaryan, chur | draft | [white-lavan-chivar-tzochar.md](entries/white-lavan-chivar-tzochar.md)

*Lavan* is the general Hebrew word: **white**, in any of its forms. *Chivar* is the Aramaic equivalent, used by the Targumim wherever the Hebrew has *lavan*; render it **white**, or **pale** where it describes a face. *Tzochar* is contested, and a translator must not assume it: Rashi reads **white**, Ibn Janach and Bedersi read **combed** or **pristine**, with whiteness only a side effect. *Chelbon* (Yechezkel 27:18) is probably **white wine**, from *chalav*, but two other readings compete. The trap: English "white" absorbs all four, and in Shoftim 5:10 or Yechezkel 27:18 the whole question is whether the word means white at all.

**מדבר / ערבה / ציה / צמאון** — wilderness | midbar, midbor, aravah, arava, aravot, arvos, arvot, tziyah | draft | [wilderness-midbar-aravah-tziyah.md](entries/wilderness-midbar-aravah-tziyah.md)

*Midbar* is the general **wilderness**: land inherently unfit for settlement, empty of people, where flocks are driven to graze. *Aravah* is a **desert plain** or **arid steppe**, not bone-dry but overrun with thorns and wild growth. *Tziyah* is a **parched land**, dried out by heat, the most desolate of the three. Malbim is the one who sorts them: *tziyah* and *aravah* are desolate from an outside cause (heat; weeds), *midbar* is desolate by its own nature. When all three appear in one pasuk, give each a distinct English phrase; do not let "desert" swallow the list.

**רימה / תולעה / תולע / תולעת / מתלעות / לע** — worm | rimah, rima, toleah, tolea, tola, tolaas, tolaat, tolaim | draft | [worm-rimah-toleah.md](entries/worm-rimah-toleah.md)

*Rimah* is the earlier and smaller thing: **maggots**, larvae, the first infestation, arguably not yet ambulatory. *Toleah* (and its common form *tola'as*) is the mature crawling **worm** that eats. The pair is always in that order in Tanach and in Chazal, *rimah v'toleah*, and the order is chronological (Netziv, Vilna Gaon), so a rendering must not reverse it or collapse it: "maggots and worms," not "worms and maggots" and not "worms" once. Where the phrase is a fixed idiom for the grave, "worms and maggots" is exactly the loss to avoid.


### other

**מצרים / מצור / פתרוס / רהב / כפתור / ארץ חם / לובים / להבים / ענמים / לודים** — Egypt | mitzrayim, mizraim, matzor, meitzar, patros, patrus, patrusim, pasrusim | draft | [egypt-mitzrayim-patros-rahav.md](entries/egypt-mitzrayim-patros-rahav.md)

*Mitzrayim* is **Egypt**, and also the name of the man from whom the Egyptians descend. *Patros* is properly **Upper Egypt** or the Patrusim who came from Mitzrayim; the *paytanim* use it, and *Ludim*, *Anamim*, and *Lehavim*, for Egypt as a whole by poetic license, not because the words are synonyms. *Rahav* is Egypt named for its haughtiness and the name of its heavenly minister; *eretz Cham* is the liturgical name. The trap is flattening all of these to "Egypt" in a piyyut, where the obscure name is the poet's whole point.

**יריחו / ירחו / עיר התמרים / ריח / ירח / תמר** — Jericho | yericho, yereicho, jericho, ir hatmarim, ir hareyach, ir hayareach, reyach, reiach | draft | [jericho-yereicho-yericho.md](entries/jericho-yereicho-yericho.md)

One city, two vocalizations, and three descriptive names. Render the place **Jericho** (or **Yericho**) throughout; what a translator must not do is flatten the vowels, since every explanation offered turns on which form a given sefer uses. *Yericho* (with a yod, the form of Yehoshua) is heard as *reyach*, scent; *Yereicho* (without it, the form of Chumash) as *yareyach*, the moon. **Ir HaTmarim**, the City of Dates, is a title, not a separate city, and where it appears without a name it means Jericho (Targum, Rashi).

**ציון / ירושלים / עיר דוד / מצודת ציון / הר ציון / ציון** — Jerusalem | tziyon, tzion, zion, har tziyon, yerushalayim, yerushalaim, yerushalem, yerushaleim | draft | [jerusalem-tziyon-yerushalayim.md](entries/jerusalem-tziyon-yerushalayim.md)

*Tziyon* and *Yerushalayim* are not interchangeable. *Tziyon* is the **City of David**, a separate walled city that only later came inside the walls of **Yerushalayim**; the nechamah formula says "the mourners of Tziyon and Yerushalayim" because they are two. Where *tziyon* stands for the Beis HaMikdash or for Klal Yisrael, it is the common noun *tziyun*, "outstanding", not the place. Render each by its own name and never write one for the other; where the Hebrew uses *Tziyon* for the whole city, that is synecdoche, the part naming the whole.

**מלאך / שליח / ציר / משחית / שלח / מלאכה / שגר / שדר** — angel | malach, mal'ach, malachim, malochim, shaliach, shliach, sheliach, shluchim | draft | [angel-malach-shaliach.md](entries/angel-malach-shaliach.md)

*Malach* is a **messenger** who stands in for the sender on a mission the sender would do himself (Pappenheim, Malbim); of Hashem's messengers it is an **angel**, and the same Hebrew word covers both. *Shaliach* is the plain **agent** or **emissary**, one who is sent (Rav Hirsch: merely a messenger; the Lissa Rav: one who chose to accept the mission). *Tzir* is an **envoy**, a diplomatic messenger who reports back (Malbim). The one thing not to get wrong: *malach* is not automatically "angel"; where the Midrash disputes whether Yaakov's *malachim* were angels or men, the English must leave the question open.

**כרובים / חיות / אופנים / אראלים / חשמלים / שרפים / מלאכים / אלהים / בני אלהים / אישים / עירין / קדישין / גלגלים** — angel classes | kruvim, keruvim, keruv, kruv, chayos, chayot, chayos hakodesh, ofanim | draft | [angel-classes-kruvim-chayos-ishim.md](entries/angel-classes-kruvim-chayos-ishim.md)

These are not synonyms for one another; they are ranks. Rambam (*Hilchos Yesodei HaTorah* 2:7) counts ten, from the *chayos* at the top down to the *ishim* at the bottom: *chayos*, *ofanim*, *erelim*, *chashmalim*, *serafim*, *malachim*, *elohim*, *bnei elohim*, *kruvim*, *ishim*. Render *kruvim* as **cherubim** and leave the rest transliterated; the one that also does double duty is *malachim*, which is both a rank of its own and the general word for **angels**. The trap is translating any of the others as "angel," which collapses a hierarchy into one word.

**תיבה / ארון** — ark | teivah, teiva, tevah, teva, aron, oron, aronos, aronot | draft | [ark-teivah-aron.md](entries/ark-teivah-aron.md)

Both words are rendered **ark** in English, and in Scripture they do not overlap: *teivah* is Noach's ark and the basket of the infant Moshe, *aron* is the Ark of the Covenant and Yosef's coffin. Where a distinction is needed, render *teivah* as **ark** or **basket** (a floating vessel, or a container for a living person) and *aron* as **ark**, **chest**, or **coffin**. The one thing not to get wrong: in Rabbinic Hebrew the two words are used almost interchangeably, so a Mishnaic *teivah* may well be an *aron*.

**סביב / היקף / עפף / חוג / חג** — around | saviv, sovev, sivuv, sevivon, sav, saba, soveh, heikef | draft | [around-saviv-heikef-afef-chug.md](entries/around-saviv-heikef-afef-chug.md)

*Heikef* is always a full circuit: **circumference**, **circuit** (hence *hakafos*). *Saviv* is going around a center without any guarantee of completing the circle, so **around**, **about**, and even a detour or a roundabout way of speaking; doubled as *saviv saviv* it does mean all the way around (Malbim). *Afef* is encircling that closes in on a man from every side, and Malbim holds it is always for the worse: **envelop**, **beset**. *Chug* is a circle seen from its center, an orbit. The trap is rendering *saviv* as "all around" where the Hebrew concedes only an arc.

**טנא / סל / כלוב / דוד / כלכלה / כפיפה** — basket | tene, teneh, teni, sal, salim, sala, keluv, kluv | draft | [basket-tene-sal-keluv.md](entries/basket-tene-sal-keluv.md)

*Sal* is the ordinary **basket**, and for Rabbi Pappenheim a **bread basket**. *Tene* is the **fruit basket** of bikkurim, loosely woven so the air can move through it. *Keluv* holds something alive or ripening, so "basket" for figs and "cage" for birds; *dud* is a basket or a pot by context, and *kalkalah* is the large Mishnaic **hamper**. The trap: English "basket" covers all of them.

**הוד / הדר / אדר** — beauty | hod, hodaah, hoda'ah, karnei hod, hod malchus, hod malchut, hadar, hadras panim | draft | [beauty-hod-hadar.md](entries/beauty-hod-hadar.md)

*Hod* is **splendor** or **majesty**: beauty that is a thing's own, inner and not seen with the eye, the sun's own light (Vilna Gaon; Malbim; Rav Hirsch, of Hashem, "His essence"). *Hadar* is **glory**: outward, visible, reflected beauty, the moon's light, the *esrog*'s fruit, the trappings of kingship without its essence (Vilna Gaon; Malbim; Rabbi Simcha Maimon). *Adar* is *hadar* with the Aramaic aleph and names the month. In the fixed pair הוד והדר keep the order and the two words: "splendor and glory". Trap: "glory" absorbs both, and "majesty" absorbs both; once one English word is assigned to *hod* in a passage, *hadar* must get the other.

**בעבור / בגלל / בשביל** — because | baavur, ba'avur, bavur, avur, biglal, bishvil, bi-shvil, shvil | draft | [because-baavur-biglal-bishvil.md](entries/because-baavur-biglal-bishvil.md)

*Biglal* is the backward-looking "because": a reason that **already exists** and justifies the act, so render it **on account of** or **because of**. *Baavur* is the forward-looking one: the reason **does not yet exist** and is the intended outcome, so render it **in order that** or **so that**. *Bishvil* is post-Biblical, usually equal to *biglal*, and is the everyday word in modern speech. The trap is that English "because" covers both directions, and a translator who writes "because" for *baavur* reverses the causality.

**מטה / ערש / ערס / עריסה** — bed | mitah, mita, mittah, eres, eress, arsa, arisah, arisa | draft | [bed-mitah-eres.md](entries/bed-mitah-eres.md)

*Mitah* is the ordinary **bed**, close to thirty appearances in Tanach, from a root meaning to incline or stretch out, since a man inclines his body to lie down. *Eres* is the rarer word, nine appearances, and every explanation of it makes it the more particular one: a fitted or special bed, an iron-framed one, a crib, a bed of woven or cross-fastened planks. The trap is that English "bed" covers both and Targum renders both by *arsa*. Ramchal holds they are genuine synonyms paired for poetic effect; most of the others do not, so where a passage puts them side by side, differentiate.

**מום / מאומה / פגם / סימפון / פג** — blemish | mum, moom, mumin, meumah, meuma, pgam, pegam, pagum | draft | [blemish-mum-pgam-simpon.md](entries/blemish-mum-pgam-simpon.md)

All three can be rendered **blemish**, and all three mean something else at root. *Mum* is a **something**, from *meumah*: a small something present that should not be, or absent that should be, which is why it covers both a lack and an excess. *Pgam* is a **nick** or **deficiency**, a groove taken out of something, and by extension a loss of worth or standing. *Simpon* does not mean blemish at all; it is a **cancelling condition**, whatever can void an agreement, and a physical defect is only one instance of it. The trap: rendering *simpon* as "blemish" in *Kesubos* 57b turns a rule about voidable kiddushin into a rule about bodies.

**אניה / ספינה / רפסודה / דוברה / עברה / דוגית / צי** — boats | oniah, oniyah, oni, oniyos, oniyot, sefinah, sefinta, safinah | draft | [boats-oniah-sefinah-rafsodah.md](entries/boats-oniah-sefinah-rafsodah.md)

*Oniah* is the general Biblical word, **ship**; *sefinah* is the ordinary Mishnaic word for the same thing, **ship** or **boat**, and appears only once in Tanach. *Rafsodah* and *dovrah* are both a **raft** of tied beams; *avarah* a **ferry**; *dugit* a small **fishing skiff**; *tzi* a **large ship**, usually a warship. In a pasuk *sefinah* is a rarity, in a Mishnah the default word.

**ספר / אגרת / מגילה / גליון / שטר / מחזור / קונטרס / כתב** — book | sefer, seforim, sfar, sofer, sippur, mispar, iggeret, igros | draft | [book-sefer-iggeret-megillah.md](entries/book-sefer-iggeret-megillah.md)

*Sefer* is the general **book** or written document, and in medieval usage specifically the **scroll**. *Megillah* is the **scroll** meant to be rolled up, and unqualified it means Megillas Esther. *Iggeret* is a **letter** or **epistle**, a short-lived document sent elsewhere. *Gilayon* is a single **sheet**, later a margin and then a **gloss**; *shtar* a **deed** meant to last; *machzor* originally the **codex**, later the yom tov siddur; *kuntres* a **pamphlet**. The trap: Esther is called all three of *sefer*, *megillah* and *iggeret*, and each name carries a halachah, so never level them to "book."

**לחם / ככר / חלה / פת / פיתא / ריפתא / נהמא** — bread | lechem, lachmah, kikar, kikkar, challah, chalah, challos, pat | draft | [bread-lechem-kikar-challah-pat.md](entries/bread-lechem-kikar-challah-pat.md)

*Lechem* is the wide word, **bread** in the sense of the staple food, over three hundred times in Tanach. *Kikar* is the **loaf**, named for its round shape, and in Tanach usually means a talent of metal or a round plain, not bread. *Challah* in Tanach is a **meal-cake**, almost always one brought as a korban, and in the Mishnah the **tithe** separated from dough, not the loaf we call by that name today. *Pat* is a **morsel**, a broken piece, which became a plain synonym for bread in Rabbinic usage. *Rifta* and *nahamah* are the Aramaic words for bread. The trap: "loaf" for both *kikar* and *challah*, which conflates a shape with a korban and a mitzvah.

**עוגה / חררה / ככר / חלה / גלוסקא / קוגל / פשטידה** — cake | ugah, ugot, ugos, ugiyah, ugiyot, ug ugah, chararah, charara | draft | [cake-ugah-chararah-gluskos.md](entries/cake-ugah-chararah-gluskos.md)

*Ugah* is not "cake" in the modern sense: its core is **round**, and as a food it is a **flat round loaf**, which is why the Torah calls the matzos of Yetzias Mitzrayim *ugos matzos* (Shemos 12:39). *Chararah* is Rashi's gloss on it. *Kikar* is a **loaf** with body, *challah* a **round loaf**, *gluska* the Rabbinic **fine roll**. The trap is English "cake," which sweetens what *ugah* does not: say "flat cake" or "round loaf."

**כסא / כסה / כס / כורסא / קתדרא** — chair | kisse, kise, kisei, kisseh, keis, kes, keseh, kursa | draft | [chair-kisse-keis-kursa.md](entries/chair-kisse-keis-kursa.md)

*Kisse* (כסא) is **throne**: not a neutral chair but a seat that marks rank, which is why it is the word for Hashem's *Kisse HaKavod*. *Kisseh* (כסה) and *keis* (כס) are the same word in two rarer spellings; do not translate them differently, but never let the shortened *keis* of Shemos 17:16 pass silently, since the missing letter is the whole point of the derashah. *Kursa* is simply the Aramaic of *kisse*, so a Targum or Gemara *kursa* is still **throne**; reserve **armchair** for Modern Hebrew. *Kathedra* in the Mishnah is an ordinary **chair**, and is the one word in the group with no rank attached.

**מקרה / קרי / תאונה / אירוע / מציאה** — chance | mikreh, mikre, keri, keriy, kri, koreh, korot, karahu | draft | [chance-mikreh-keri-teunah-irua.md](entries/chance-mikreh-keri-teunah-irua.md)

*Mikreh* is an **occurrence** or **happenstance**, the collision of a person with circumstances he did not arrange. *Keri* is the same root in the Torah's rebuke (Vayikra 26), where it is not a neutral event but **offhandedness** or **flippancy** toward Hashem, and in Mishnaic Hebrew it is the euphemism for a seminal emission (leave it transliterated there). *Te'unah* is a **mishap** or **befalling**, named for the causes that converge rather than the point of contact. *I'rua* is post-biblical, a plain **event** or **incident**. The trap: English "accident" flattens *keri* into an accident and loses the whole argument of Vayikra 26, which is about attitude, not chance.

**תם / תמים / תמימות / שלם / שלמות / שלום** — completeness | tam, tamim, temimah, temimos, temimot, temimus, temimut, tom | draft | [completeness-tam-shalem-tamim.md](entries/completeness-tam-shalem-tamim.md)

*Shalem* is **complete**, **whole**: nothing is now lacking, measured as a quantity, and having more than enough does not disqualify it (Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim). *Tam* / *tamim* is **whole** in quality: an exact match with nothing missing and nothing extra (Rabbeinu Bachaya; Pappenheim); of a korban **unblemished**, of a person **wholehearted** or **wholesome**, of the Torah **perfect**. So *shleimus* is **completeness** or **wholeness**, not "perfection": "perfection" names a ceiling reached, while *shalem* says only that nothing is missing and leaves room for more. Keep "perfect" for *tamim*, where the exactness earns it, and even there prefer "whole" or "wholehearted" of a person.

**פאה / קרן / זוית / פינה / מקצוע / קצה / עתיק / דמשק** — corner | peah, pe'ah, peot, peyot, paat, keren, qeren, karnayim | draft | [corner-peah-keren-zavit-pinah.md](entries/corner-peah-keren-zavit-pinah.md)

*Peah* is the broad **corner** or **side** of an area, and in most pesukim simply a **direction** (Onkelos: *ruach*). *Keren* is a **horn**, and a corner only where something juts out, chiefly the corners of the Altar. *Zavit* is the inside of a corner; *pinah* is the outside of it, and by extension a **prominence** or **cornerstone**, so *pinah* often wants "cornerstone" or "leading man," not "corner." *Miktzoa* is the sharp **angle** where two sides meet at a point; *katzeh* is the **edge/end**, a right-angled corner. The trap: Onkelos renders *peah*, *pinah*, *miktzoa*, *katzeh* and *paam* all as *zavit*, which flattens the Hebrew; a translation into English must not follow him and print "corner" five times.

**גרזן / קרדם / מעצד / כשיל / כילפות** — cutting tools | garzen, garzan, kardum, kardom, kordom, kardumo, kardumim, kardumot | draft | [cutting-tools-garzen-kardum-maatzad.md](entries/cutting-tools-garzen-kardum-maatzad.md)

*Garzen* is the general Biblical **axe**, and equally the stonecutter's **pick**; it is the only one of these words Chazal do not use in their own Hebrew. *Kardum* is the double-headed **adze-axe** or **mattock**, adze on one face and axe on the other. *Maatzad* is the **adze**, the woodworker's shaping tool and the smaller implement of the pair. *Kashil* is the **large axe** and *keilapot* the **hatchets**, both appearing once only, together in one pasuk. The one thing not to get wrong: *maatzad* and *kashil* are separated by size, and a real halachah in Bava Kamma turns on it, so never render both "axe."

**מיתה / מות / מת / פטירה / נפטר / פטור / נח נפשיה** — death | mitah, misah, mita, mavet, maves, mos, mot, meis | draft | [death-mitah-petirah.md](entries/death-mitah-petirah.md)

*Mitah* (*maves*, *meis*) is **death** as the physical end, the parting of body and soul, said of anyone and of animals. *Petirah* (*nifter*) is **passing**, literally "exiting, taking leave," the Rabbinic word for a person leaving This World for the Next; it is reserved by usage for the righteous and implies a continuation. Render *nifter* as "passed away" or "departed," never as "died" when the text sets it against *meis*; and do not soften a pasuk's *mos* into "passing."

**חורבן / חרבה / שממה / שמם / שם** — destruction | churban, chorban, churban habayis, churban habayit, charbah, charba, chorvah, chareiv | draft | [destruction-churban-shemamah.md](entries/destruction-churban-shemamah.md)

*Churban* is **destruction** in the sense of a place cut off from what kept it alive; the buildings may be standing. *Shemamah* is **desolation**, ruin visible to anyone who looks, and the stronger of the two. Vayikra 26:31 puts both in one pasuk, so a translation must not use one English word twice: "I will make your cities a ruin (*charbah*) and lay your sanctuaries waste (*vahashimosi*)."

**דין / רחמים / תקיעה / תרועה / אלקים** — divine judgment | din, rachamim, tekiah, tekiyah, teruah, teruos, teruot, Elokim | draft | [judgment-din-rachamim.md](entries/judgment-din-rachamim.md)

*Din* is **judgment** and *rachamim* is **mercy**, but the axis between them is not severity against leniency; it is where the attention rests. Under *rachamim* the focus is on the Giver, so the flow is constant and unearned. Under *din* the focus is on the receiver, so the flow is measured out by what he deserves. Keep both words as **judgment** and **mercy**, and never render *din* "punishment": Hashem gives under *din* too. The Divine Name of *din* is *Elokim*; the four-letter Name is the Name of *rachamim*. In the shofar, *tekiah* (one unbroken sound) answers to *rachamim* and *teruah* (a broken, fragmented sound) to *din*.

**עריסה / בצק / עיסה / חלה / ארוסה / ערבה** — dough | arisah, arisa, aristeichem, batzek, batzeik, issah, isah, issa | draft | [dough-arisah-batzek-issah.md](entries/dough-arisah-batzek-issah.md)

All three mean **dough**, and in most contexts plain "dough" is right for each. Where a passage distinguishes them: *arisah* is dough as a **mixture** just kneaded together (the Torah's word in the mitzvah of *challah*), *batzek* is dough already worked into one batch and **rising** (the Torah's word for ordinary dough), and *issah* is the Mishnaic word, dough as the thing that is **kneaded and pressed**. Ibn Janach and Radak hold *arisah* and *batzek* are simply synonyms; the distinguishing reading is the minority but is the one that carries halachic weight.

**כרת / כריתות / ערירי / ערירים** — excision | karet, kares, kareis, krisus, keritot, kerisos, sefer krisus, ariri | draft | [excision-karet-ariri.md](entries/excision-karet-ariri.md)

*Karet* is **excision**, the sinner himself cut off: from his years, from his soul's place in the World of Souls, or from the source his soul hangs on. Render it *karet* with a gloss, or "cut off," never "extermination." *Ariri* is **childless**, the line ending rather than the man. Whether they are one punishment or two is a *machlokes*: Rashi holds every *karet* can carry *ariri* with it; Riva holds *ariri* is a separate penalty attaching only to the forbidden marriages. The trap is the English "cut off," which fits both and hides which one the pasuk means.

**קמח / סולת / גרש / סמידא / קמה** — flour | kemach, soles, solet, solta, geres, geress, semida, semidah | draft | [flour-kemach-soles.md](entries/flour-kemach-soles.md)

*Kemach* is ordinary **flour**, ground with the bran and germ still in it: **whole wheat flour**, and by extension anything of the coarser grade. *Soles* is the sifted, refined grade: **fine flour** (Rav Hirsch: white flour, endosperm only). The difference is not chiefly fineness of grind but purity; *kemach* has everything in it, *soles* has been sifted clean. Whenever a source sets them opposite each other, *soles* is the better and *kemach* the lesser, and English "flour" must not be allowed to carry both.

**טוב / נעם / נעים / שפר / שפיר** — good | tov, tob, tovah, noam, no'am, naim, na'im, neimah | draft | [good-tov-noam-shapir.md](entries/good-tov-noam-shapir.md)

*Tov* is **good**, the plain and general word. *Noam* (adjective *naim*) is **pleasant**: goodness felt as beauty that draws a person in; Rabbi Bedersi (*Chotam Tochnit*) defines it as *yofi*. *Shapir* / *shefer* is **fine** or **fitting**: goodness in the sense of well done, proper, meeting the standard; Bedersi defines it as *tov*, and in the Gemara *shapir* is the adverb "well". Two readings of Bedersi's split: the column (Rabbi Klein) takes *noam* as subjective, personally felt goodness and *shapir* as objective goodness; Rabbi Shaul Goldman takes *noam* as attraction (whatever its basis) and *shapir* as plain positivity with no pull in it. Either way the rendering holds: *noam* "pleasant", *shapir* "fine". Trap: "good" absorbs all three; when *tov* and *naim* sit in one pasuk (Bereishis 49:14) they need two English words.

**מקבת / פטיש / קורנס / הלמות / ארזפתא** — hammer | makeves, makevet, makavos, makavot, makavan, patish, patisha, kurnas | draft | [hammer-makevet-patish-kurnas.md](entries/hammer-makevet-patish-kurnas.md)

*Makeves* is the heavy hammer, **mallet** or **sledgehammer**, the tool Yael used (Shoftim 4:21); *halmus* is the poetic word for the very same tool (Shoftim 5:26), so render both **mallet**. *Patish* is the plain **hammer**, in the Gemara the blacksmith's; *kurnas*, Mishnaic only, is the **smith's hammer**. The sources grade their sizes against each other and contradict each other doing it, so follow whichever source is being quoted rather than a fixed hierarchy.

**קשה / קשיח / פרך / מקשה / הקש / קושיא / פרוכת** — hard | kashah, kasha, kashe, akasheh, kashiach, kashiah, perech, parech | draft | [hard-kashah-kashiach-perech.md](entries/hard-kashah-kashiach-perech.md)

*Kashah* is the general word, and covers both English senses of hard: **hard** as in rigid and unyielding, and **hard** as in difficult. *Kashiach* is not hardness of substance at all but hardness of heart toward those one should love: **cruel, callous**. *Perech* literally breaks, so *avodas perech* is **back-breaking** labor, or, on a second line of reading, **pointless** labor, work with no end and no product. Rabbi Shlomo of Urbino (*Ohel Moed*) lists the three as the Hebrew words for hard; do not render all three "hard," since only *kashah* is about hardness itself.

**חברון / קרית ארבע / ממרא / אשכול** — hebron | chevron, hevron, chevrone, kiryas arba, kiryat arba, kiryas haarba, mamre, mamrei | draft | [hebron-chevron-kiryas-arba-mamre-eshkol.md](entries/hebron-chevron-kiryas-arba-mamre-eshkol.md)

One city, four names (*Bereishis Rabbah* 58:4). **Chevron** is the standing name, built on ח-ב-ר, connection or friendship. **Kiryas Arba** is the older one (Yehoshua 14:15), and the live question is whether *arba* is the number, "City of Four," or the personal name of the giants' father, "City of Arba"; Rashi holds each way in a different place. **Mamre** and **Eshkol** are the names of Avraham's two allies, and the Torah uses both of the place as well. Do not translate Kiryas Arba in a running text; give the name and let a note carry the "four."
