**הלום / פה / הנה / זה / כה / כאן** — here | halom, poh, po, pe, heinah, heina, hena, zeh | draft | [here-halom-poh-heinah-zeh-koh.md](entries/here-halom-poh-heinah-zeh-koh.md)

*Poh* is the ordinary **here**, and the precise one: a specific spot. *Halom* is directional, **hither**, a step arrived at rather than a place stood in (Rav Hirsch), and Chazal read it as marking a place of royal weight (Zevachim 102a). *Heinah* is **here** as a destination reached from elsewhere, and alone among them also **until now** (Bedersi). *Zeh* for place is the loose one, a general area; *koh* is **thereabouts**. English "here" flattens all five.

**צפון / ספון / טמון / גנוז / נסתר / מוסתר / נעלם / סתר / מסתור / גניזה / הטמנה / תעלומה** — hidden | tzafun, tzafoon, tsafun, tzofun, safun, sfun, tamun, tomun | draft | [hidden-tzafun-safun-tamun-ganuz.md](entries/hidden-tzafun-safun-tamun-ganuz.md)

All six mean "hidden," and Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim is the one authority who separates them. *Tamun* is **buried, concealed within** something (usually the ground). *Safun* is **stowed, hidden away** deliberately, its outer face covered. *Tzafun* is **hidden away for safekeeping, stored up**: concealed in order to preserve, the treasured hidden, not the merely unseen. *Ganuz* is **stored away, put in the treasury** (Rabbinic: consigned to *genizah*). *Nistar* / *mustar* is **hidden** from sight or from knowledge, the plain general word. *Ne'elam* is **hidden from the mind, beyond one's grasp**. The thing not to get wrong: *tzafun* is not a synonym of "lost from view"; it is hidden *and kept*, so אור הצפון is light stored away, not light gone dark.

**וו / ווים / אגמון / שפתים / אנקליות / עגם / גמא** — hook | vav, vavim, vavin, agmon, agmonim, shfataim, shefataim, shefatayim | draft | [hook-vav-agmon-shfataim.md](entries/hook-vav-agmon-shfataim.md)

*Vav* is the plain Biblical word for a **hook**, the fittings of the Mishkan's pillars, and it occurs nowhere else in Tanach. *Agmon* is a **hooked reed** or thistle, used as a fishhook and then for anything bent. *Shfataim* occurs once and is disputed: **hooks** (Targum, Rashi) or a **ledge or setting for pots** (Radak, Abarbanel). *Ankliyot* is the Mishnaic word, of Greek origin, the barbed part of a hook, and it is what Targum reaches for whenever it renders any of the others. Render *vav* "hook" and let the disputed words carry a gloss naming the machlokes.

**כנרת / גניסר / טבריה / כנור** — kinneres | kinneres, kinneret, kineret, yam kinneres, ginosar, genosar, ginnosar, tiveria | draft | [kinneres-ginosar-tiveria.md](entries/kinneres-ginosar-tiveria.md)

One lake, three names, each taken from whichever city on its shore was most prominent at the time: **Kinneret** in Tanach, **Ginosar** in the Targumim and the literature of the Second Temple period, **Yamah shel Tiveria**, the Sea of Tiberias, in the Gemara. The lake has no name of its own. Render each name as the source gives it and do not harmonize them into "the Sea of Galilee," which is a later regional label and not the term any of these sources uses.

**מדרגה / מדרגות / מעלה / מעלות / סולם / דרגא** — level | madreigah, madreiga, madrega, madregah, madreigos, madreigot, madregot, maalah | draft | [level-madreigah-maalah-sulam.md](entries/level-madreigah-maalah-sulam.md)

*Maalah* (plural *maalos*) is a step *up*, an ascent; in the abstract a **step up, an advantage, a virtue**: physically **step, stair**. *Madreigah* is a graded step or terrace; in later Hebrew a **degree, a level** (of achievement), which is the word a mussar text means. *Sulam* is a **ladder**, though Chazal define it by *maalos* and *madreigos* alike, so in Rabbinic usage it may be a stair. The one thing not to get wrong: in Tanach *maalos* is the ordinary word (some fifty times; *madreigos* only twice), and Rabbi Shlomo Aharon Wertheimer, the one authority who separates them, makes *madreigos* a terraced way cut up a mountainside and *maalos* a free-standing stair in a house or courtyard; that is why the altar prohibition says *maalos* (Shemos 20:23).

**שש / בד / בוץ / פשתים / פשתה / כיתנא / כרפס / צמר גפן / כותנה** — linen | sheish, shesh, bahd, bad, butz, buts, pishtah, pishtan | draft | [linen-sheish-bahd-butz-pishtim.md](entries/linen-sheish-bahd-butz-pishtim.md)

All four are linen. *Pishtim* (and *pishtah*, *pishtan*) is **flax**, the raw material. *Butz* is **fine linen** as a material, the Aramaic-Hebrew equivalent of the Egyptian *sheish*. *Sheish* is **fine linen** thread, six fibers to the strand, and is the Torah's word for the Mishkan's weaving. *Bahd* is the **single-fiber** linen thread, from *bad*, "alone," and it is the word for the Kohen Gadol's white garments on Yom Kippur. Cotton is not in this family at all: it did not exist in the ancient Near East, and where Rabbinic Hebrew needs it, it says *tzemer gefen*.

**אולי / פן / שמא / דלמא** — maybe | ulai, ulay, oolai, pen, shema, sheme, shemay, dilma | draft | [maybe-ulai-pen.md](entries/maybe-ulai-pen.md)

*Ulai* is **maybe** for an outcome the speaker wants (or at least expects); *pen* is **lest**, a maybe the speaker hopes will not happen. Biblical Hebrew, on the dominant view, has no neutral "maybe" at all: every *ulai* and every *pen* carries the speaker's leaning. Aramaic *shema* / *dilma* is the neutral word. Do not flatten *pen* into "maybe"; "lest" or "in case" keeps the aversion that the derashos hang on.

**אמצע / מרכז / לב / טבור / רכס / אליבא** — middle | emtza, emtzai, metzia, metziyata, merkaz, merkazi, rikuz, lev | draft | [middle-emtza-merkaz-lev-tabur.md](entries/middle-emtza-merkaz-lev-tabur.md)

Sort them by period first. *Emtza* is the **middle** of Mishnaic Hebrew and does not occur in Tanach. *Merkaz* is **center** and is later still, first used in medieval Hebrew and borrowed from Arabic (Rabbi Shmuel Ibn Tibbon says so himself); it carries the extra sense of "principal, main," as "central" does in English. Tanach says middle with two body words: *lev*, the **heart** or midst of a thing, and *tabur*, the **navel** or central point of a land. So do not put *merkaz* or *emtza* into the mouth of a pasuk, and do not render *belev yamim* as "in the center of the sea."

**רסן / מתג / זמם / פרומביא / אפסר / זמה** — muzzle | resen, ressen, meteg, metheg, zemam, zemama, zomem, zamam | draft | [muzzle-resen-meteg-zemam.md](entries/muzzle-resen-meteg-zemam.md)

*Resen* and *meteg* are both the gear that controls a beast, and no single English word will separate them: render *meteg* **bit** and *resen* **bridle** or **reins**, and reverse the pair only if you are following Rabbi Moshe Tedeschi Ashkenazi rather than Rabbi Avraham Bedersi. Aramaic *zemam* covers both and is a **muzzle**, the device that closes a mouth. *Prumbia* is the rabbinic **muzzle/halter**; *afsar* is the **reins** by which one takes legal possession of an animal. Do not confuse the noun *zemam* (muzzle) with its homograph *zomem/zimah* (plot, scheme), though the sources tie them.

**מרדכי / פתחיה / בלשן / אסתר / הדסה** — names of Mordechai and Esther | mordechai, mordecai, merodach, petachyah, pesachyah, bilshan, esther, ester | draft | [names-mordechai-esther-hadassah.md](entries/names-mordechai-esther-hadassah.md)

All five are names and stay transliterated in roman type; what a translator must not do is treat the pairs as one name each. **Mordechai** is the Babylonian name the Megillah uses throughout, **Petachyah** his Hebrew name, **Bilshan** a title of his mastery of languages. **Esther** is the name the Megillah uses; **Hadassah** appears once, at Esther 2:7, whose *hi Esther* says they are one woman. Which was her birth name is a Tannaic dispute, so a translation should not settle it silently.

**משה / ירד / אבי גדור / חבר / אבי סוכו / יקותיאל / אבי זנוח** — names of Moshe | moshe, mosheh, yered, yered, avigdor, avi gedor, chever, chaver | draft | [names-moshe-yered-avigdor-yekusiel.md](entries/names-moshe-yered-avigdor-yekusiel.md)

These are not synonyms but one man's ten names, and every one of them stays a transliterated **name**, never an English gloss. The six of Divrei HaYamim I 4:18 (Yered, Avigdor, Chever, Avi-Socho, Yekusiel, Avi-Zanoach) are read as Moshe by *Vayikra Rabbah* 1:3, each expounded from a Hebrew root; four more (Toviah, Shemayah, Ben Nesanel, Levi) follow. The one thing not to get wrong: the Midrash concludes that of all ten, the Torah and Hashem Himself call him only **Moshe**, the name Basyah gave him, and that conclusion is the point of the whole passage. Render the derashos by keeping the name in Hebrew and putting the root's meaning in a gloss.

**שלמה / ידידיה / קהלת / אגור / יקה / איתיאל / למואל / עלוקה** — names of Shlomo HaMelech | shlomo, shelomo, shelomoh, shalem, shalom, yedidyah, yedidiah, yedid | draft | [names-shlomo-koheles-agur-lemuel.md](entries/names-shlomo-koheles-agur-lemuel.md)

Rabbi Yehoshua ben Levi counts seven names for Shlomo HaMelech: **Shlomo**, **Yedidyah**, **Koheles**, and the four proper names in the last two chapters of Mishlei, **Agur**, **Yakeh**, **Isiel** and **Lemuel**. Three of them turn on completeness and peace (Shlomo, from *shalem* or *shalom*), three on gathering and giving out wisdom (Koheles and Agur gather; Yakeh spits out), and Isiel and Lemuel both record what he said to himself about the Torah's limits on a king. The one thing not to get wrong: Agur, Yakeh, Isiel and Lemuel read on the surface as ordinary proper names, and every English translation of Mishlei treats them so; the Midrashic identification is a second layer, not a replacement.

**יעקב / ישראל / ישורון** — names of Yaakov | yaakov, yakov, jacob, yisrael, yisroel, israel, yeshurun, yeshurin | draft | [names-yaakov-yisrael-yeshurun.md](entries/names-yaakov-yisrael-yeshurun.md)

All three are names and none of them is translated: **Yaakov**, **Yisrael**, **Yeshurun**, in roman type. What a translator must get right is that they are not interchangeable. *Yaakov* is the man, and by the Ohr HaChaim's reading the man in a low moment; *Yisrael* is the man as founder of the nation, an added name rather than a replacement (Rabbeinu Bachya); *Yeshurun* is a rare title of praise, four times in all Tanach, built on *yashar*, and it answers Esav's charge that *Yaakov* means crooked dealing (Ramban). Where a passage switches names in mid-sentence, the switch is the point; do not smooth it to one name.

**יוסף / יהוסף / צפנת פענח / אברך / רך** — names of Yosef | yosef, yoseif, yehosef, yehoseif, tzafnas paneach, tzafnat paneach, paneach, paneiach | draft | [names-yosef-yehosef-avreich-tzafnas-paneach.md](entries/names-yosef-yehosef-avreich-tzafnas-paneach.md)

*Yosef* is a name, not a word to translate; Rachel gave it from *yosef*, "He shall add" (Bereishis 30:24). *Yehosef* is the same person with a hei of Hashem's Name added (Tehillim 81:6), so it is **Yehosef**, never "Yosef" with a note. *Tzafnas Paneach* stays transliterated; if it must be glossed, Rashi's **revealer of mysteries**. *Avreich* is the one genuinely contested word here, and the choice between **father to the king**, **a father in wisdom though young in years**, and **one before whom all kneel** commits you to a mefaresh. Do not render *rach* as "royal" without knowing you have left the mainstream.

**הבל / ריק / אפס / תהו / בהו / בלימה / מאומה / כלום / מה / בלי** — nothing | hevel, hevle, havel, rik, reik, reikanya, larik, efes | draft | [nothing-hevel-rik-tohu-bohu.md](entries/nothing-hevel-rik-tohu-bohu.md)

*Hevel* is **futility** or **vanity**: a thing that really exists but yields no benefit, and whose apparent worth proves illusory. *Rik* is **emptiness** or **for naught**: no substance at all, and in practice the word for labor that produces nothing. *Efes* is the absolute **there is none besides**. *Tohu* and *bohu* are the pre-Creation pair, best kept as **formless and void** with the commentary carrying the reading you follow, since the *rishonim* divide sharply over which is matter and which is form. *Meumah* and *klum* are **anything** in a negative construction, not "nothing" as a standalone noun. Bedersi and Shadal put *hevel* below *rik* in intensity; Malbim reverses them. Follow the majority (Bedersi and Shadal) unless the passage is Malbim's own.

**זבול / ארמון / אפדנא / טירה / פלטין** — palace | zvul, zevul, yizbeleini, zevel, armon, almon, apadna, apadena | draft | [palace-zvul-armon-apadna-tirah.md](entries/palace-zvul-armon-apadna-tirah.md)

*Armon* is the general word and covers both English senses, **palace** and **castle**, so it is the safe default. *Apadna* is the luxury sense alone: a **palace**, or in the Gemara a grand hall or room. *Tirah* is the defensive sense: a **fortress** or fortified enclosure, never a byword for opulence. *Paltin* is the late Hebrew for a **palace** in the imperial sense. *Zvul* is not really in this set: it is a **lofty abode**, the dwelling that perfectly suits the one it is meant for, and it is used almost always of the Beis HaMikdash.

**דרך / ארח / אורח / אורחות** — path | derech, derekh, derachim, drachim, dorech, doreich, orach, orah | draft | [path-derech-orach.md](entries/path-derech-orach.md)

*Derech* is the **way** or **road**: broad, public, open to everyone, the general term, and the one that extends to conduct ("the way of Hashem"). *Orach* is the **path**: short, narrow, seldom traveled, branching off the road toward a particular place (Vilna Gaon; Malbim). Keep "way"/"road" for *derech* and "path" for *orach* and never swap them; English treats the two as interchangeable, the Hebrew does not. Pappenheim's dissent (one road, *derech* its length, *orach* its width) is recorded below; the Gaon and Malbim carry the rendering. *Nativ* and *mesillah* are not treated here (the article promised them for a sequel).

**נתיב / רחוב / מסילה / מעגל / משעול / שביל** — paths | netiv, nesiv, netivot, nesivos, rechov, rechovot, rechovos, mesilah | draft | [paths-netiv-mesilah-shvil-mishol.md](entries/paths-netiv-mesilah-shvil-mishol.md)

Ranked by width, from the open road down: *derech* and *orach* (treated separately), then *rechov* the **city street**, *mesilah* the **paved highway**, *netiv* the narrow **path** that only some people use, *mishol* the **footpath** too weak for a wagon, and *shvil* the **trail** narrow enough for one ankle at a time. *Maagal* is outside the ranking: a **roundabout way** that goes nowhere in particular. The trap is that English "path" and "way" will take any of them; a pasuk that pairs two (Bamidbar 20:17 against 20:19, or Shoftim 5:6) is distinguishing them on purpose.

**מקום / אתר / דוכתא / אשר** — place | makom, mokom, mekomot, mekomos, atar, atra, asra, atarim | draft | [place-makom-atar-duchta.md](entries/place-makom-atar-duchta.md)

*Makom* is the only native Hebrew word for "place," and it is the default: **place**, or **site/location/spot** where English wants variety. *Atar* is the standard Aramaic word for the same thing, the Targum's regular rendering of *makom*; *duchta* is the Bavli's Aramaic word for "place," and probably a loanword. The one thing not to get wrong: these are not shades of meaning but shades of *language*. Where a passage sets *makom* against *atar*, the contrast is Hebrew against Aramaic, not one kind of place against another.

**כן / דוכן / בימה / בסיס / אצטבא / במה** — platform | kan, kein, ken, duchan, duchen, duchenen, duchta, bimah | draft | [platform-kan-duchan-bimah-basis.md](entries/platform-kan-duchan-bimah-basis.md)

*Kan* is the Biblical word: a **base**, **stand** or **pedestal**, and by extension a man's **post**. *Duchan* is Mishnaic, the **platform** the Leviim sang from and, in the Gemara, the platform the Kohanim bless from (hence the Yiddish *duchenen*); it is a noun, not a verb. *Bimah* is the **stage** or **dais**, and *basis* the **base** of a vessel, both Mishnaic. *Itztaba* is a raised **platform** or **colonnade** in the Beis HaMikdash. The trap is translating *duchan* as an action; a Kohen goes up *to* the *duchan*.

**מוט / בד / נס / תורן / שפוד / זמורה** — pole | mot, mott, motah, motot, motos, bad, badd, badim | draft | [pole-mot-bad-neis-toren-shapud.md](entries/pole-mot-bad-neis-toren-shapud.md)

*Mot* is a thick carrying pole borne on the shoulders, and in the feminine *motah* the bar of a yoke: **carrying pole, yoke-bar**. *Bad* is the same object in the Mishkan vessels, a stave slid through rings: **stave**. *Neis* is a tall upright pole raised to be seen, hence **banner, standard**, and on a ship the **sail**. *Toren* is the **mast** that holds the *neis* up. *Shapud* is a pointed **skewer** or spit. The trap: "pole" will absorb all five. Reserve "stave" for the Mishkan *badim* (never "poles of the Ark" where a derashah turns on *bad*, "alone"), and never render *neis* as "pole" where it carries a banner or where the pasuk plays on *neis*, "miracle."

**חוט / חבל / כבל / אטון / מיתר / מוסרה / עבות** — rope | chut, chutim, chevel, chavalim, chavalos, kevel, kavel, kabel | draft | [rope-chevel-chut-avot.md](entries/rope-chevel-chut-avot.md)

Sort them by thickness and by what the cord is doing. *Chut* is the **thread**, the single strand; *chevel* the ordinary **rope**; *avot* the **thick rope** or cable, braided from three. *Meitar* is the cord under tension, so a **tent cord** or a **bowstring**; *moseirah* the **bond** that restrains a captive, and *kevel* the iron **fetter**; *eitun* a **cord of fine linen**, once only. The trap is that English "cord" and "rope" will swallow five of these at once, and Shoftim 16:12 sets *avot* against *chut* in one sentence precisely to contrast them.

**סוד / רז / רזא / טמיר / כמוס / דבר סתר / סתר / רמז / רוזנים** — secret | sod, sohd, sodos, sodot, raz, roz, raza, razin | draft | [secret-sod-raz-tamir.md](entries/secret-sod-raz-tamir.md)

*Sod* in Tanach is **counsel** or **council** (the circle of advisors and the advice they give: Radak, uniting Ibn Janach's two senses); only in Chazal does it become **secret**, the confidence shared with a few. The Biblical word for a secret proper is *dvar seter*, **a hidden matter** (Pappenheim). *Kamus* is **stored away, sealed up** (Devarim 32:34, once only): classified information. *Raz* (Aramaic, Daniel) is **mystery**, an idea closed off from the many (Rashi: *seter*, *satum*). *Tamir* is Aramaic **hidden**, Targum's word for most Hebrew hiding-words. The thing not to get wrong: in a pasuk, סוד is usually "counsel" or "assembly," not "secret"; in a Gemara or a mussar sefer it is "secret."

**חרמש / מגל** — sickle | chermesh, chermeish, magal, maggal, magla, magal yad, magal kotzer | draft | [sickle-chermesh-magal.md](entries/sickle-chermesh-magal.md)

Both mean **sickle**, and the weight of the sources treats them as one tool under two names: *chermesh* is the Hebrew word, *magal* the Aramaic one taken into Hebrew. That is why the Torah says *meihachel chermesh bakamah* (Devarim 16:9) while the Mishnah's ceremony of cutting the Omer asks the crowd twice *magal zeh?* (Menachos 10:3). Do not translate one "sickle" and the other "scythe"; the sources that distinguish them do so homiletically, not by tool.

**אות / מופת / עד** — sign | oht, ot, os, otot, osos, mofet, mofes, mofsim | draft | [sign-oht-mofet.md](entries/sign-oht-mofet.md)

*Oht* is a **sign** or **token**, the general word, and it can be entirely natural: the luminaries are an *oht* for the seasons, tefillin are an *oht*, the tribal flags were an *oht*, a letter of the alef-beis is an *oht*. *Mofet* is the narrower word, a **wonder** or **portent**, on most readings one that bends nature. Malbim's formula is the one to keep: every *mofet* is an *oht*, not every *oht* a *mofet*. Do not render both "sign"; where they stand together (the makkos are called both, Shemos 10:1-2 and 11:9-10) the pairing is doing work.

**חלק / שוע / שעיע / משעי / שעשוע** — smooth | chalak, chelkat, chelkas, chaluk, shua, shoa, shiiya, shia | draft | [smooth-chalak-shua-mishi.md](entries/smooth-chalak-shua-mishi.md)

*Chalak* is the adjective: **smooth**, and where a surface lets things slide, **slippery**. *Shua* and its Aramaic form *shiiya* are not that. They name the act of making smooth by laying or pressing one material onto another, so render them **smoothed on**, **daubed**, **plastered**, or in the *shaatnez* sugya **combed smooth together** (Rabbi Yaakov Tzvi Mecklenburg). The trap is translating both as "smooth"; the halachah in *Kilayim* 9:8 turns on the difference.

**נפש / רוח / נשמה / חיה / יחידה** — soul | nefesh, nefesh chayah, ruach, ruach hakodesh, neshamah, neshama, nishmas chaim, nishmat chaim | draft | [soul-nefesh-ruach-neshamah-chayah-yechidah.md](entries/soul-nefesh-ruach-neshamah-chayah-yechidah.md)

One soul, five names, ascending (Bereishis Rabbah 14:9). *Nefesh*, the life bound to blood and body, seat of appetite and will: **soul** (in Tanach also **life**, **person**, **self**). *Ruach*, the breath-like middle tier, seat of emotion and speech: **spirit**. *Neshamah*, Hashem's breath, the soul only man has: **higher soul**. *Chayah* (the life-force that outlives the body) and *yechidah* (the singular point) stay transliterated. The tier is the thing not to get wrong: *nefesh* is never "spirit," *ruach* never "soul."

**רמח / חנית / כידון / אלונקי** — spear | romach, romcha, chanit, chanis, kidon, keedon, alunki, alunkei | draft | [spear-romach-chanit-kidon.md](entries/spear-romach-chanit-kidon.md)

*Romach* is the full-size **spear** or **lance**, the weapon Pinchas took (Bamidbar 25:7), and the only one of the three in the Chumash. *Chanit* is the **spear** of the army camp, named from *chanah*, encamping. *Kidon* is a smaller thrown weapon, **javelin** or **dart**; Rambam calls it a small *romach*. *Alunki* is the rabbinic **lance**, a Greek loan-word. Do not get this wrong: *romach* and *chanit* are the two standard Biblical spears, and *kidon* is the odd one, small and thrown.

**צבי / צביון / צבא / צב / יציב** — splendor and desire | tzvi, tzevi, tsvi, tzvi tiferes, ateres tzvi, tzivyon, tzva, tzava | draft | [splendor-tzvi-tzivyon-tzava.md](entries/splendor-tzvi-tzivyon-tzava.md)

*Tzvi* carries three senses to keep apart by context: **splendor, glory, beauty** (Rashi); **desire, what is wanted** (Radak); and the animal, **deer** or **gazelle**. *Tzivyon* is either **consent** or **ideal form**, depending on the commentator. *Tzava* is a **legion**, a body joined by one shared want. The trap is *eretz hatzvi*: "the Desired Land" on the plain reading, "the Land of the Deer" in the derashos of Chazal, and no English phrase carries both.

**כח / גבורה / חזק / אמץ / עצם / עז / איתן / אדיר / כביר / תקיף** — strength | koach, koah, ko'ach, kochos, kochot, gevurah, gevura, gibbor | draft | [strength-koach-gevurah-ometz-chozek.md](entries/strength-koach-gevurah-ometz-chozek.md)

*Koach* is **strength**, the general word: plain ability to do or withstand, shared by man and beast (Rabbi Pappenheim; Rabbi Soloveitchik). *Gevurah* is **might** or **heroism**: *koach* put into action, and for Chazal the conquest of oneself (Avos 4:1; Rabbi Soloveitchik; Rabbi Mecklenburg). *Chozek* is the standard word for being **strong**; *ometz* is strength above the normal measure, **fortitude** or **resolve**, of the heart (Bedersi and Wertheimer; Vilna Gaon), so חזק ואמץ reads "be strong and resolute", the greater word second. *Eitan* **steadfast**; *adir* **mighty**, the mightiest of a group; *oz* bold **might** of spirit, at root Divine; *takif* **overpowering**. Trap: one English word for all; never let *koach* and *gevurah* both be "strength".

**מתוק / ערב / נעים** — sweet | matok, mesek, metek, areiv, arev, arav, naim, naeem | draft | [sweet-matok-areiv-naim.md](entries/sweet-matok-areiv-naim.md)

*Matok* is **sweet** to the tongue, the objective taste opposed to bitter. *Areiv* is **pleasing**, sweetness as this particular person finds it, and it attaches to a voice or a scent as readily as to food. *Na'im* is **pleasant**, the enjoyment itself rather than the property that causes it. The trap is rendering all three "sweet": where the Hebrew moves from *matok* to *areiv* it has moved from what everyone tastes to what one soul happens to love, and that move is usually the point of the passage.

**דבר / ויאמר / וידבר / מלוכה / מושל / בא / אתה** — synonyms | davar, devarim, vayomer, va'yomer, amirah, vayidaber, va'yedaber, dibbur | draft | [synonyms-davar-vayomer-vayidaber.md](entries/synonyms-davar-vayomer-vayidaber.md)

The column's founding premise: in Lashon HaKodesh a word is not a convention but the essence of the thing, which is why *davar* means both **word** and **thing**; every element of creation is a Divine word set into matter. Two Hebrew words for one idea therefore always differ. Render *vayomer* **he said** (brief) and *vayidaber* **he spoke** (extended); *melucha* **kingship** accepted by the people and *moshel* **ruler** whether accepted or not; *ba* and *atah* both **came**, the second being Aramaic and chosen for that reason. The rule a translator must not break: never render two different Hebrew words with one English word in a passage that has set them side by side.

**שולחן / פתורא / טכא / טבלא** — table | shulchan, shulchani, petora, petorah, patora, patorta, petoraah, taka | draft | [table-shulchan-petora-taka-tavla.md](entries/table-shulchan-petora-taka-tavla.md)

*Shulchan* is the Biblical **table**, and the Aramaic *petora* is its standard equivalent; render both "table." *Taka* also glosses as *shulchan*, but on the best reading it is the small personal **tray-table** set before one diner, while *petora* is the long communal one. *Tavla* is a **board** or table top, a flat surface without legs. Where *petora* and *taka* stand together the difference is size and privacy, not vocabulary.

**גיהנום / שאול / אבדון / שחת / בור שאון / טיט היון / צלמות / ארץ תחתית / תפת / עלוקה** — underworld | gehinnom, gehinom, sheol, shol, avadon, avaddon, shachat, shachas | draft | [underworld-gehinnom-sheol-shachat-avadon.md](entries/underworld-gehinnom-sheol-shachat-avadon.md)

*Gehinnom* is a place name pressed into service for the afterlife and should stay transliterated, never "hell" and never "purgatory," both of which carry another religion's doctrine. *Sheol* is literally **the grave** (Ibn Ezra), and only by derashah the soul's place of judgment (Rashi). *Shachat* is a **pit**, with "destruction" audible in it; *avadon* is **loss** or ruin. Eruvin 19a lists seven Biblical names for Gehinnom, and the list is a list of *places within* it (Maharsha), not seven synonyms, so a translation that flattens them into "hell" seven times destroys the sugya.

**צעיף / רדיד / רדידים / עיפא / סודר** — veil | tzaif, tza'if, tzeif, tzanif, redid, radid, redidim, merudad | draft | [veil-tzaif-redid.md](entries/veil-tzaif-redid.md)

*Tza'if* is the **veil**: the covering a woman spreads over her face, the word used of Rivkah and of Tamar. *Redid* is the **shawl** or wrap, a thin garment worn over the head and shoulders, and in Rambam's usage the outer wrap without which a woman would not appear in public. Targum Yonasan renders *tza'if* as *redida* and Onkelos as *eifa*, so the two Hebrew words are close, but the difference to hold is that *tza'if* is defined by what it covers, the face, while *redid* is defined by how it hangs, thin and draped downward.

**פך / קנקן / כד / חבית / קנה** — vessel | pach, pachim, kankan, kankanim, kad, kadim, chavit, chavis | draft | [vessel-pach-kankan-kad-chavis.md](entries/vessel-pach-kankan-kad-chavis.md)

*Pach* is a small pottery **flask** or **jug**, used in Tanach only in *pach hashemen*. *Kankan* is a Mishnaic **jug** or **jar**; *kad* a **jug** or **pitcher**; *chavis* a **barrel**. The trap is over-differentiating: Chazal themselves use these words interchangeably, because the vernacular never fixed them (Bava Kamma 27a), which is why *Maoz Tzur* can sing of *kankanim* where the Gemara says *pachim*. *Kaneh* is not a container but a hollow **reed** or **tube**.

**חומה / קיר / שור / חיל / חיץ / כותל / מחיצה** — wall | chomah, choma, chomos, chomot, kir, kiros, shur, shura | draft | [wall-chomah-kir-shur-kotel.md](entries/wall-chomah-kir-shur-kotel.md)

*Chomah* is the **city wall**, which encircles and defends. *Kir* is the ordinary **structural wall** of a building. *Cheil* is the **low outer wall** and the cleared space it makes. *Chayitz* is a **partition**, and by Rabbi Moshe Zacuto a flimsy one, where *mechitzah* covers any divider. *Shur* is the Aramaic for *chomah*, *kotel* the Aramaic for *kir*. Rabbi Shlomo of Urbino holds the six are simply synonyms; this reading follows Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim's root analysis. The trap is "wall" for all six, especially where *chomah* and *cheil* stand together.

**אי / איה / איכה / איפה / אן / אנה / לאן / מאין** — where | ey, ei, ayeh, aye, ayeka, ayekah, eifoh, eifo | draft | [where-ayeh-eifoh-anah.md](entries/where-ayeh-eifoh-anah.md)

*Eifoh* is the plain **where**, the ordinary request for a location. *Ayeh* (and its forms *ey*, *ayekah*) is **where is**, and in most of the authorities it carries a further charge: the questioner expected the thing to be here and it is not. *Anah* is **to where**, a question of destination, not of present place, and with a prefixed mem, *me'ayin*, it flips to **from where**, a question of origin. The trap is that English "where" covers all of them; where a pasuk turns on *ayeh*, the rebuke is inside the word.

**למה / מדוע** — why | lamah, lama, lammah, madua, maduah, mah deah | draft | [why-lamah-madua.md](entries/why-lamah-madua.md)

Both mean **why**. *Madua* is the neutral question, asking on what reasoning a thing rests; *lamah* asks toward what end, and in most of the authorities it carries a charge, either complaint or rebuke, and often expects no answer. English has one word for both, so the difference has to be carried by the rest of the sentence: render *madua* "why" plainly, and *lamah*, where the context supports it, as "why on earth," "for what purpose," or with the objection said outright.

**חלון / אשנב** — window | chalon, chalonot, chalonos, eshnav, eshnabh, ashnav | draft | [window-chalon-eshnav.md](entries/window-chalon-eshnav.md)

*Chalon* is the ordinary **window**, an opening in a wall, and it is what a translator should reach for unless a passage marks the difference. *Eshnav* appears only twice in Tanach and is the narrower or latticed kind: render it **small window** or, where the source says so, **lattice**. Shoftim 5:28 puts both in one pasuk, Sisera's mother looking out of the *chalon* and then out of the *eshnav*, so "window" twice loses what the navi is doing. What the *eshnav* was is genuinely disputed, and a translation should not settle it silently.

**יין / תירוש / חמר / חמרא / שכר / עסיס / מלאה** — wine | yayin, yayn, yain, tirosh, tiroush, chemer, chamer, chamar | draft | [wine-yayin-tirosh-chemer-shechar.md](entries/wine-yayin-tirosh-chemer-shechar.md)

*Yayin* is **wine**, the default word. *Tirosh* is **new wine**, on the weight of the authorities unfermented or barely fermented juice; do not render it "wine" where a pasuk pairs it with *dagan* or sets it against *yayin*. *Chemer* is **wine** as well, the ordinary Aramaic word, read by most as "the red one." *Shechar* is **strong drink**, though the Targumim narrow it for the Nazir.

**את / אתו / אתי / עם / עמו / עמי** — with | es, et, ito, itto, iti, itti, itah, itam | draft | [with-es-im.md](entries/with-es-im.md)

Both render **with** in English, and no English preposition separates them. The difference is whether the two parties stand as equals. The Vilna Gaon and Malbim: *im* is "with" in the full sense, two equal and cooperative parties, while *es* is the weaker "with," one party dominant and the other along for the ride. Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim and Rabbi Yaakov Tzvi Mecklenburg hold the exact reverse: *im* is circumstantial, they merely happened to be together, while *es* is deliberate and full. Neither view is marginal; when a source darshens a switch between the two words, find out which school it belongs to before translating, because the two produce opposite readings of the same pasuk.


### quantity

**שקל / סלע / בקע / דרכמון** — coins | shekel, shekalim, sela, selah, selaim, sila, beka, bekah | draft | [coins-shekel-sela-beka-darkemon.md](entries/coins-shekel-sela-beka-darkemon.md)

*Shekel* does not mean the same thing in Tanach and in Mishnah. In Tanach a *shekel* is the full weighed coin; the Mishnah calls that coin a *sela* and uses *shekel* for the **half-shekel** of the annual donation (Ramban to Shemos 30:13). Render Biblical שקל **shekel** and Mishnaic סלע **sela**, never "shekel," and check the register of a Mishnaic שקל before writing "shekel." *Beka* is **half a shekel**, from "splitting."

**ספירה / ספר / מספר / מונה / מנין / מפקד** — counting | sefirah, sefira, sefiras haomer, sofer, safar, mispar, moneh, monah | draft | [counting-sefirah-mispar-minyan.md](entries/counting-sefirah-mispar-minyan.md)

*Sofer* / *sefirah* is a count where each unit is marked and matters in itself, and *mispar* is a number that sets a limit. *Moneh* / *minyan* is a count whose point is the total; the single unit carries nothing. Render *sefirah* "counting" and, where the sense is the daily obligation, keep the qualitative weight; render *moneh* "count" or "tally" and *minyan* "tally" or, in the shul sense, "quorum." The one thing not to get wrong: the Torah's choice of *sofer* for *Sefiras HaOmer* is deliberate, so do not flatten it into the same English as the *moneh* of the Kohen Gadol's sprinklings.

**אחד עשר / אחת עשרה / עשתי עשר / עשתי עשרה** — eleven | achad asar, achad-asar, achat esreh, achat-esrei, ashtei asar, ashtei-asar, ashtei esreh, ashtei-esrei | draft | [eleven-achad-asar-ashtei-asar.md](entries/eleven-achad-asar-ashtei-asar.md)

Both mean eleven, and both stand in the opening pesukim of Devarim: "eleven (*achad asar*) days from Chorev... in the eleventh (*ashtei asar*) month" (Devarim 1:2-3). Render *achad asar* **eleven** and *ashtei asar* **eleventh** where the context allows, because the working distinction is that *achad asar* is eleven as a number of its own and *ashtei asar* is eleven as an outgrowth of ten. The tell that settles most cases: *ashtei asar* never takes the definite article, and every other number does.

**זהב / פז / כתם / חרוץ / בצר** — gold | zahav, zohov, dahav, paz, mufaz, meufaz, ufaz, mifazez | draft | [gold-zahav-paz-kesem-charutz.md](entries/gold-zahav-paz-kesem-charutz.md)

*Zahav* is the general word: **gold**. *Paz* is the purest and rarest grade, **fine gold**, and where it stands beside *zahav* in a pasuk the pair is ascending, so render "gold and fine gold," not two flat "golds." *Kesem* is also fine gold, named for the malleability that lets it be beaten thin. *Charutz* is gold as mined, dug out of the ground. *Betzer* is contested and should not be rendered "gold" without a note: gold, silver, a stronghold, and unrefined ore are all live readings of Iyov 22:24. The trap: English has one word where Hebrew has five, and Tehillim 19:11 and 119:127 pair two of them deliberately.

**אחד / אחת / אח / חד / חדה** — one | echad, echod, achad, achat, achas, echat, echas, ach | draft | [one-echad-achad-chad.md](entries/one-echad-achad-chad.md)

*Echad* is the ordinary and overwhelmingly common word for **one**, and where Chazal read it closely it means **the one that matters**, the choice or unique one. *Achad* is the rarer form and splits in two (Rabbi Wolf Heidenheim): in the construct ("one of the mountains") it carries the same weight as *echad*, **one of the important ones**; in the absolute, recognisable because a *mem* follows ("*achad me...*"), it means **any one of them**, and implies the lesser, not the greater. *Chad* is *echad* with the *aleph* dropped, Aramaic in almost every occurrence. *Achat* / *echat* are the feminine, one word vowelised two ways. Never let "one" flatten the *echad* / absolute-*achad* contrast where a derashah turns on it.

**ערמה / חמרים / גדיש / עמר / חמר / ערום** — pile | areimah, aremah, aremot, areimos, chamarim, chomer, cheimar, chamran | draft | [pile-areimah-chamarim-gadish.md](entries/pile-areimah-chamarim-gadish.md)

*Areimah* is the standard word for a **pile** or **heap**, in Tanach and in Mishnaic Hebrew alike. *Chamarim* (always doubled, *chamarim chamarim*) is **heaps upon heaps**, a mass gathered together. *Gadish* is a **stack** of grain still in the sheaf; *omer* is the **bundle** or **sheaf** itself. The trap is *areimah* against *gadish*: Rambam treats them as one thing, but Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim separates them, and the difference is threshed grain (*areimah*) against unthreshed (*gadish*). No source distinguishes *areimah* from *chamarim* in meaning.


### ritual

**מזבח / מצבה / במה** — altar | mizbeach, mizbeiach, mizbayach, matzeivah, matzevah, matzeva, bamah, bama | draft | [altar-mizbeach-matzeivah-bamah.md](entries/altar-mizbeach-matzeivah-bamah.md)

*Mizbeach* is the **altar** proper, built of many stones (or of plated wood in the Mishkan), square, with four horns. *Matzeivah* is the **single-stone altar** or **pillar**, beloved in the days of the Avos and forbidden afterwards; render it "pillar" or "monument" where no sacrifice is in view, and note that Ramban denies it is an altar at all. *Bamah* is a **high place**, a **private altar** away from the central place of worship. The trap: all three become "altar" in English, and then the Torah's own contrast between the commanded *mizbeach* and the hated *matzeivah* disappears.

**אירוסין / קידושין / נישואין / הקדש / שידוכין / מקודשת / ארוסה** — betrothal | erusin, eirusin, arusah, arusa, meorasah, kiddushin, kidushin, mekudeshet | draft | [betrothal-erusin-kiddushin-nissuin.md](entries/betrothal-erusin-kiddushin-nissuin.md)

*Erusin* and *kiddushin* name the same halachic act, the first of the two stages of marriage: **betrothal**, after which she is halachically his wife and adultery applies, though the couple may not yet live together. *Nissuin*, effected by chuppah, is the second stage: **marriage** proper. *Shidduchin* is what English calls **engagement**, an agreement to marry later. The trap is rendering *erusin* "engagement": modern Hebrew uses it that way, but Rav Hirsch (to Shemos 22:16) already warns that the Torah's *erusin* is not that. Where the two words are contrasted, *kiddushin* carries the *hekdesh* sense of setting apart and *erusin* the sense of binding or tying.

**אפריון / חופה / סוכה / סכך** — canopy | apiryon, apirion, chuppah, chupah, chuppa, chofeh, mechapeh, succah | draft | [canopy-apiryon-chuppah-succah.md](entries/canopy-apiryon-chuppah-succah.md)

*Succah* is a **booth**, named for its *sechach*, and the word carries the sense of shelter, a covering that protects from what is above. *Chuppah* is a **canopy**, from *chofeh*, to cover; it says only that the thing is covered on top, with nothing about protection. *Apiryon* is not a canopy at all in its plain sense but a **covered litter**, a carriage a person is seated in and carried. The trap: the mefarshim who gloss *apiryon* as a *chuppah* are stating its halachic function at Amram and Yocheved's remarriage, not equating the two words.

**מוהל / גוזר / רופא / אומן** — circumciser | mohel, mohala, mohalin, mal, milah, mahul, gozer, gozeir | draft | [circumciser-mohel-gozer-rofeh-uman.md](entries/circumciser-mohel-gozer-rofeh-uman.md)

*Mohel* is the **circumciser**. *Gozer* is the **cutter**, the older title, which in a name carries a second sense, halachic decisor. *Rofeh* is a **physician** who performs the circumcision, *uman* a **practitioner** or bloodletter who does; neither means "circumciser" as such. Rabbi Yissachar Tamar's rule sorts them: a man who did only circumcisions was a *mohel*, one who also bled and healed a *rofeh* or an *uman*.

**פרוכת / מסך / וילון / פרגוד** — curtain | parochet, paroches, poroches, masach, massach, vilon, vilan, pargod | draft | [curtain-parochet-masach-vilon-pargod.md](entries/curtain-parochet-masach-vilon-pargod.md)

*Paroches* is a **dividing curtain**, the one that marks off one domain from the next; *masach* is a **screen**, a heavy cloth that covers and shields. In the Mishkan the *paroches* separates the Kodesh from the Kodesh HaKodashim and the *masach* hangs over an entrance. *Vilon* and *pargod* are the later Rabbinic words: *vilon* a plain **curtain**, *pargod* a **partition curtain** standing where the *paroches* stands, and in aggadic contexts the screen between Hashem and what is below. The trap: *paroches* names a function, not a fabric, so "curtain" alone can miss it.

**צום / תענית / עינוי / צומת** — fasting | tzom, tzam, tzamnu, tzomos, tzomot, taanis, taanit, taaniyos | draft | [fasting-tzom-taanis.md](entries/fasting-tzom-taanis.md)

*Tzom* is the Biblical word for a **fast**, abstaining from food; *taanis* is the Mishnaic word for a **fast day**, built on *inui*, **affliction**, and so wider than food alone. The names are not free variants: *Tzom Gedaliah* and *Taanis Esther* carry their words for a reason, and the two proposed reasons are given below. Do not render *inui* as "fasting"; it is affliction, of which fasting is one form.

**ציצית / ציץ / גדילים / גדיל / פתיל / טלית / כרוספדין** — fringes | tzitzis, tzitzit, tzitz, tzitzin, gedilim, gedilin, gadil, gedil | draft | [fringes-tzitzis-gedilim-petil.md](entries/fringes-tzitzis-gedilim-petil.md)

*Tzitzis* are the **fringes**, named for what protrudes and is therefore seen. *Gedilim* are the same strings named for the **braided** third that is thickened by twisting. *Petil* is the single **thread** of *techeiles*, named for snug twisting and, in Rav Hirsch's reading, for its separateness. *Tallis* is not the fringes at all but the **four-cornered garment** that carries them, and it is Rabbinic Hebrew only. *Kruspidin* is the Targum's Aramaic for *tzitzis* and *gedilim*, a Greek loanword for the **hem or fringe** of a garment. The one thing not to get wrong: *tzitzis* and *tallis* are not interchangeable, and "prayer shawl" for *tallis* imports a usage the word itself does not carry.

**מגבעת / מצנפת / כובע / אבנט / חגור / חגורה / אזור / סינר** — hat and belt | migbaas, migbaat, mitznefes, mitznefet, tznif, tzonef, kova, koba | draft | [hat-and-belt-mitznefes-migbaas-avnet.md](entries/hat-and-belt-mitznefes-migbaas-avnet.md)

*Migbaas* is the ordinary *kohen*'s **headdress**, *mitznefes* the *kohen gadol*'s **turban**, and *kova* a **helmet**, not a hat at all: every one of its six appearances in *Tanach* is a soldier's. For belts, *avnet* is the **sash** of the priestly service, *chagor* the general **belt** for anything that goes around the body, and *eizor* the **girdle** at the waist, worn by strong men. Rashi and Ramban hold *migbaas* and *mitznefes* are simply two names for one hat; the majority read them as two different hats, and that is the safer default.

**שופר / קרן / יובל / חצוצרות / שפופרת / שפר / שפיר** — horn | shofar, shofarot, shofaros, keren, karnayim, kranot, yovel, yoveil | draft | [horn-shofar-keren-yovel-chatzotzros.md](entries/horn-shofar-keren-yovel-chatzotzros.md)

*Shofar* stays transliterated; where a gloss is wanted it is **ram's horn**, and the word carries the sense of a **hollow tube** (Ran, from *shefoferes*) and of **beauty and fitness** (root ש-פ-ר; Ritva). *Keren* is the general **horn**, still on the animal or off it, and by extension a **ray**, a **corner**, **strength**, and in the Mishnah the **principal** of a debt or investment. *Yovel* is a horn too (Shemos 19:13) and gives its name to the fiftieth year. *Chatzotzros* are the silver **trumpets** of Bamidbar 10:2, though the terms crossed after the Churban. The trap: never render *keren* and *shofar* both as "horn" in Rosh Hashanah 3:2, since the whole halachah turns on the bovine horn being *called* a *keren*.

**אלילים / פסל / מסכה / מצבה / עצבים / גלולים / תרפים / שקוצים / חמנים / אשרים** — idols | elilim, elil, pesel, pesilim, masechah, masecha, matzeivah, matzevah | draft | [idols-elilim-gilulim-shikutzim.md](entries/idols-elilim-gilulim-shikutzim.md)

Ten words, and they are not interchangeable. Three name the manufacture: *pesel* is a **carved image**, *masechah* a **molten image**, *matzeivah* a **standing pillar**. Four are terms of contempt: *elilim* are **nothings** (or hollow things), *gilulim* **dung-idols**, *shikutzim* **abominations**, *terafim* **rotten things**. *Atzabim* are **images** made of parts, or the things that grieve their worshippers. *Chamanim* are **sun-pillars**, left standing in the sun. *Asherim* are the idols that depend on others to prop them up. The one thing not to get wrong: unlike the ten Names of Hashem, which name one Being, these ten name different things and different reproaches.

**להט / לט / אטים** — magic | lahat, lahad, lat, laat, lot, ittim, ittym, itim | draft | [magic-lahat-lat-ittim.md](entries/magic-lahat-lat-ittim.md)

*Lahat* (Shemos 7:11) is the trick the practitioner works himself, **sleight of hand** or **magic arts**; *lat* (Shemos 7:22, 8:3, 8:14) is the covert method that works through *sheidim*, **secret arts**. Both are **incantation** if you follow Onkelos, who treats them as one word; do not let a translation settle the machlokes silently. *Lahat* elsewhere means **flame** (Bereishis 3:24).

**סעודה / סעד / משתה / מסיבה / הסבה** — meal | seudah, seuda, seudos, seudot, saad, saad lev, mishteh, mishte | draft | [meal-seudah-mishteh-mesibah.md](entries/meal-seudah-mishteh-mesibah.md)

*Seudah* is a **meal** built on bread, from *saad*, to sustain; it is the word for the three Shabbos meals. *Mishteh* is a **feast** built on wine, from *shoseh*, to drink; it is the word for the Purim meal and for the banquets of Megillas Esther. *Mesibah* says nothing about food or drink at all: it is the meal at which people **recline**, and only later came to mean a party in general. The trap is rendering all three "feast," which loses the one thing each word actually names: the bread, the wine, or the posture.

**שבועות / עצרת / קציר / ביכורים / חמישים / קהל** — names of Shavuos | shavuos, shavuot, chag hashavuos, chag hashavuot, atzeres, atzeret, asartha, chag hakatzir | draft | [shavuos-atzeres-katzir-bikkurim.md](entries/shavuos-atzeres-katzir-bikkurim.md)

Three names are the Torah's: **Shavuos**, Festival of Weeks; **Chag HaKatzir**, Festival of the Harvest; **Yom HaBikkurim**, Day of the First Fruits. **Atzeres** is the name of Chazal and the one the Mishnah and Gemara normally use, though the Torah never applies it to this yom tov. Render *Atzeres* by its sense, either **Assembly** or **Conclusion** depending on the reading a passage follows, and say which. Do not translate *Shavuos* as "Pentecost."

**טוטפות / טוטפת / תפילין / תפילה / נטיפות** — phylacteries | totafot, totafos, totefes, totafta, tefillin, tefilin, tefillah, tefilah | draft | [phylacteries-totafos-tefillin.md](entries/phylacteries-totafos-tefillin.md)

*Totafos* is the word of Chumash and belongs to the head alone: render it **frontlets**, or leave it as *totafos*, but never as "tefillin" in a passage that turns on the difference. *Tefillin* is the Mishnaic word, plural because it names the pair of head and arm together; the singular of one box is *tefillah*. The fact not to lose: the Torah never calls the arm box *totafos*, which is one of the two reasons the berachah before laying them says *tefillin* and not the biblical term.

**תפילה / בקשה / תחינה / תחנון / פלילה / חן** — prayer | tefilla, tefillah, tefila, bakasha, bakashah, bakashot, bakashos, techina | draft | [prayer-tefillah-bakashah-techinah.md](entries/prayer-tefillah-bakashah-techinah.md)

*Tefillah* is prayer proper: the fixed, formal **prayer** with its nusach and its halachos, and, on Rabbi Yaakov Tzvi Mecklenburg's reading of the root, a plea that Hashem set right what the petitioner feels is unjust. *Bakashah* is the **request**, the unscripted asking for one's needs. *Techinah* or *tachanun* is **supplication**: the appeal of one who admits he has no claim and asks to be given anyway, out of *chen*, favor. The trap is rendering all three "prayer"; where Yaakov's sword and bow are darshened (*Bava Basra* 123a), *tefillah* and *bakashah* must be kept apart in English.

**כהן / כהנים / כומר / כמרים / נכמרו / מכמר** — priest | kohen, kohanim, komer, komrim, kemarim, nichmeru, nichmaru, michmar | draft | [priest-kohen-komer.md](entries/priest-kohen-komer.md)

*Kohen* is the general word: a **priest** of any cult, and in Tanach it is used of Poti Phera, Yisro, and the priest of Michah's idol no less than of Aharon's sons. *Komer* is specific: an **idolatrous priest** (Radak, Ibn Kaspi). So in a halachic or Mikdash context render *kohen* as **Kohen** and keep it a proper term; where Tanach uses it of a gentile functionary, "priest" is right and carries no slur. Never render *komer* as plain "priest," and never render *kohen* as "idolatrous priest" on your own; the Targum and Rashi decide that case by case.

**אזהרה / איסור / לאו / אסור / זוהר** — prohibition | azharah, azhara, azharos, azharot, hizhartem, lehazhir, issur, isur | draft | [prohibition-azharah-issur-lav.md](entries/prohibition-azharah-issur-lav.md)

*Azharah* is the prohibition as **forewarning**, the notice given before the penalty can attach; several authorities read its root as light, so it is closer to "putting a person on notice by making his duty clear" than to scolding. *Issur* is the prohibition as a **binding**, the rule that ties a person's hands and takes the act off the table. *Lav* is simply a **negative commandment**, a "thou shall not," named for the word *lo*. The one thing not to get wrong: *azharah* and *issur* are not two names for one thing. *Azharah* is the warning half of a prohibition as against the *onesh*, its punishment half (*Yevamos* 2b); *issur* is the substantive prohibited status.

**משכן / מקדש / בית המקדש** — sanctuary | mishkan, mishkon, mikdash, beis hamikdash, bet hamikdash, batei mikdash, shechinah, shechina | draft | [sanctuary-mishkan-mikdash.md](entries/sanctuary-mishkan-mikdash.md)

*Mishkan* is the **Mishkan** (English "Tabernacle"), the portable Sanctuary of the wilderness and of Gilgal, Shiloh, Nov and Givon. *Mikdash*, or *Beis HaMikdash*, is the permanent **Temple** in Yerushalayim. The distinction is temporary against permanent, and it shows in the building itself: curtains and cloth for the *Mishkan*, walls and a roof for the *Mikdash*. The one thing not to get wrong: the Gemara does sometimes use the words for each other, but those are borrowed usages, not evidence that they are synonyms.

**מצבה / ציון / נפש** — tombstone | matzeivah, matzevah, matzeva, netziv, nitzav, tziyun, tziun, tzion | draft | [tombstone-matzeivah-tziyun-nefesh.md](entries/tombstone-matzeivah-tziyun-nefesh.md)

*Matzeivah* is the **monument** or **headstone**, the ordinary word, and the one the Torah uses at Kever Rachel. *Tziyun* is the **marker**, which fixes where the body lies. *Nefesh* is the Mishnah's word, best rendered **memorial**, and never literally "soul" in this sense. Rabbi Yechiel Michel Tukachinsky reads the three as three reasons for erecting a stone at all: to be visible for those who come to daven, to warn off tumah, and to honor the departed. A translator who renders all three "tombstone" loses the reason each is being used.

**תרומה / תנופה / נפה / נופת / ענף / אנף / רמות** — waving | terumah, teruma, terumos, terumot, torem, tenufah, tenufa, tenufos | draft | [waving-terumah-tenufah.md](entries/waving-terumah-tenufah.md)

Both are the ritual waving of a korban. By Rashi's definition, which is the working one, *tenufah* is **waving** back and forth on the horizontal axis and *terumah* is **raising and lowering**, the vertical. Outside the korban, *terumah* is the **separated portion**: the donation to the Mishkan and the tithe given to a Kohen, where Rashi renders it "separation," not "lifting." The trap: English "elevation offering" or "wave offering" will collapse the pair, and in the several pesukim that name both for the same limb the reader must be able to see two distinct acts.


### society

**מליץ / מליץ יושר / פרקליט / סניגור / קטגור / מלצר** — advocate | meilitz, melitz, meilitz yosher, melitz yosher, praklit, peraklit, paraklet, parakilit | draft | [advocate-meilitz-praklit-sanegor.md](entries/advocate-meilitz-praklit-sanegor.md)

*Meilitz* is the only native Hebrew word of the three: a **go-between**, in Bereishis an **interpreter**, and in the phrase *meilitz yosher* a **defending advocate** who speaks up for a man's uprightness. *Praklit* is the patron or **character witness** whose presence and testimony to good character help the defendant, not necessarily by arguing the case. *Sanegor* is the **defense counsel** who does argue it, the standing antonym of *kategor*, the **accuser** or **prosecutor**. Do not flatten all three into "defender"; where a source pairs *sanegor* against *kategor* the point is courtroom advocacy, and where it says *praklit* the point is who vouches for the man.

**גבול / תחום / מיצר / מצרים / ספר / גבלים** — border | gevul, gvul, gvulim, techum, techoma, techumin, meitzar, metzar | draft | [border-gevul-techum-meitzar-sfar.md](entries/border-gevul-techum-meitzar-sfar.md)

*Gevul* is the Biblical word and, on Rav Hirsch's reading, names the whole **territory** inside the line, not only the line: "throughout your borders" means the whole country. *Techum* is the Targum's rendering of *gevul* and the Mishnaic word for **the bounded area** (*techum Shabbos*). *Meitzar* (plural *mitzarim*) is the Rabbinic word for a **boundary** between fields, distinct, per Ibn Kuraish, from the Biblical *meitzar* meaning **straits**. *Sfar* is the **frontier**, the borderland exposed to what lies outside. The trap is *bein hametzarim*, which Rashi reads two ways at once: "between the straits" (from *tzarah*) and "between the boundaries."

**ילד / ילדה / עולל / רובה / עלם / טליא / רביא** — child | yeled, yaldah, yalad, valad, toldos, toldot, olel, olal | draft | [child-yeled-olel-roveh-ravya.md](entries/child-yeled-olel-roveh-ravya.md)

*Yeled* is the unmarked word for a **child**, and carries no age with it; use it wherever the Hebrew does not mark a stage. *Olel* is the **toddler** who has been weaned and gets himself up to look for food. *Roveh* is a **youngster** between *naar* and *elem*. *Elem* is the **young man** who has nearly reached his full height. In Aramaic, *ravya* and *talya* both come out as **boy** or **lad**, and the Targum is not consistent about which Hebrew word each renders. This entry covers the etymologies and the child-words; for *naar* and *almah* themselves see `youth-naar-almah-elem.md`.

**משכון / חבל / עבוט / רהן / אפותיקי** — collateral | mashkon, maskon, mashkanta, lemashken, chaval, chavol, choveil, chavalah | draft | [collateral-mashkon-chaval-avot-rahan.md](entries/collateral-mashkon-chaval-avot-rahan.md)

*Mashkon* is the general **collateral** or **pledge**. *Chaval* is the Biblical verb for **distraining**, seizing a pledge by force once payment is due. *Avot* is a **pledge** handed over by agreement. *Rahan* is security **deposited up front**. *Apoteiki* is property **designated** for collection on default and never surrendered. Not to be lost: *chaval* is taken, *avot* and *rahan* given.

**עסק / עשק / עסקן / עוסק / שדל / שתדלן / השתדלות / שדר** — communal activist | esek, eisek, osek, iska, issuk, askan, asakan, hitasku | draft | [activist-askan-shtadlan-macher.md](entries/activist-askan-shtadlan-macher.md)

An *askan* is a **communal activist**: he is busy with the matter himself and gets it done. A *shtadlan* is an **intercessor** or lobbyist: he works on the authorities to change their position. The Yiddish *macher* is simply a **doer**. Behind them stand *esek*, **dealing** with a matter (and in Tanach, fighting over one), and *shadal*, **making an effort** or **persuading**. Do not render both *askan* and *shtadlan* as "activist"; the whole distinction is that one acts and the other persuades.

**כתר / עטרה / נזר / זר / כליל / תג / תגין** — crown | keter, kesser, kether, atarah, atara, ateres, nezer, neizer | draft | [crown-keter-atarah-nezer.md](entries/crown-keter-atarah-nezer.md)

*Keter* is the **royal crown**: all three appearances in Tanach are in the phrase *keser malchus* (Esther 1:11, 2:17, 6:8), and by the Malbim it sits around the head without rising above it, a coronet. *Atarah* is the general **crown** or **wreath**, of any wearer, and it encircles the head and rises above it. *Nezer* is the crown of **consecration and separation**, worn by the nazir and the Kohen Gadol, which is why the word marks a man off as set apart, not merely as honored. Some hold *keter* is the general word and the other two are particular kinds, but most read all three as distinct kinds; the trap is rendering *nezer* "crown" where it means the state of being set apart.

**גט / גיטין / שטר / שטרות / ספר כריתות / ספר המקנה** — document | gett, get, git, gittin, gitin, shtar, shetar, shtarot | draft | [document-gett-shtar.md](entries/document-gett-shtar.md)

Neither *gett* nor *shtar* is biblical. In the language of Chazal both mean **document**, and both normally take a modifier (*gett chov*, *shtar erusin*). *Gett* is the broader of the two for Rabbi Yisrael Yaakov Algazi: every *shtar* may be called a *gett*, not every *gett* a *shtar*. Unmodified, *gett* means **a bill of divorce** (Rashi; Rambam), and that is how to render it standing alone. In Tanach the bill of divorce is *sefer kerisus*, **a scroll of severance** (Devarim 24:1-3; Yeshayahu 50:1), and a deed of sale is *sefer hamiknah* (Yirmiyahu 32). Do not read a biblical *sefer* as a *shtar*.

**אויב / שונא / צר / צורר** — enemy | oyev, oyeiv, oyvecha, soneh, sonei, sonecha, sinah, sina | draft | [enemy-oyev-soneh-tzar.md](entries/enemy-oyev-soneh-tzar.md)

*Oyev* is the enemy who acts, or at least plans to act: **enemy**, **foe**. *Soneh* is hatred held inside, shown by what a man refrains from doing: **hater**. *Tzar* (*tzorer*) is open, self-costing hostility: **adversary**, **persecutor**. The trap is that all three collapse into English "enemy"; when a pasuk pairs them, as Bamidbar 10:35 does, the pair must stay visibly different in English or the tefillah loses its point.

**משפחה / בית / מחנה / שאר / שפחה / פמליא** — family | mishpachah, mishpacha, mishpocho, bayis, bayit, bayti, machaneh, machane | draft | [family-mishpachah-bayis-sheer-machaneh.md](entries/family-mishpachah-bayis-sheer-machaneh.md)

*Mishpachah* is the ordinary word for **family** (clan, extended kin). *Bayis* is **household** in the sense of the people under one roof, and in several places specifically **wife**. *Machaneh* is **camp**, never "family" proper, even where it names Yaakov's people. *She'er* is a **blood relative**, kinsman, from the "flesh" sense of the word, and it too can mean **wife**. *Pamalia* is an **entourage** or retinue, human or angelic. The trap: three of these can be pointed at one family group in a single parashah, and English "family" will flatten all three.

**חירות / חופש / דרור / חורים / חרות** — freedom | cheirus, cheirut, cheiruseinu, cheiruteinu, chofesh, chofshi, chofshit, dror | draft | [freedom-cheirus-chofesh-dror.md](entries/freedom-cheirus-chofesh-dror.md)

*Dror* is freedom from being owned by another: **release**, "free as a bird," the Yovel word. *Chofesh* is freedom from the obligation to serve: **free, released** (a *chofshi* slave owes no more labor). *Cheirus* is the later, rabbinic word, and it is the positive one: **freedom** as a standing, the *ben chorin*. Do not use one English word for all three where a passage turns on the difference; and note that *cheirus* never carries the sense of freedom in Tanach, only in Chazal.

**רע / רעה / חבר / עמית / רעיה** — friend | reya, rea, reah, reyah, reiacha, rayacha, reiati, rayati | draft | [friend-reya-chaver-amit.md](entries/friend-reya-chaver-amit.md)

The three run on a scale of closeness. *Amis* is the loosest: **associate, acquaintance**, the man one deals with rather than confides in. *Chaver* is the middle and the default: **friend**, one joined to you in some shared thing. *Reya* is the closest: **companion** or **confidant**, the one you are with constantly and to whom you tell your secrets. Rabbi Elazar Rokeach of Worms and Rabbi Shlomo Aharon Wertheimer both set them in this order. Do not render all three "friend" in a passage that lists them.

**שער / תרע / בבא** — gate | shaar, shear, shearim, shiur, tera, tra, tarah, bava | draft | [gate-shaar-tera-bava.md](entries/gate-shaar-tera-bava.md)

All three render **gate**: *shaar* in Hebrew, *tera* in Targumic Aramaic, *bava* in the Aramaic of the Gemara. They are not synonyms within one language, so nothing hangs on choosing between them; what a translator must not miss is that each carries a second, non-architectural sense. *Shaar* in a Mishnah is often a market **price**, and *bava* in a Gemara a **section** of a text, as in Bava Kamma. Neither is a gate.

**מנחה / מתנה / מתן / שי / תשורה / אשכר / דורון** — gift | minchah, mincha, matanah, matana, matan, matanos, matanot, shai | draft | [gift-minchah-matanah-shai.md](entries/gift-minchah-matanah-shai.md)

*Minchah* is a gift of **homage or tribute**: it serves the giver, who is showing his dependence on the receiver. *Matanah* is a gift that serves the **receiver's need**, given with nothing coming back, which is why it is the word for *matanos la'evyonim* and why Rabbi Mecklenburg holds it is never used of an offering to Hashem. *Shai* is a **substantial gift**, and in Tanach it is always given to Hashem. *Teshurah* is the **showpiece gift** brought when one comes before a great man. *Eshkar* is obscure and disputed. *Doron* is not Hebrew at all; it is the Greek word for "gift" that the Targum uses. The trap: *minchah* is not "meal-offering," it is "gift," and only the sacrificial context narrows it.

**אורח / אושפיז / אכסנאי / אכסניה / ארוחה / אורחה** — guest | oreach, orach, orchim, orcha, aruchah, arucha, ushpiz, ushpizin | draft | [guest-oreach-ushpiz-achsanai.md](entries/guest-oreach-ushpiz-achsanai.md)

*Oreach* is the biblical **guest**, more precisely the **wayfarer** taken in while travelling; the word is built on *orach*, a road. *Achsanai* is the rabbinic **guest**, and *achsanyah* the **lodging** or the **host** who provides it. *Ushpiz* is Aramaic and cuts both ways: in the Gemara usually the **host**, in the Zohar (the *Ushpizin* of Sukkos) the **guest**. The trap is exactly that ambiguity: never decide *ushpiz* or *achsanyah* from the word alone, decide it from the sentence, and check Rashi on the spot.

**אמה / שפחה / עבד** — handmaid | amah, amahot, ama, shifcha, shifchah, shefachot, shifchot, eved | draft | [handmaid-amah-shifchah-eved.md](entries/handmaid-amah-shifchah-eved.md)

*Shifchah* is a **bondwoman**, the female counterpart of the *eved*, set to whatever work the household needs. *Amah* is a **maidservant** or **handmaid** in the higher sense: a nursemaid to her master's children (Bedersi) or a housekeeper over the domestic work rather than under it (Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim). *Eved* is the one word for both the Jewish and the non-Jewish male **slave**. The shorthand that *shifchah* means a non-Jewish woman and *amah* a Jewish one holds in Rabbinic usage but not in Tanach, where both words cross the line.

**מין / אפיקורוס / כופר / משומד / מומר** — heretic | min, minim, minus, minut, apikores, apikoros, epikoros, apikorsim | draft | [heretic-min-apikores-kofer-meshumad.md](entries/heretic-min-apikores-kofer-meshumad.md)

Rambam's taxonomy (*Hilchos Teshuvah* 3:6-9) is the one to translate by. *Min* is a **sectarian**, wrong about Hashem Himself. *Apikores* is a **scoffer**, wrong about prophecy, and in practice one who insults Torah and its scholars. *Kofer* is a **denier**, wrong about the Torah, techiyas hameisim or Moshiach. *Meshumad* is an **apostate**, one who has left Klal Yisrael for another faith or is notorious for deliberate transgression. *Mumar* is the censor's tamer replacement for *meshumad*, best rendered **convert-out**. The trap: "heretic" absorbs all five, and Rambam's point is that each names a different denial.

**כבוד / יקר** — honor | kavod, kovod, koved, kaved, kabed, l'chabed, lechabed, kibud | draft | [honor-kavod-yakar.md](entries/honor-kavod-yakar.md)

*Kavod* is **honor**: weight, the honor a thing commands by what it is (Malbim: inherent worth, as with a talmid chacham). *Yakar* is **precious** as an adjective and **esteem** or **preciousness** as a noun: value that comes from rarity and from the impact a thing makes (Rabbi Pappenheim; Malbim). In Megillas Esther the noun *yikar* is "esteem" or "honor". The authorities split on which of the two is inherent: Malbim gives that to *kavod*, Rabbi Avraham Vilner to *yakar*; follow Malbim, since it agrees with *yakar* meaning "rare" elsewhere. Read *yakar* by context: "rare" (Shmuel I 3:1), "costly" (Tehillim 116:15), "dear" (Yirmiyahu 31:19). Trap: "honor" for both when they sit in one passage.

**חם / חמות / חתן / חותן / חותנת / מחותנים** — in-laws | cham, chami, chamos, chamot, chosen, choten, chosni, chotni | draft | [in-laws-cham-chosen.md](entries/in-laws-cham-chosen.md)

Both pairs mean **father-in-law** and **mother-in-law**; in Biblical Hebrew they differ by whose in-laws they are. Radak: *cham* and *chamos* are always a **wife's** parents-in-law, *chosen* and *choseness* always a **husband's**. In Mishnaic Hebrew *cham* and *chamos* widened to cover a husband's in-laws too, but *chosen* and *choseness* never widened to cover a wife's (*Pri Megadim*). So an English "father-in-law" is correct for both but loses the one thing the Hebrew is telling you, which side of the marriage is speaking.

**נחלה / ירושה / אחוזה / יורש** — inheritance | nachalah, nachala, nochel, yerushah, yerusha, yoresh, achuzah, achuza | draft | [inheritance-nachalah-yerushah-achuzah.md](entries/inheritance-nachalah-yerushah-achuzah.md)

*Nachalah* is **inheritance** in the sense of a patrimony: property that comes down the generations from father to son, ordinarily as one portion among several. *Yerushah* is **inheritance** as a bare legal transfer: property that passes out of one man's *reshus* into another's, with no assumption that it was willingly given or that anyone else shares in it. *Achuzah* is the **holding**, inherited property the heir has actually taken hold of. The trap: English "inheritance" absorbs the first two, so where a passage turns on the difference, render *nachalah* "patrimony" or "ancestral portion" and reserve plain "inheritance" for *yerushah*.

**מלך / מושל / מלוכה / ממשלה** — king | melech, moshel, meloch, timshol, meluchah, melucha, memshalah, malchus | draft | [king-melech-moshel.md](entries/king-melech-moshel.md)

Both words name the same position of power; they differ in how the man got there. A *melech* is **king** by the people's willing acceptance. A *moshel* is a **ruler** who holds them by force or by proven superiority, accepted or not. The translator's trap is that English "ruler" and "king" are near-synonyms and will collapse the pair; where a pasuk sets them side by side, the second word is never mere repetition.

**דין / משפט / חק / חוק / דת / נימוס** — law | din, deen, mishpat, mishpot, chok, chuk, chukim, chukkim | draft | [law-din-mishpat-chok-dat.md](entries/law-din-mishpat-chok-dat.md)

*Chok* is **statute** (or **decree**): a law whose reason is not revealed, set and unchanging (Yoma 67b, Rambam). *Mishpat* is **ordinance** or **judgment**: the rational, mostly interpersonal law, and the verdict a judge reaches by weighing two sides (Rambam, Rabbi Moshe Shapiro). *Din* is **judgment** or **law** in the sense of the case and its penalty: the dispute itself (Bedersi), the penal outcome (Wertheimer). *Dat* is **law, edict, royal decree**; in Megillas Esther the king's law, in Devarim 33:2 Divine law. Never "religion." *Nimus* (Rabbinic, from Greek *nomos*) is **law, rule** as society sets it. Do not let "law" swallow a pair: חוקים ומשפטים is "statutes and ordinances."

**גורל / פור / פייס / חולש / קלפי / חבל** — lottery | goral, gorel, pur, purim, payis, payes, pais, cholesh | draft | [lottery-goral-pur-payis-cholesh.md](entries/lottery-goral-pur-payis-cholesh.md)

*Goral* is the general Hebrew word for a **lot**, and also for the **portion** that falls to a man by lot. *Pur* is the word Megillas Esther itself glosses as *goral*; most rishonim call it Persian, so render it **pur** and let the pasuk's own gloss do the work. *Payis* is the Mishnaic word for the **lottery** among the *kohanim* in the Beis HaMikdash, and *kalpi* the **box** the lots are drawn from. *Cholesh* is a rare word for casting lots (Yeshayahu 14:12). The one thing not to get wrong: *goral* is both the drawing and the thing drawn, so in Vayikra 16:8 and Bamidbar 26:55 it must sometimes be "lot" and sometimes "portion."

**אדם / אנוש / איש / גבר / אנשים / גבירה / גברת** — man | adam, odom, enosh, anosh, ish, eesh, gever, gevar | draft | [man-adam-ish-enosh-gever.md](entries/man-adam-ish-enosh-gever.md)

*Adam* is the species name and the highest term: **man** or **mankind** (in a mussar text, keep it *adam* when the passage turns on his likeness to Hashem). *Enosh* is man in his weakness: **mortal**, **frail man** (the plural *anashim* flips to "men of standing," Rashi). *Ish* is one individual, physically mature: **a man**, "man" as against woman or child. *Gever* is man as strength: **man** in the sense of the strong male, never generic humanity. The one thing not to get wrong: the Zohar ranks them (*adam* > *enosh* > *ish* > *gever*), so a derashah on which word a pasuk uses is a derashah on rank.

**אדון / רבון / רב / רבי** — master | adon, adonai, adoni, ribbon, ribono, rav, rebbi, rebbe | draft | [master-adon-ribbon-rav.md](entries/master-adon-ribbon-rav.md)

*Adon* is the Hebrew word for a **master**, and the form *Adonai* is a Name of Hashem. *Ribbon* is the Aramaic equivalent that Targum Onkelos uses for *adon*, an honorific of *rav*, which is itself **master** and in the Mishnah the ordinary word for a slave's owner. Two readings of the difference: the plain one, that it is Hebrew against Aramaic; and Rabbi Baruch Aryeh HaLevi Fischer's, that *adon* is a standing title while *rav*/*ribbon* is used only where there is a subordinate in the master's charge. Do not render both "Lord" in one phrase.

**סוחר / רוכל / כנעני / תגר** — merchant | socher, socharim, sechorah, rochel, rocheles, rochelet, rochlim, cnaani | draft | [merchant-socher-rochel-cnaani-tagar.md](entries/merchant-socher-rochel-cnaani-tagar.md)

Where the three appear as a graded set, follow Malbim (to Yechezkel 17:4, 27:3): *socher* is the **wholesaler**, *cnaani* the **trader** in the middle, *rochel* the **peddler** who buys from the *socher* and goes door to door. Standing alone, *socher* is simply **merchant**, and *Tagar*, the Aramaic word, also renders **merchant** (Rashi, Eruvin 55a, makes him the local seller against the travelling *socher*). The trap is *cnaani*: in a dozen pesukim it means "trader," not "Canaanite."

**כילי / צייקן / קמצן / קמיצה / קומץ** — miser | kilay, keilay, kili, tzaykan, tzaikan, tzaykanim, tzaykanut, kamtzan | draft | [miser-kilay-tzaykan-kamtzan.md](entries/miser-kilay-tzaykan-kamtzan.md)

Three words for the same vice, one per layer of the language: *kilay* is the Biblical **miser**, *tzaykan* the Mishnaic **skinflint**, *kamtzan* the post-Mishnaic and Modern Hebrew **cheapskate**. Each carries its own image: *kilay* locks his money up, *tzaykan* is narrow and hard about parting with it, *kamtzan* measures out the smallest possible amount. The one thing not to get wrong: *kilay* is disputed. Sephardic rishonim read it as "miser," Ashkenazic rishonim (Rashi, Mahari Kara, Rabbi Eliezer of Beaugency) as "trickster," and a translation of Yeshayahu 32:5-7 must choose.

**ממון / דמים / מעות / זוז / כסף / מחיר / הון / רכוש** — money | mammon, mamon, mammonos, mammonot, damim, damin, dmei, demei | draft | [money-mammon-damim-maos.md](entries/money-mammon-damim-maos.md)

*Mammon* is **money** in the broadest sense, one's assets and holdings; it is Aramaic in origin and never appears in Tanach, only from the Mishnah on. *Damim* is **the price** or **the value** of a thing, always plural, and it is the homonym of *damim*, blood, which is a live trap. *Maos* and *zuzim* are coin names that widened into general words for **money**; *kesef* is **silver** that widened the same way. *Mechir* is the Biblical **price**. The one thing not to get wrong: in a Mishnaic passage *damim* is far more often money than blood, and in a Chumash passage it is far more often blood than money.

**אשכנז / גרממיא / גרמניא / לותיר / אלמניא / גומר** — names for Germany | ashkenaz, ashkenazi, ashkenazic, leshon ashkenaz, germamia, germania, germaniya, lotir | draft | [germany-ashkenaz-germamia-lotir.md](entries/germany-ashkenaz-germamia-lotir.md)

*Ashkenaz* in Tanach is a person and a kingdom (Bereishis 10:3; Yirmiyahu 51:27), and should be left as the name **Ashkenaz**, never rendered "Germany"; Shadal states outright that the Biblical Ashkenaz has nothing to do with Germany, and that the later usage borrows an old name for a new place. From Rashi onward it does mean **Germany**, and *leshon Ashkenaz* means **German**. *Germamia* (the Vilna Gaon: *Germania*) is Chazal's name for that land; *Lotir* is the Rishonim's own **Rhineland**; *Almenia* is **Alemannia**.

**עברי / עבר / ישראל / בני ישראל / יהודי / יהודה** — names of the Jewish people | ivri, ivriim, ivrim, ever, eved ivri, yisrael, bnei yisrael, yisroel | draft | [people-ivri-yisrael-yehudi.md](entries/people-ivri-yisrael-yehudi.md)

*Ivri* is **Hebrew**, first applied to Avraham; *Yisrael* and *Bnei Yisrael* are **Israel** and **the Children of Israel**, the Torah's ordinary name for the nation; *Yehudi* is **Jew**, strictly a subject of the Kingdom of Yehudah before it became the name of the whole people. The one thing not to get wrong is that these are not interchangeable registers of the same word: Rabbeinu Bachya holds *Ivri* marks a lower standing than *Yisrael*, which is why the Torah keeps *eved Ivri* for the Jewish slave even after Sinai, when the term had otherwise fallen out of use.

**גוי / עם / לאום / אומה** — nation | goy, goyim, am, ammim, leom, leum, leumim, leomim | draft | [nation-goy-am-leom-umah.md](entries/nation-goy-am-leom-umah.md)

*Goy* is the loose word: a **nation** in the sense of a mass of people with no one king over them. *Am* is the tight word: a **people** united under a single king, leader, or under Hashem, and the only one of the four that regularly takes a possessive (*ami*, "My people"). *Leom* is a **nation** bound by a shared religion, so it can cover more than one kingdom. *Umah* stands with *leom* against *goy* as a nation that does have a crown. Every *am* is also a *goy*; not every *goy* is an *am* (Heidenheim). The trap is the colloquial "gentile": *goy* literally means nation, and in Tanach it is applied to Yisrael too.

**אדרת / גלימא / איצטלא / תכריך / טלית / גולם** — outer garment | aderet, aderes, adderet, glima, gelima, gelem, itztala, itztela | draft | [garment-aderet-glima-itztala.md](entries/garment-aderet-glima-itztala.md)

*Aderes* is the biblical word: a **mantle**, a large outer garment marking the wearer's stature. *Glima* is its Aramaic counterpart in the Gemara, an **unfitted robe**, a flat sheet of cloth rather than a tailored garment. *Itztala* is a **ceremonial cloak**, an outer garment tied to office and rank. *Tachrich* is a **wrap**, and in the plural *tachrichin* the burial shrouds. Rashi glosses all three of the first as *tallis*, so the trap is treating them as identical: *glima* stresses the garment's shapelessness, *itztala* the wearer's standing.

**עני / אביון / מסכן / רש / דל / דך / מך / איש תככים / הלך** — poverty | ani, oni, evyon, evyonim, misken, miskein, rash, dal | draft | [poor-ani-evyon-dal-rash.md](entries/poor-ani-evyon-dal-rash.md)

*Ani* is **poor**, the general word, a man still holding onto his dignity. *Evyon* is **destitute**, worse off and past the shame of asking. *Dal* is **reduced**, one who has lost what he had, not necessarily a charity case. *Rash* is **dispossessed**, poor from the cradle or stripped of property. *Misken* is **wretched, unfortunate**, poor and despised with it. *Dach* is **emaciated** (physically crushed), *mach* **downtrodden** (crushed by circumstance). *Heilech* is the **itinerant**, a door-to-door beggar or wanderer. The one thing not to get wrong: *ani* and *evyon* are graded, and *evyon* is the lower of the two.

**שרי / שרה / יסכה / שר / שררה / נסיכה** — princess | sarai, saray, sarah, sara, yiscah, yiskah, iscah, sar | draft | [princess-sarai-sarah-yiscah.md](entries/princess-sarai-sarah-yiscah.md)

All three names turn on rank. *Sarai*, with its final yod, reads as **"my princess,"** a princess of her own house and nation; *Sarah* is **"princess"** without limit, over the whole world (*Berachos* 13a, Rashi to Bereishis 17:15). *Yiscah* is the third name Chazal identify with her (*Megillah* 14a, *Sanhedrin* 69b), read either as **the one who gazed** with ruach hakodesh or **the one all gazed upon** for her beauty; Rashi adds a fourth sense, *nesichah*, princess again. The trap: rendering both *Sarai* and *Sarah* as "Sarah" in English erases the whole point of Bereishis 17:15, where the change of name is the berachah.

**רכוש / מקנה / נכסים / רכש / כיס** — property | rechush, rechus, rachash, reches, mikneh, miknei, miknah, kinyan | draft | [property-rechush-mikneh-nechasim.md](entries/property-rechush-mikneh-nechasim.md)

*Rechush* is **property** or **possessions**, the movable goods a man has gathered. *Mikneh* is not property at large but **livestock**, the herds that were the ancient world's measure of wealth. *Nechasim*, always plural, is **assets**, the word the Mishnah uses where Tanach would say *rechush* or *mikneh*. The trap: *mikneh* shares the root of *kinyan* and looks like a general word for what one has acquired, but unless context forces otherwise it is herds.

**נביא / רואה / חוזה / מחזה / מראה / חזון / ניב / מתורגמן** — prophet | navi, nevi, neviim, roeh, roe, chozeh, choze, machazeh | draft | [prophet-navi-roeh-chozeh.md](entries/prophet-navi-roeh-chozeh.md)

All three are rendered **prophet**, but they name different sides of the office. *Navi* is the prophet as **mouth**, the one who brings and speaks Hashem's word, and in Shemos 7:1 it means plainly **spokesman**. *Roeh* is the **seer**, the earlier kind, sought out by individuals, whose vision was sharp. *Chozeh* is the later **visionary**, the Aramaic word for a seer, and the Vilna Gaon takes the switch to Aramaic as a mark of dimmer clarity. Do not render *roeh* and *chozeh* identically where a passage sets them side by side.

**רבים / ציבור / פרהסיא / פרסום / פומבי / צנעא** — public | rabim, b'rabim, rav, harbeh, ribo, arbeh, aruvah, rahav | draft | [public-rabim-tzibbur-parhessya-pumbi.md](entries/public-rabim-tzibbur-parhessya-pumbi.md)
