*B'rabim* is **in front of many**, the plain word for a public setting. *Tzibbur* is the **community** as a body that can own and appoint, so *b'tzibbur* is "with the congregation," not merely "in public." *Parhessya* is **openly**, in the eyes of the community, and its foil is *b'tzina*, in private. *Pirsum* is the act, **publicizing**, and *b'pumbi* is a public **spectacle**, an affair made deliberately conspicuous. What must not be flattened is the *parhessya* / *tzina* pair: wherever a sugya sets them against each other, the point is who sees, not how many.

**פשע / מרידה / מרי / ממרה / מורה / מורד** — rebellion | pesha, pasha, poshea, nifsha, vayifsha, meridah, mered, mored | draft | [rebellion-pesha-meridah-meri.md](entries/rebellion-pesha-meridah-meri.md)

Three words that all come out "rebel," and the article's own summary separates them cleanly. *Pesha* at its core is **negligence** born of laziness, and means **rebellion** only by extension. *Meridah* is **revolt** as a counter-response, throwing off a present ruler. *Meri* and *mamre* are **defiance** as switching allegiance, replacing one authority's word with another's. The trap: a lazy *poshea* is not a revolutionary, and in the *shomrim* sugya (Shemos 22:8) *poshea* must be "negligent," never "rebel."

**גר / תושב / אזרח / זר / ישיבה / דירה** — resident | ger, gerim, gur, lagur, garti, toshav, toshavim, ezrach | draft | [resident-ger-toshav-ezrach.md](entries/resident-ger-toshav-ezrach.md)

*Ger* is the **sojourner**, cut off from his place of origin and living somewhere temporarily; *toshav* is the settled **resident** who is there for the long term; *ezrach* is the **native** or **citizen**, whose family and origins are known to everyone. *Zar* is the plain **stranger**. Rabbi Shlomo Aharon Wertheimer's summary is the one to hold: a *ger* is neither an *ezrach* nor a *toshav*, but a man passing through. The trap is that *ger* in halachic usage means a convert, and in Chumash usually does not; decide from the context, and never render *ger v'toshav* of Vayikra 25:35 as two people.

**סופר / סופרים / לבלר / ספרא** — scribe | sofer, sofeir, sofrim, soferim, safra, lavlar, livlar, libler | draft | [scribe-sofer-lavlar.md](entries/scribe-sofer-lavlar.md)

Both words mean **scribe**, and Rashi uses each to define the other. *Sofer* is the Hebrew word, at home in Tanach and in the Mishnah; *lavlar* is the later term, confined to Mishnaic and Talmudic Hebrew. The one thing not to get wrong is the plural: *sofrim* in rabbinic usage usually does not mean "scribes" at all but **the Sofrim**, the early Sages, so *divrei sofrim* is "the words of the Sofrim," never "the words of the scribes."

**יבם / יבמה / ייבום / גיס / גיסה / חתן / במה** — sibling-in-law | yavam, yevamah, yevamos, yevamot, yibbum, yibum, giss, gis | draft | [sibling-in-law-yavam-yevamah-giss.md](entries/sibling-in-law-yavam-yevamah-giss.md)

*Yavam* and *yevamah* belong to *yibbum*: render them **the levir** and **the widow**, or **brother-in-law** and **sister-in-law** where the mitzvah context is plain. *Giss* is the Mishnaic word, a man's word for his wife's brother and by most usage his sister's husband too. Biblical Hebrew has no general word for a sibling-in-law, only *eishes achiv*. English "brother-in-law" covers four relations, so name the relation rather than leaning on it.

**בן / בר / בת / ברת / בנות** — son | ben, bar, bat, bas, banos, barat, beris, bar mitzvah | draft | [son-ben-bar.md](entries/son-ben-bar.md)

Both mean **son**, and the difference is language, not sense: *ben* is Hebrew, *bar* is its Aramaic equivalent, which is why Targum renders *ben* as *bar* everywhere. Render both "son of," and keep whichever form the text itself uses in a name. What must not be lost is that *bar* in Aramaic also means **outside** or **separate**, which is why halachah prefers *ben* in a get and why the darshanim read a lesson into *bar mitzvah*.

**שלל / בז / בזה / מלקוח / שבי / עד / עדי** — spoils of war | shalal, shallal, baz, bizah, biza, bizas, bizat, malkoach | draft | [spoils-shalal-bizah-malkoach.md](entries/spoils-shalal-bizah-malkoach.md)

*Shalal* is the general word for **spoils**, and where it is paired with others it means the clothing, vessels, and valuables. *Baz* / *bizah* is the **lesser loot**, what is stripped afterward, named from a root of contempt. *Malkoach* is **live captives**, animals, and where it stands alone, people and animals both. *Shevi* is **human captives**. *Ad* is booty in the sense of **ornaments** stripped from the fallen. The trap: English "spoils" and "booty" will cover all five, and Bamidbar 31:11-12 uses four of them in two pesukim, where each is doing separate work.

**מטה / שבט / משענת / מקל / שרביט / חוטר / חוטרא** — staff and tribe | mateh, matot, matos, mattos, shevet, shevatim, shvatim, mishenet | draft | [staff-mateh-shevet-makel.md](entries/staff-mateh-shevet-makel.md)

*Mateh* is a **staff** of office, the stick of a man with authority, and by extension a **tribe** (chiefly in Bamidbar and Yehoshua). *Shevet* is a **rod** swung with force, the instrument of discipline, and equally a **tribe**. *Mishenes* is a **cane**, leaned on. *Makel* is a **rod for striking**, the shepherd's stick. *Sharvit* is the royal **scepter**. *Choter* is a Hebraized form of Aramaic *chutra*, staff. The one thing not to get wrong: *mateh* and *shevet* are both stick and tribe, and English has no word that carries both, so the pun in a derashah has to be built rather than translated.

**זר / זרה / נכרי / נכריה / נכר / חילוני / הדיוט / ממזר** — stranger | zar, zarah, zara, zarim, nachri, nochri, nachriah, nochriyah | draft | [stranger-zar-nachri-hedyot.md](entries/stranger-zar-nachri-hedyot.md)

*Zar* and *nachri* both mean "stranger," but they measure different distances. A *zar* is estranged **in one respect**, and is often a member of the same nation or household: render him **outsider** or **stranger**. A *nachri* is a **foreigner** in every respect, from another nation entirely. Malbim states the rule plainly: the *nachri* is more unfamiliar than the *zar*. In Kodashim contexts *zar* is a technical term, the **non-Kohen**, and should be rendered that way, never "stranger." *Chiloni* is the Targum's Aramaic for *zar* and the Midrash's word for a non-Kohen; *hedyot* is a Greek loanword for a **commoner** or **layman**, the ordinary man against the leader, the amateur against the craftsman, the *Kohen hedyot* against the Kohen Gadol.

**מס / מסים / מכס / מכסת / מנדה / בלו / הלך / כרגא / גולגלתא / ארנונא / טסקא** — tax | mas, missim, misim, meches, mechess, michsas, michsat, minda | draft | [tax-mas-meches.md](entries/tax-mas-meches.md)

*Mas* is the general Hebrew word and often means not money at all but **conscripted labor**: a levy of men, as with the *sarei missim* over the Jews in Mitzrayim. *Meches* is the narrower word, a share **reckoned off a total** and given to the king; in Bamidbar 31 it is the portion of the Midianite booty set aside for Hashem, so render it **levy** or **tribute**, not "tax." The Aramaic terms are technical and each names a different tax; keep them transliterated and gloss.

**פרעה / מלך מצרים / פרעה מלך מצרים** — the Egyptian monarch | paroh, pharaoh, paro, melech mitzrayim, melech mitzraim, paroh melech mitzrayim, poel ra, peh ra | draft | [pharaoh-paroh-melech-mitzrayim.md](entries/pharaoh-paroh-melech-mitzrayim.md)

*Paroh* is a title, not a personal name: render it **Pharaoh** and never treat it as the man's name. *Melech Mitzrayim* is **the King of Egypt**, the office in its national capacity. The Torah's choice between them is deliberate, and in six places it writes both (*Paroh Melech Mitzrayim*). The trap is collapsing all three forms into "Pharaoh"; where a passage alternates, the alternation is the point, and the doubled form must stay doubled.

**דוד / דודה / מסרפו / מסרף / שרף / אחבוה / חביבי** — uncle | dod, dode, dodah, doda, misaref, mesaref, misarfo, mesarfo | draft | [uncle-dod-misaref.md](entries/uncle-dod-misaref.md)

*Dod* is not the English "uncle." It is specifically **father's brother**: Rashi states flatly that Scripture never uses *dod* for a mother's brother. Render it **paternal uncle** wherever the kinship is load-bearing, and plain **uncle** only where nothing turns on it. *Misaref* is a *hapax legomenon* (Amos 6:10) and is disputed: **maternal uncle** (Ibn Kuraish, Ibn Janach, Radak in *Sefer HaShorashim*), a generic **kinsman** (Rashi, Ibn Kaspi), or not a kinship term at all but a verb of **burning** (Targum Yonasan, Radak in his commentary). Do not translate it confidently; give one reading and footnote the others. A mother's brother in Tanach is normally spelled out as *achi imo*, never *dod*.

**רווק / פנוי / פנויה / מופנה / בחור / ריק** — unmarried man | ravak, ravok, revakim, panui, penuyah, penuiah, mufneh, mufnah | draft | [unmarried-ravak-panui-bachur.md](entries/unmarried-ravak-panui-bachur.md)

*Ravak* is the Mishnaic word for an **unmarried man**, and whether it covers a divorcé and a widower (Rashi) or only a man never married (Tosafos, Rambam) is a machlokes with halachic consequences, so say which reading you follow. *Panui* means **unattached** in the sense of **free, available, cleared**, from *panah*, to clear away; it is the man's availability, not his status, that the word names. *Bachur* is a **young man**, not a bachelor: it is used of men of military age and can even be said of a married young man. None of the three appears in Tanach in this sense except *bachur*, and *bachur* there never means "unmarried" exclusively. The trap to avoid: rendering *bachur* "bachelor."

**אלמנה / אלמן / אלמנות / ארמלתא / כרמלית** — widow | almanah, almana, alman, almon, almenus, almenut, almenos, almenot | draft | [widow-almanah-alman-armalta.md](entries/widow-almanah-alman-armalta.md)

*Almanah* is **widow**, *alman* **widower**, *almenus* **widowhood**. The Aramaic *armalta* is not a synonym but the same word in Aramaic, so a bilingual text should render both "widow" and not manufacture a distinction. Watch the Mishnaic widening: in Kesubos 1:2 *almanah* covers any previously married woman, divorced as well as widowed, and translating it "widow" there is a halachic error. And *almenos* in Yeshayahu 13:22 is not widows at all but **deserted palaces** on the majority reading.

**נער / נערה / עלם / עלמה / נערים** — youth | naar, na'ar, naarah, na'arah, nearim, elem, almah, alma | draft | [youth-naar-almah-elem.md](entries/youth-naar-almah-elem.md)

*Naar* and *naarah* are the wide words: **youth**, **lad**, **young woman**, and by extension anyone serving as an **attendant**, whatever his age. The strict age sense (a girl from twelve until six months past maturity) is halachic, not biblical usage. *Elem* and *almah* are the young person whose growth is still **hidden**: render **young man** and **maiden**. The one thing not to get wrong is that *naar* in Tanach is not an age bracket; it is applied to a three-month-old Moshe and to a fifty-seven-year-old Yehoshua, and in the latter sort of case it means an attendant.


### speech

**צעקה / זעקה / שועה / שוועה** — crying out | tzaakah, tzeakah, tzaaka, tzaak, vayitzak, zaakah, zeakah, zaaka | draft | [crying-out-tzaakah-zaakah-shaavah.md](entries/crying-out-tzaakah-zaakah-shaavah.md)

*Tzaakah* and *zaakah* both mean **to cry out**, and most authorities treat them as one word with two interchangeable opening letters (Rashi, Ramban, the Zohar, Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim); render both "cry out" and do not manufacture a difference. *Shaavah* is not the same: it is **a cry for help**, and Rabbi Yehuda Leib Shapira-Frankfurter holds it is addressed only to Hashem. The one thing not to get wrong: in *tzaakah* and *zaakah* the sufferer voices his pain, while in *shaavah* he asks outright to be saved (Rabbi Yaakov Tzvi Mecklenburg). Where a minority does separate *tzaakah* from *zaakah*, *tzaakah* is the graver of the two.

**ארור / קללה / אלה / קבה / מארה** — curse | arur, arrur, kelalah, klalah, kellala, kilel, alah, allah | draft | [curse-arur-kelalah-allah-kavah.md](entries/curse-arur-kelalah-allah-kavah.md)

*Arur* is the strong, unrestricted curse: **cursed, a curse** with nothing specified, calling down whatever may come. *Kelalah* is the weaker word, rooted in *kal*, light: **disparagement**, or a **curse** that must name what it wishes on its target. *Allah* is the curse that either invokes Hashem by name or is laid down conditionally: **oath-curse, imprecation**. *Kavah* is the curse pronounced in public with the victim named outright. The trap is treating *arur* and *kelalah* as one word; Shemos 22:27 uses both of them in a single pasuk, and the switch is the whole point.

**בזיון / קלון / זלזול / גנאי / גנות** — disgrace | bizayon, bizayyon, bizzayon, vayivez, bazui, bozeh, bozei, kalon | draft | [disgrace-bizayon-kalon-zilzul-gnai.md](entries/disgrace-bizayon-kalon-zilzul-gnai.md)

Four words for putting something down, and they are graded by what the disparager claims about the object's worth. *Kalon* treats a person as a lightweight, worth less than he is: **dishonor, disgrace**. *Bizayon* is stronger, treating him as worth nothing at all: **contempt, disparagement** (Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim). *Zilzul* is cheapening, selling low what should be dear: **making light of, cheapening**. *Gnai* is post-biblical and is simply the opposite of praise (Rambam): **disgrace, discredit**. The one thing not to get wrong is the *kalon*/*bizayon* order; *kalon* lowers a value, *bizayon* cancels it.

**הבאי / הואי / גוזמא / גזמא / גזם / נדרי הבאי** — exaggeration | havai, hava'i, hovai, guzma, guzmah, gozma, gozem, gazam | draft | [exaggeration-havai-guzma.md](entries/exaggeration-havai-guzma.md)

Both mean **hyperbole**, and Chazal use them interchangeably in the same sugya (Tamid 29a; Chullin 90b); Rabbi Eliyahu HaBachur says outright that they mean the same thing. Render *divrei havai* as **words of hyperbole** or **a manner of speech**, and *guzma* as **an overstatement**. The one thing not to get wrong is that neither word means a falsehood. Rabbi Yaakov Emden: a statement called a *guzma* is literally true, and it is only its plain implication that overshoots. Rabbi Menachem Azariah of Fano: *havai* marks something that must be taken seriously, though not literally.

**פירוש / ביאור / הסבר / פשר / פתרון / פותר / באר / מפורש / סברא / מסביר** — explanation | peirush, perush, pirush, beiur, biur, be'ur, ba'er, hesber | draft | [explanation-peirush-beiur-hesber-pesher.md](entries/explanation-peirush-beiur-hesber-pesher.md)

*Peirush* is the plain **explanation** or **commentary**: it makes known what a text says on its surface, its legal and practical sense (Malbim: exoteric). *Beiur* is **elucidation**: it goes beneath the surface to bring up what is hidden (Malbim), at length and in writing, so that the matter is clear (Shadal). *Hesber* is **explanation of the reasoning**, the *svara*: it answers *why*, where *beiur* answers *what* (Rabbi Reuven Subar). *Pesher* and *pitaron* are **interpretation**, the solving of an enigma; *pitaron* in Tanach is dream interpretation only (Radak, Malbim). Do not render *beiur* as "commentary"; it promises more than a *peirush* does.

**שקר / כזב / כחש / אמת** — falsehood | sheker, shekker, kazav, kozev, chazav, kachash, kichesh, michachesh | draft | [lie-sheker-kazav-kachash.md](entries/lie-sheker-kazav-kachash.md)

*Sheker* is **falsehood** outright, false when it was said and knowingly so. *Kazav* is **a lie that time exposes**: it sounded true, and only later proves false (the Gra: he meant it when he said it and then did not keep his word). *Kachash* is **denial**, a false answer to a claim already standing against him. The trap is rendering all three "lie"; the Yom Kippur confession *b'kachash uvchazav* needs two different English words, and a *sheker* is never merely a mistake.

**אנכי / אני / אנך** — first person | anochi, anoki, ani, anoch, anach | draft | [first-person-anochi-ani.md](entries/first-person-anochi-ani.md)

Both mean **I**. *Ani* is the plain first person; *anochi* is the emphatic, **I myself**, and it is the weightier and rarer word (*ani* outnumbers it more than two to one in Tanach). Where the two are contrasted, the working rule is Malbim's and Rabbi Pappenheim's: with *anochi* the news is in the subject (I, and no one else, did it), with *ani* the news is in the predicate (what I did, as against something else I might have done). A translator who renders both "I" loses every derashah built on the difference, beginning with *Anochi Hashem Elokecha*.

**שפה / לשון / לשון הקודש** — language | safah, safa, sfat, sefat, lashon, loshon, leshon, lashon hakodesh | draft | [language-safah-lashon.md](entries/language-safah-lashon.md)

Both mean **language**, and both are body parts taken over for speech: *safah* is the lip, *lashon* the tongue. Rav Dessler: the lip is outside the body and the tongue inside, so a *lashon* is a language whose speakers have taken its inner content into themselves, while a *safah* is one they hold only externally, its mechanics without its values. That is why Hebrew is *Lashon HaKodesh*, and why the generation of the Haflagah, who spoke the same words with none of their content, had one *safah* (Bereishis 11:1). When translating, keep the body image where a derashah leans on it; elsewhere "language" serves for both.

**לא / אל / בל / בלי / בלתי** — negation | lo, al, bal, bli, b'li, bilti, bilui, blo | draft | [negation-lo-al-bal.md](entries/negation-lo-al-bal.md)

All three negate. *Lo* is the Torah's word for a standing prohibition and also the plain factual "did not"; *al* is a negative imperative aimed at a future act, "do not"; *bal* is the word Chazal use to *name* a prohibition already issued in the Torah with *lo*. So *lo sashchis* in a pasuk is **"you shall not destroy"**, while *bal tashchis* in a sugya is **"the prohibition of bal tashchis"**, not a fresh command. *Bal* is not a Rabbinic coinage: it appears close to seventy times in Tanach, though never in Chumash.

**הודאה / הלל / שבח / פאר / רומם / הדר / ברכה / עילוי / קילוס / נצח / להודות / להלל / לשבח / לפאר / לרומם / להדר / לברך / לעלה / לקלס / לנצח** — praise | hodaah, hoda'ah, lehodos, lehodot, hallel, lehallel, shevach, leshabeach | draft | [praise-hallel-shevach-hodaah-kilus.md](entries/praise-hallel-shevach-hodaah-kilus.md)

The siddur's list (end of Pesukei DeZimrah on Shabbos and Yom Tov, the Haggadah, the close of Hallel) reads, in order: *lehodos* **to thank**, *lehallel* **to praise**, *leshabei'ach* **to laud**, *lefa'er* **to glorify**, *leromem* **to extol**, *lehader* **to beautify**, *levarech* **to bless**, *le'aleh* **to raise high**, *lekales* **to exalt**; Nusach Sefard (Arizal) adds *lenatzei'ach* **to eternalize** after *lehader*, Nusach Ashkenaz (Maharal) omits it. Keep the order and give each verb its own English: thanks comes before praise because personal gratitude precedes the general duty (Wertheimer), and *lekales* is "exalt," not "disparage," though the Biblical verb means the latter.

**מיאון / סירוב / מרי / מרידה / סרבן / סרבים / סלונים** — refusal | miun, mieun, vayimaen, maen, meen, siruv, seiruv, sarvan | draft | [refusal-miun-siruv.md](entries/refusal-miun-siruv.md)

*Mi'un* is the biblical word for **refusing**, forty-six occurrences; *siruv* is Aramaic and rabbinic, at most once in Tanach. Treat them as synonyms by default, differing only in language of origin. Where a source presses the difference, follow Rabbi Yaakov Dienna: *mi'un* is declining to comply with a request, *siruv* is the flat rejection of the proposition itself, the stronger word. Both have technical rabbinic senses that must never be translated as plain refusal: *mi'un* is the minor's annulment of her marriage, *siruv* is contempt of beis din.

**חרש / שתיקה / דום / חשה / הס** — silence | cheresh, cheiresh, hachareish, tacharishi, tacharishun, charishah, choresh, chorshah | draft | [silence-cheresh-shetikah-dom-chashah.md](entries/silence-cheresh-shetikah-dom-chashah.md)

*Cheresh* is thinking silence: **holding one's peace** while turning something over, and never merely quiet. *Shetikah* is the **quieting down** that follows a commotion. *Dom* is **falling silent**, silence as a state that comes over a person, and at root simply stoppage. *Chashah* is **keeping still**, an inward stillness or a held-back answer. *Hass* alone is transitive: to **hush** others. The trap is that English "be silent" flattens all five; where a passage turns on which one it is (Mordechai's *hachareish tacharishi*, Esther 4:14), the choice of word is the whole point.

**שיר / שירה / זמר / זמרה / מזמור / זמירות / שיר מזמור / מזמור שיר** — song | shir, sheer, shirah, shira, shiros, shirot, zemer, zimrah | draft | [song-shirah-zimrah-mizmor.md](entries/song-shirah-zimrah-mizmor.md)

*Shir* / *shirah* is **song**: the sung, worded song, and the general term for song sacred or secular (Malbim). *Zemer* / *zimrah* is **music**, or **melody**: instrumental music (Ibn Ezra, Radak, Sforno, the Vilna Gaon, Rav Hirsch, Wertheimer), or the tune as against the words (Mecklenburg), and in Malbim's second view the specifically religious song of Hashem's praise; when the two stand together *shirah* comes first, *zimrah* second, because *zimrah* is the higher (Malbim). *Mizmor* is **psalm** as a title, and in the superscriptions *mizmor shir* / *shir mizmor* the *mizmor* is the tune and the *shir* the words (Pappenheim, Pincus; Ibn Ezra sees no difference). The thing not to get wrong: שירו וזמרו is "sing and play," two acts, not "sing and sing."

**דיבור / אמירה / מאמר / דיברות / לאמר / חטיבה** — speech | dibbur, dibur, dibber, vayidaber, vayedaber, dibbros, dibbrot, dibrot | draft | [speech-dibbur-amirah.md](entries/speech-dibbur-amirah.md)

*Dibbur* is speech as an act: forceful, bold, sustained, and complete whether or not anyone receives it. Render it **speak, address, discourse**. *Amirah* is speech as communication: gentle, brief, aimed at a hearer who takes the meaning in. Render it **say, tell**. The one thing not to get wrong: Rashi's "*dibbur* is harsh, *amirah* is soft" is a register claim about tone, not a claim that *dibbur* means rebuke; Rav Hirsch's reading (act of speech versus act of communication) is the more portable rule and the one to use when the pasuk gives no tonal cue. *Aseres HaDibros* is "Ten Statements" or "Decalogue," never "Ten Commandments"; *Asarah Maamaros* is "Ten Utterances."

**נאם / יחוה / יביע / שיח / שיחה** — speech | neum, ne'um, num, nam, namta, neumta, numita, numati | draft | [speech-neum-yichaveh-yabia-siach.md](entries/speech-neum-yichaveh-yabia-siach.md)

*Ne'um* is not a plain word for "word." It is the solemn declaration whose whole force is that it is true and settled: render it **declaration**, **utterance**, or in a *nevuah* **thus says**, and never flatten it to "said." *Yichaveh* is speech that brings an idea out into the open and joins speaker to listener: **express**, **declare**. *Yabia* is speech welling up from the depths like a spring: **pour forth**, **utter**. *Siach* is discursive, wandering speech that unburdens the speaker: **converse**, **muse**, **pour out one's talk**. The trap is that "speak" and "say" absorb all four.

**הגדה / מגיד / להגיד / והגדת / תגיד / הגיד / ספור / מספר / לספר / תספר / אגדה** — telling | haggadah, hagadah, higid, lehagid, maggid, magid, vehigadeta, vehigadata | draft | [telling-haggadah-sippur.md](entries/telling-haggadah-sippur.md)

*Haggadah* is **telling** in the strong sense: something new, said forcefully, and drawn into the listener's present. *Sippur* is the general **relating** of a story, old or new, and it carries the sense of parts joined into one whole. Ramban (to Devarim 26:3) treats the two as synonyms; most commentators do not, and the Bottom line follows the majority. That is why the Torah commands *v'higadta l'vincha* while Chazal call the mitzvah *sippur yetzias Mitzrayim*: when the Torah spoke, the story was still news, and by the time of Chazal it was a story handed down.

**נדר / שבועה / נדבה / קונם / נדרים / שבועות** — vow | neder, nedarim, shevuah, shevuot, shevuos, nedavah, nedavos, konam | draft | [vow-neder-shevuah-nedavah.md](entries/vow-neder-shevuah-nedavah.md)

*Neder* is a **vow**: it works on an **object**, giving it a forbidden status on the model of hekdesh. *Shevuah* is an **oath**: it works on the **person**, binding him to do or not do, and it ordinarily invokes Hashem's name. They are not two grades of one thing, and the Gemara (Nedarim 2b) treats them as two distinct instruments, each with its own masechta. A *nedavah* is a subclass of *neder*, a **freewill offering** that both undertakes the korban and designates the animal. The trap is the common English "promise" and "oath," which make the pair look like a weaker and a stronger version of the same act.

**שריקה / צפצוף / משרוקיתא / שרקרק / רחם / שורק** — whistle | shreikah, shrekah, sherikah, sharak, sharku, yishrok, tishrok, sharakrak | draft | [whistle-shreikah-tziftzuf.md](entries/whistle-shreikah-tziftzuf.md)

*Shreikah* is a **whistle** made with the mouth alone, in two opposite settings: the hiss of astonishment at a ruined city, and the signal-whistle by which a man calls the scattered back to him. *Tziftzuf* is the bird's **chirp**, and by extension a low, rapid, half-intelligible **prattle**. Do not let English "whistle" cover both: a *tziftzuf* is a sound made about something, a *shreikah* is a sound made at something. The Aramaic *mashrokita* (Daniel) is the one place the root leaves the mouth and becomes an instrument.

**עד / עדות / סהד / קדוש** — witness | eid, ed, eidim, eidus, eidut, eidos, sahad, sahed | draft | [witness-eid-sahad.md](entries/witness-eid-sahad.md)

*Eid* is **witness** and *eidus* **testimony**; *sahad* is the Aramaic for the same, so render it **witness** too. They are not true synonyms, since they belong to two languages (Yerushalmi Sotah 7:2; Shabbos 115b), which is the whole point of Lavan naming the mound *Yegar Sahadusa* and Yaakov *Gal-Eid* (Bereishis 31:47). *Eid* also means **warn**, and that sense must not be flattened into "testify." One who dies al kiddush Hashem is a *kadosh*, never a "witness."

**מילה / תיבה / דבר / מלל / מילתא** — word | milah, mila, milin, millel, milel, yimallel, timallel, millelu | draft | [word-milah-teivah-davar.md](entries/word-milah-teivah-davar.md)

*Milah* is the **spoken word**, the biblical term (Tehillim 19:5, 139:4; Iyov throughout). *Teivah* is the **written word**, a later grammarians' term, and the one behind *roshei teivos*. *Davar* is a **word** that is also a **thing**, and that double sense is the point wherever a source works with it. The trap: English "word" flattens all three. If a passage distinguishes what was said from what is written on the page (a Masoretic or dikduk discussion), *milah* and *teivah* must not both come out as "word."

**כתיבה / חרות / חרט / חרושה / חקוק / פסל / פיתוח / גליף / רשם** — writing | ketivah, kesivah, ktov, kasav, katav, vayikatev, charut, charus | draft | [writing-ketivah-charut-chakuk.md](entries/writing-ketivah-charut-chakuk.md)

*Ketivah* is the general **writing**, ink on a surface, about 260 times in Tanach. The rest are all ways of cutting: *charut* is writing that goes **through** the material (Pappenheim: from *chor*, a hole); *charushah* is **engraving** understood as plowing a furrow; *chakuk* is **incising** grooves with a stylus; *pasal* is **hewing** out of stone; *pituach* is the reverse operation, **embossing**, where the background is cut away and the letters stand proud. The trap: English "engrave" will take *charut*, *charushah*, *chakuk* and *pasal* all at once, and will also wrongly take *pituach*, which is its opposite.


### time

**ראשית / תחילה / ראשון / ראש / קדם** — beginning | reishis, reishit, breishis, bereishit, rishon, rishonah, rosh, reish | draft | [beginning-reishis-techilah-kedem.md](entries/beginning-reishis-techilah-kedem.md)

*Reishis* is the **beginning** that is also the **foremost**: an absolute starting point that carries importance in its own right, and that need not imply anything before or after it. *Rishon* is **first** in a counted series, one of several. *Techilah* is the **onset**, the point marking that a thing did not exist before, without any claim of primacy. *Kedem* is **before**, and in space **east**. The one thing not to get wrong: *reishis* is valued, not merely ordinal. Rendering it flatly "the first" throws away the importance on which Rashi's opening comment and the Netziv's *terumah* reading both depend.

**בושש / איחור / התמהמה / בשש / בושה** — delay | boshesh, bosheish, bosh, ichur, icher, acheir, achar, hitmahmah | draft | [delay-boshesh-ichur-hismahmeah.md](entries/delay-boshesh-ichur-hismahmeah.md)

*Ichur* is the neutral word: **being late**, simply past the appointed time. *Boshesh* is **long overdue**, a delay so far past the hour that those waiting give up, and the word carries the sting of *bushah*, shame. *Hismahmeah* is **tarrying** or **dawdling**, going slower than one should, and Rabbi Shlomo Pappenheim reads it as the one delay that can be right. Shemos 32:1 says *boshesh*, not *ichur*, and the difference is the whole peshat of the Eigel: render it "was long overdue," not "was late."

**עולם / נצח / סלה / ועד / עד** — forever | olam, leolam, le'olam, netzach, netzah, selah, sela, vaed | draft | [forever-olam-netzach-selah-vaed.md](entries/forever-olam-netzach-selah-vaed.md)

*Olam* is a very long but bounded span: **for all time**, and in a legal context often "until the Yovel," not literally forever. *Netzach*, *selah*, and *va'ed* are the true unbounded words: **forever**, **everlasting**, **for all eternity**. The trap is *le'olam*: Rashi to Shemos 21:6 caps it at Yovel, and Maharsha builds on that, so a translator who writes "forever" for *le'olam* in a halachic pasuk has overshot. When *olam* stands beside one of the other three, the second word is doing work; do not collapse the pair into one English "forever."

**תיכף / מיד / לאלתר / אותיום / על אתר** — immediately | teikef, tekef, techef, miyad, mi'yad, miyyad, lealter, le'alter | draft | [immediately-teikef-miyad-lealter-otyom.md](entries/immediately-teikef-miyad-lealter-otyom.md)

All four are Rabbinic Hebrew, none of them in Tanach, and all four mean "immediately." *Teikef* is **immediately after**, one thing hard on the heels of another (it also means physically pressed close together). *Miyad* is **straightaway**, literally "from hand," the readiness of what is already in your hand. *Le'alter* is **on the spot**, a contraction of *al atar*. *Otyom* is a technical Mishnaic term, best left as *otyom* with a gloss; the majority hold it is a Greek loanword. The trap is that English "immediately" flattens all four, and the pair *teikef umiyad* then reads as mere repetition.

**חדש / ירח / ירח** — month | chodesh, chodesh, chadash, chiddush, rosh chodesh, yerach, yeirach, yareach | draft | [month-chodesh-yerach.md](entries/month-chodesh-yerach.md)

Both words come out **month** in English, but they name different things. *Chodesh* is properly the **new moon**, the month's beginning, from *chadash*, "new"; "month" is a secondary, borrowed sense (Malbim, Rav Hirsch). *Yerach* is the month as a full span of days, kin to *yareach*, the moon. Where a pasuk uses *chodesh* in a place that calls for *Rosh Chodesh*, render "new moon," not "month." Practically: a *kesubah* says *chodesh* and a *get* says *yerach* (Levush), so the two are not interchangeable even in a document.

**שחר / בוקר / צפרא / קריצתא / שחרות / שחור** — morning | shachar, shakhar, shacharis, shacharit, boker, boqer, tzafra, tsafra | draft | [morning-shachar-boker-tzafra.md](entries/morning-shachar-boker-tzafra.md)

*Shachar* is **dawn** or **daybreak**, the first light breaking the night before the sun is up. *Boker* is **morning**, beginning at *haneitz hachamah* and running on into the day. *Shachar* is a point; *boker* is a stretch, and *shachar* comes first. In Aramaic, Onkelos renders both as *tzafra*, but in Iyov, Mishlei and Tehillim the Targum separates them: *boker* is *tzafra* and *shachar* is *kritzuta*. Both Hebrew roots also carry the sense of seeking out and discerning, which is why *shachar* and *boker* can each mean "search."

**עתה / נא / עכשיו / כען / כדון / השתא / האידנא / עת / עידן** — now | atah, ata, attah, na, achshav, achshov, achshaiv, kean | draft | [now-atah-na-achshav.md](entries/now-atah-na-achshav.md)

*Atah* is the ordinary biblical **now**, over four hundred times, and it carries *eis*, time, in its own letters. *Na* also means **now**, and only secondarily "please" or "raw." *Achshav* is **now** in the language of Chazal and in Modern Hebrew, and never occurs in Tanach; do not use it to render a pasuk. In Aramaic, *ke'an* is the biblical form and the Targum's regular rendering of both *atah* and *na*; *ke'don*, *hashta*, and *ha'idna* are its later companions. The one thing not to get wrong is the stratum: *atah* and *na* for Tanach, *achshav* and *hashta* for Chazal.

**זקן / שיבה / ישיש / קשיש / ישן / עתיק / ותיק** — old age | zaken, zakein, seivah, seiva, sevah, yashish, kashish, yashan | draft | [old-zaken-seivah-yashish.md](entries/old-zaken-seivah-yashish.md)

The three age words run in ascending order: *zaken* is **an elder** (sixty), *seivah* is **old age** proper (seventy), *yashish* is **the aged**, older still. *Yashan* is **old** of things, not of people ("stale," "aged," "last year's"). *Atik* is **ancient**, old by remoteness in time. *Vatik* is not an age word at all: render it **veteran** or **senior**, of standing and reliability, never "old." The trap is English "old," which will flatten all six.

**זו / זיו / אייר / אויר** — second month | ziv, ziw, iyar, iyyar, iyar, avir, aveir, zivsanei olam | draft | [second-month-ziv-iyar.md](entries/second-month-ziv-iyar.md)

The Torah numbers the months rather than naming them (Ibn Ezra to Devarim 16:1), so the second month is "the second month." Where Tanach does name it, it is **Ziv** (Melachim I 6:1, 6:37), a word meaning **radiance**, and specifically radiance as something given off rather than the source itself (Rabbi Moshe Shapiro). **Iyar** is the name in use since Bavel and appears nowhere in Tanach. The practical point a translator or scribe must not miss: how to spell Iyar, with one yud or two, is an unsettled halachic question in a *get*, and the poskim conclude for two.

**שבע / שביעית / שבוע / שבת / שבועה / שבויה** — seven | sheva, sheviis, sheviit, shviis, shavua, shavuah, shavuos, shavuot | draft | [seven-sheva-shavua-shevuah.md](entries/seven-sheva-shavua-shevuah.md)

*Sheviis*, "the seventh," is the Sabbatical year, and it is the Mishnah's word for it. *Shavua* is a **week**, but in Daniel and in several mishnayos it is a **seven-year cycle**, so context decides. *Shabbos* is the day, and by synecdoche the **week** it closes and the Sabbatical **year** itself (Ramban). *Shevuah* is an **oath**, tied to *sheva* by every authority here, though they part on why. Do not translate *sheva* as "seven" where the sources read it as "many" (Rasag to Bereishis 4:24 and Mishlei 24:16).

**פתע / פתאום / פתי / פתוי** — suddenly | peta, feta, pesa, pitom, pitom, pitomi, pisom, peti | draft | [suddenly-peta-pitom.md](entries/suddenly-peta-pitom.md)

Both mean **suddenly**. *Peta* is the narrower and rarer word (seven times in Tanach), and the Gemara pins it to *shogeg*, a mistake one could have avoided. *Pitom* is the wider word (twenty-five times), reaching *shogeg*, *oness* and even *meizid*. Where they stand together, as in Bamidbar 6:9, they are a halachic pair and not a stylistic doubling; render the couplet with two words ("suddenly, all at once") and never collapse it into one.

**בול / מרחשון / חשון / מבול / גשם** — the eighth month | bul, marcheshvan, mar cheshvan, cheshvan, marachshwan, shvan, mabul, geshem | draft | [month-bul-marcheshvan.md](entries/month-bul-marcheshvan.md)

**Bul** and **Marcheshvan** name the same month and both stay transliterated; in most of Tanach it is simply *chodesh hashemini*, **the eighth month**, counted from Nissan. Bul is the older, pre-exilic name (Melachim I 6:38); Marcheshvan never appears in Tanach and first shows up in Mishnah. Do not translate either by an etymology, and in particular do not render the *mar* of Marcheshvan as "bitter": whether *mar* is even a separate element is disputed. In the same family, *mabul* is **flood** and *geshem* **rain**, and the difference is not quantity.

**עת / זמן / מועד / שעה / עתיד / מזומן** — time | eit, eis, et, es, ittim, itim, zman, zeman | draft | [time-eit-zman-moed-shaah.md](entries/time-eit-zman-moed-shaah.md)

*Zman* is **time** in general, the span or flow (Wertheimer: the most general of the three; Rav Hirsch; Maharal). *Eit* is **a time**, a point or portion within the span, often the natural season for something (Malbim, Rav Hirsch: "rain in its *eit*"). *Moed* is **appointed time**, the preset point fixed by man or by Hashem, hence the *moadim* (Malbim, Wertheimer). *Shaah* beside *eit* is **hour**, the unit of the day against the unit of the year (Rama). The thing not to get wrong: בכל עת ובכל שעה is not a redundancy to be collapsed into "at all times"; it is two measures, "at every season and every hour" (Rama, against those who dropped the second word).
